## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities are responsible for ensuring that all relevant information is included in the data provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the project proposal and/or annexes must be reflected accordingly.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 150 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
* The recommended font is Arial, size 11.
* Under the GCF's Openness and Transparency Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. Accredited Entities are required to inform the IDP Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G-4.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

The two main Executing Entities (EE) are FAO and the Government of Malawi acting through (i) the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), (ii) the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (MoFEA). Detailed information on the project team profiles can be found below. More details are provided in Section B.4 and in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).

The Government of Malawi acting through:
(i) The Ministry of Agriculture (MoA): MoA is strongly engaged since the beginning of the project design process. Through its network of decentralized staff, MoA will be able to ensure that the project activities are implemented at the districts and all the stakeholders of the decentralized local government system i.e. District Agricultural Officers (DAO), District Veterinary Officers (DVO), District Director of Agriculture and Natural Resources, Traditional Authorities (TA), Guidance Heads (GH), Village Natural Resources Management Committees (VNRMCs).

(ii) The Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs(MoFEA): MoFE is implementing the IFAD-funded project “Malawi Food Security and Agricultural Modernization Project” (MIFARM). MIFARMS’s development objective is to increase access to, and use of a range of sustainable agricultural inputs and services by smallholder farmers through micro-enterprises (MSMEs). It has a national coverage and has a strong comparative advantage to support EAM in enhancing the financial inclusion of smallholder farmers.

(iii) The National Local Government Finance Committee (NLGFC): NLGFC is the public oversight body for local government finances. As part of its role, it will provide technical management and local development in local governments. As this is the case for the FAO-managed GEF7 project, NLGFC will be responsible for ensuring that the impact programme - DLE - NLGFC will provide procurement services to the EAM project beneficiaries. This includes providing support on contract negotiations with suppliers on price and delivery and monitoring the transfer of procurements from the central government to districts.

(iv) The United Nations (UN): UN is supporting the Government of Malawi through its co-financing EASAM MoA, MoFE and NLGFC – see below). This will allow Malawi to access additional resources for the implementation of the project. UN will provide technical assistance agency from the United Nations (UN), while providing opportunities for the government to learn about best practices in the design, financing, planning, and implementation of activities under the three components. FAO will also execute the activities co-financed by the FAO managed GEF7 project and Technical Cooperation Program.

Details of activities implemented by each executing entity is provided in Section B.4 and in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

## Project Overview

### Background and Rationale for the Project

- **Location**: Malawi is classified as a Least Developed Country (LDC) by the United Nations (UN), and one of the 20 most vulnerable countries according to the UNDP Human Development Index, ranking among the top 20 most vulnerable countries in terms of climate change.
- **Project Climate Vulnerability Definition**: The project's climate vulnerability is defined as a function of climate exposure, climate sensitivity, and adaptive capacity. Malawi faces high exposure to climate change due to its location at the southern end of Lake Malawi, which is a major source of water for the country. The project area has been identified based on the rationale for the project’s scope and selection of targeted areas.
- **Project Area Characteristics**: Malawi's high exposure to climate change can be characterized by extreme weather events such as heavy rainfall leading to flooding in some parts of the country, increased frequency of climate-induced natural disasters, including droughts and floods. At the same time, the country is very vulnerable due to its low adaptive capacity, which is reflected in its limited access to water resources, poor soil quality, degraded ecosystems, large rural population in deep poverty, high food insecurity and precarious health situation.
- **Adaptation Objectives**: The project aims to increase climate-resilience of smallholder farmers at the household level through improved livelihoods. It will focus on increasing resilience of smallholder farmers in the following districts: Chiradzulu, Ntchisi, Zomba, Phalombe, Machinga, Thyolo, Nsanje, Mwanza, Ngwira, Sinazongwe, Nkhotakota, Nsanje, Lilongwe, Rumphi, Karonga and Chilanga in the South. The project will also focus on increasing climate-resilience of smallholder farmers at the household level through improved livelihoods.
- **Project Objectives**: The project's objective is to increase climate-resilience of the most vulnerable rural communities at watershed-level in Malawi. The Project is built on a baseline that targets 287,500 hectares under ESM planning (including 267,500 hectares and generates mitigation benefits of about 323,400 tCO2e/year, hence contributing to the achievement of the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) for Malawi. The project will benefit 189,500 beneficiaries adopting low-emission practices and T5 Ecosystems (267,500 hectares), which will contribute to the support dissemination of climate information to communities through SMS and community radio, directly contributing to the achievement of the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) for Malawi. The project will also contribute to the National Climate Change Fund and local conservation trusts). These impacts will be achieved through the implementation of three main components (described in section 2.1): (i) Integrated landscape management, PR resilient livelihoods and food systems, and (ii) Enabling institutional and financial environment.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

### a) Component 1: The objectives of the component are to increase the climate resilience of water ecosystems (Outcome 1.2) and reduce GHG emissions from improved wetland ecosystems (Outcome 1.1). This component will also support the development of an integrated landscape management approach that includes ecosystem-based adaptation at scale. It will use ESA as the main tool to build climate resilience, which will be firmly integrated in local policies and plans. The component will focus on strengthening the capacity of communities to manage their ecosystems through community-managed wetlands (sub-component 1.2), enhancing communities' technical capacity, understanding and know-how, as well as increase stakeholders' engagement, including women's participation, in ecosystem-based adaptation at scale. It will also support the development of adaptation interventions based on EBA (sub-component 1.3). Through the implementation of integrated management plans, the component aims to improve the resilience of wetland ecosystems by promoting the restoration of functioning ecosystems that are more adapted to extreme climate, and hence increase resilience of landscapes and livelihoods.

### b) Component 2: The objective (Outcome 2.0) of this component is to stabilize productivity and farmers' incomes thanks to more resilient livelihoods and food systems. By enhancing extension services through farmers field schools (sub-component 2.1), the component will support farmers in adapting their agricultural practices to changing climatic conditions and enhance their capacity to integrate EBA into their farming systems. The component will also promote the development of a market-based approach for climate-smart agriculture, including the local food system to access markets (sub-component 2.2) and finance – such as village savings and loans associations (VSLAs), microfinance institutions (MFIs), and insurance schemes (sub-component 2.3). This will support adoption. The component's interventions, by boosting nature-positive food production and building resilience to vulnerability, will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals.

### c) Component 3: The objective (Outcome 3.0) of this component is to enhance the enabling environment (finance and policies) to sustain, replicate and scale-up climate resilient watered ecosystems and agriculture practices. It will work with the Ministry of Agriculture, Water Resources and Forestry (MAWRF), the National Local Government Finance Committee (NLGFC) and the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (MoFA) to address the financial and policy barriers of low and volatile investments in integrated landscape management, as well as promote the development of a climate-smart agriculture finance framework. The component will support conservation trusts; leveraging private sector experience on carbon credits with Climate Asset Management - CAM and other innovative financing mechanisms such as green bonds (sub-component 3.1). It will also develop plans that will allow replication and scale-up of ESA through other programs and programmes. In addition, interventions will be designed to ensure the alignment of climate change adaptation strategies with national development agendas at sectoral policy levels – for massive-scale impact and sustainability.

### d) The project will be executed by FAD together with the Government of Malawi, through (i) the Ministry of Agriculture, Water Resources and Forestry (MAWRF), (ii) the Department of Land Resource Management (DLRM); (iii) the National Local Government Finance Committee (NLGFC) and (iv) the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (through the FARMASE project). The component will be implemented at national level, with a focus on the implementation of technical activities at district level, together with all the actors of the decentralized local government system. NRC will provide technical assistance to the Government of Malawi in the design and implementation of the Project, for an increased sustainability and ownership by districts. MoFA will execute component 2.3 on access to finance through VSLAs, MFIs and insurance schemes. The Ministry of Environment and Tourism (MoET) will engage relevant government counterparts from MoA, Ministry of Climate Change and Natural Resources (MiCCNR), National Local Government Finance Committee (NLGFC), Department of Land Resource Management (DLRM), conservation trusts, finance service providers and the private sector.

### e) Total Project costs are USD 23.2 million. This includes: i) USD 19.48 million for component 1; ii) USD 22.56 million for component 2; iii) USD 0.72 million for component 3. (iv) 3.23 million for M&E and (v) 2.42 million for project management. In addition, in accordance with the EU grant request, the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (MoFA), the Ministry of Agriculture (MAWRF), the Department of Land Resource Management (DLRM) and the FARMASE project will provide USD 10.58 million.

### f) The articulation between assumptions, barriers, risks, project activities, outputs, outcomes and goal is highlighted in the Theory of Change (Section B.2b). The Project Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) system, based on the Results Framework (Section B.3), will be used to monitor progress against the indicators set out under the CIF Investment Criteria (Section D). It has built a Gender Strategy, based on a comprehensive gender assessment (see Annex A), in line with EU’s environmental and social EBA policy and gender guidance (section G).

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

See also Annex 2 (Feasibility Study) for more detail on the climate context. A list of Acronyms is provided at the end of this document.

#### DEVELOPMENT PROBLEM OF MALAWI: AGRICULTURE, WATERSHED ECOSYSTEMS AND FOOD INSECURITY

1. Malawi is a predominantly agricultural country with 80% of its population employed in agriculture and consequently low productivity in agriculture. Malawi is a country defined by smaller farmers, who constitute 75-95% of the total farming households. In 2016, Malawi’s agricultural sector accounted for approximately 34% of GDP attributable to agriculture, forestry and fish was over 20%, which was above the average of sub-Saharan Africa (SSA). However, in terms of contribution to national income, the agricultural sector has been declining as a share of the economy; the sector has been plagued by environmental/cosystem degradation and erratic weather, in addition to other factors such as poor infrastructure and lack of access to markets. The government has therefore prioritized agriculture*, for which effective management of water and soil is particularly important. The availability of water has become increasingly critical due to climate change and population growth. Malawi’s agricultural sector has been integrated into one of the most degraded in sub-Saharan Africa. The need for more fertile farmlands – created by population growth, deforestation, and land degradation – has led to increased soil erosion and reduced fertility. The practice has resulted in a cycle of decline and degradation of the environment, leading to low yields and agricultural productivity. Malawi’s food security is also threatened by climate change, which is expected to lead to deforested farmlands and a greater water shortage.

2. Total land area per capita is 0.5 ha, and arable land and permanent crops are increasing in the population increasing in tandem with a larger water shortage, which is indicative of increased pressure on resources in agriculture and hence the overall economy; adoption of sustainable management of watersheds with a focus on farming.

3. The most vulnerable are the most affected by climate change, predominantly so in a society marked by high poverty levels. In 2016, Malawi’s Gini coefficient was 48%, which is one of the highest in SSA and the second-highest in the world in 2014.* Its Gini index has been historically high*; roughly 75% of Malawi's population lived below the international poverty line (USD $1.90/day) in 2016, with a further 38% living on less than USD$1.90 per day. Consequently, nearly all smallholder farmers are likely under the poverty line. The COVID-19 pandemic is estimated to have pushed an additional 5 million people into extreme poverty (defined as living below $2/day). This has been exacerbated by increased fuel and fertilizer costs caused by the conflict between Ukraine and Russia.* National household consumption is expected to shrink, with larger impacts on poor and rural households and leading to an increase in inequality.

4. The food security situation in Malawi is characterized by a high prevalence of undernourishment. A national food systems model in Malawi was held in 2019 to contribute to the United Nations Food Systems Summit in the same year.* It emphasized the importance of protecting ecosystems, especially watersheds and their soil functions, which are critical for water retention and supply, as well as for maintaining biodiversity and reducing floods and floods, which are more frequent and intense due to climate change. The government sees landscape restoration as essential to address these challenges and has prioritized this in its National Development Plan (2016-2035) under Phase Classification). Malawi’s situation for 2022-2028 is characterized by one of the population experiencing moderate chronic dietary food insecurity* which includes the sub-food security category of famine vulnerability in the country.*

# CLIMATE-DEVELOPMENT NEXUS: CLIMATIC DRIVERS AND CLIMATE CHANGE IMPACTS

Climate change is intensifying the potency of climate drivers – El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD). These two phenomena are expected to increase in frequency, intensity, and duration. The impacts on crops include reduced yields, increased soil loss* (and consequently, soil fertility decline) and are expected to inflict greater damage under climate change according to the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS) of Malawi.

The DCCMS has projected that the mean temperature in Malawi will increase by 0.5°C for the period 2014–2039, with a range of 0.4°C to 0.6°C. The mean annual rainfall is expected to decrease from 870 mm (for the period 1981-2010) to between 850 and 870 mm for the period 2031–2050, with a range of 840 mm to 890 mm. The Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS) of Malawi concluded that the annual mean temperature in Malawi will increase by 0.6°C between 2014-2039 compared to the period 1981–2010, with a range of 0.5°C to 0.7°C (more information generated by DCCMS on historical and projected temperature and rainfall is contained in Appendix 1 of this document). The mean annual rainfall for Malawi has been estimated at between 840 mm and 920 mm.

The effects of climate change are likely to trigger a downward spiral in agriculture, food, nutrition and the rural economy. Climate hazards identified (i.e., higher frequency and intensity of extreme heat, drought/flooding, strong winds and disruptions in the water cycle) are experienced by ecosystems as higher frequency and intensity of plant stressors, increased pest pressure, reduced soil fertility, and altered nutrient cycling. These climate change-induced changes in the ecosystem will result in a decline in crop yields, increased pest diseases and weeds, and inconsistent water supply (see Appendix 1 of Annexure 2, Table 3). In other words, a combination of climate change and land degradation is likely to reduce the productivity of Malawi’s agricultural sector. The onset of rainfall, the onset of the rainy season can have significant impacts on climate-change also explores the water sector in increasing crop yields.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

### Mitigation of climate change through sustainable land management practices

A potential for dynamic climate adaptation exists as the median age of the population is 17 years, and the youth is a key driver of social change. The distribution of youth population showed a pattern very similar to those of population density and poverty levels. The youth are more likely to be affected by climate change than adults due to their vulnerability in terms of livelihood conditions. Emigration and remittances from the diaspora are thought to be increasing rapidly (1). The emigration rate has been estimated at 20% for the period 2005-2014, with a further increase expected over the next decade. This is an important factor for successful adaptation, especially for ecosystem-based adaptation and agroecology. Such information, including migration patterns, should be integrated into climate change adaptation strategies.

However, it has not been fully integrated into interventions (2) or supplemented by western science for maximum efficacy in addressing this issue.


## GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSION PROFILE

### 15. According to the Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMA): 2015, the category of Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Use (AFOLU) was responsible for 34% of global greenhouse gas emissions.

The AFOLU emissions exist in three subcategories: cropland agriculture (7.1%), livestock production (6.8%) and Forest and Other Land Use Change (FOLU) (20%). As Usual (AU) scenarios as of 2015 indicated that the dominance of FOLU will be eclipsed somewhat by the two coal power plants, which are expected to be commissioned in 2017. The AU scenario also indicates that the NAMA will not stem the gravity of deforestation and degradation as a solution forest regeneration and reforestation as mitigation actions based on the current policy framework. The potential for land use change (LUC) is significant; however, the extent to which agriculture has turned from carbon sink to carbon source around 2014, emitting 1.96 million tCO2e in 2017 (3), is the sum of all emissions from cropland agriculture and livestock production.

The NAMA does not mention decaying biomass from land flooded by hydroelectric dams, which could be a significant source of carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions. The extent to which agriculture has turned from carbon sink to carbon source around 2014, emitting 1.96 million tCO2e in 2017 (3), is the sum of all emissions from cropland agriculture and livestock production.

### 16. The two major sources of emissions in AFOLU are expansion of agriculture and settlements and unsustainable management practices. The potential for mitigation of crop agriculture in NAMA is likely larger than currently estimated. The updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) (4) from 2021 did not include forestry and other land uses (FOLU) in its baseline and target, but it does include cropland agriculture (CA). The CA baseline for the NAMA is 39 million tCO2e (5), of which 3.9 (14 million tCO2e) sustainable forest management (31.8 million tCO2e) and reforestation (0.55 million tCO2e). In the baseline, 7% of CA is under organic farming practices. The NAMA does not mention decaying biomass from land flooded by hydroelectric dams.

In FOLU is conditional on international support: 5% of AU scenario in 2040 with unconditional funds and 51% with conditional funds. In the baseline, 37% of FOLU is under organic farming practices. The NAMA does not mention decaying biomass from land flooded by hydroelectric dams.

The CA baseline for the NAMA is 39 million tCO2e (5), of which 3.9 (14 million tCO2e) sustainable forest management (31.8 million tCO2e) and reforestation (0.55 million tCO2e). In FOLU is conditional on international support: 5% of AU scenario in 2040 with unconditional funds and 51% with conditional funds.

The NAMA does not mention decaying biomass from land flooded by hydroelectric dams.


## PROPOSED CLIMATE SOLUTION: ECOSYSTEM-BASED ADAPTATION

### The proposed climate solution to the problem of deforestation and degradation (EBA) approach strengthens the capacity of ecosystems to provide goods, services and resilience in order to prevent and heal damages to them.

An additional benefit: Many water systems and communities are experiencing compound climate impacts. Measures to halt the vicious cycle of ecosystem degradation and loss of biodiversity are essential for a sustainable future. Ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) is one such solution which restores ecosystems and accommodates the climate uncertainties. Since farmers are part of larger water systems, they can play an important role in this process.

The NDC points to ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA), which restores and strengthens food security and the overall economy. This also points to EBA as a key strategy for addressing climate change impacts on agriculture. The NDC includes it as one of its priorities through the development of adaptation planning tools, EBA, community-based adaptation options, integrated watershed

B

## Ecosystem-based adaptation strategies in five aspects:

### 19. **EBA** (Ecosystem-Based Adaptation) uses the ecosystem to adapt to climate change. The Green Climate Fund supports EBA through restoring, strengthening and sustainably managing ecosystems.

* **EBA** is an agriculture (or agroecology), which is the application of ecological principles in agricultural systems. It aims at increasing productivity while maintaining or enhancing environmental services.
* **EBA** measures effectiveness by a wider range of climate situations than other adaptation strategies. EBA generates short- and long-term benefits, including increased food security, improved livelihoods, reduced poverty, enhanced ecosystem resilience, and climate change adaptation than grey infrastructure when economic, social, and environmental contexts are included.

### 20. **EBA** involves use of biodiversity and ecosystem services to assist people and agriculture to adapt to climate change. Agriculture constitutes major users of watered lands, which may negatively affect the surrounding ecosystems that provide essential services for agriculture (e.g., pollination). Ecosystem-based adaptation strategies aim at controlling pest insects, habitat for crop pollinators, soil fertility and so on.

* **EBA** in agriculture, thus, aims at creating an ecosystem that is resilient to climate change. The Integrated Land Management (ILM) is a tool used by the GCF to strengthen the effectiveness of the Project.
* **EBA** is also a paradigm-shifting pathway listed by the GCF Sectoral Guide on Agriculture and Food Security.

### 21. The following additional information is provided:

* **Planned activities:** EBA, when properly planned, climate-resilient landscape management for watersheds, is not well integrated into long-term plans, such as Village-Level Action Plans (VLAPs). Long-term planning for climate change adaptation is limited by the lack of capacity to plan and implement. The limited capacity is due to a lack of human resources, long-term management/coordination and limited knowledge on climate change adaptation solutions among the stakeholders. This has led to weak implementation of climate change adaptation strategies in the Project.
* **Ecosystem-based technical capacity to adapt to the changing climate is limited among farmers and extension workers. Insufficient extension capacity** has been correlated with stagnating productivity and national development. Limited outreach coverage is also a problem, as it is not possible for all farmers to be reached satisfactorily. They are limited in nature, scope or both. Limited outreach coverage is also to blame.
* **Detailed information on the ecosystem-based adaptation strategy:** EBA is an approach that aims at creating an ecosystem that is resilient to climate change. The Integrated Land Management (ILM) is a tool used by the GCF to strengthen the effectiveness of the Project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FINANCING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

## B

### The project description and rationale

The Project is based on beneficiary-driven approaches: Integrated Landscape Management (ILM), and Farmer Field Schools (FFS) extension methodologies, such as FFS, increases the participation of vulnerable people and promote the uptake of climate-smart agricultural practices. These interventions will be implemented in a participatory manner to build capacity building on both ecological/technical and socio-economic issues. The beneficiaries, whose capacities are widened and strengthened through these approaches, will be able to better manage their resources, including land, water, the government and FAO. For example, it will be the farmer beneficiaries who choose the plants (trees, grasses, crops) that they want to grow in their fields. These interventions will be implemented based on the National Land Management Plans will be based on EIA, including ecosystem restoration.

The project is expected to directly benefit around 270,000 farmers and indirectly benefit an additional 150,000 people through the creation of 300 jobs in the project area. The project will also benefit 400 schools with 60,000 students and 30 main sub-catchments. By mid-term of the project, more than 287,000 beneficiaries will enjoy improved health, well-being, food security, livelihoods, and environmental sustainability.

The project is expected to directly benefit around 547,000 people. The details on the expected performance against investment criteria are in Section D.1 and those on the expected performance against climate change adaptation criteria are in Section D.2. The expected performance against climate, the summary is given in Table B.1.1 below.

The EIA techniques in the field associated with future climatic events are given in Annex 3.1 and the list of selected interventions:

| Type of Climate Barrier | Drivers/Drivers of Change | Impacts | Interventions |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Temperature | Increase in temperature | Plantation stress, reduced yields, increased pest pressure | Development of climate-resilient land-use/land-management plans |
| | Decrease in temperature | Reduced yield and productivity | Development of climate-resilient land-use/land-management plans |
| (b) Precipitation | Significant delays or the absence of rainfall during the growing season | Further deforestation and degradation of forest cover, reduced yields and productivity | Intensified soil erosion, reduced water conservation, increased vulnerability to droughts |
| | Increase in frequency and intensity of extreme weather events | Increased frequency of pests, diseases, and weeds; increased water supply | Intensified soil erosion, reduced water conservation, increased vulnerability to droughts |
| (c) Extreme Weather Events | Droughts, Flooding, Storms, Disruptions in water cycle | Reduced agricultural food production, reduced economic development | Reduction of agricultural food production, reduced economic development |
| | On people's social and cultural life | Increased risk of flash floods, increased vulnerability to droughts, increased risk of various diseases | Development for disaster-dietary interventions (e.g., vitamin A, iron) |
| | On people's livelihoods | Reduced access to water resources, loss of livelihoods | Integrated pest management (IPM), integrated agroforestry techniques and practices, development of climate-resilient land-use/land-management plans |
| | On people's health | Increased risk of malaria, increased vulnerability to various diseases | Development for disaster-dietary interventions (e.g., vitamin A, iron) |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## Climate Drivers: El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and Oceanic Dipole (ODD)

- **El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO):**
  - No clear link between ENSO and dry/wet seasons in local areas.
  - Intensity to increase under climate change.
  - More frequent extreme events.

- **Oceanic Dipole (ODD):**
  - Brings cyclones; causes extreme weather events on this event.

## Main Climate Drivers

- **Unsuitable agricultural practices and land degradation:**
  - Low and stagnant agricultural productivity.
  - Development and implementation of integrated land management plans based on EIA (EIA 1.1 and RC 1.2).

- **Landscape/habitat fragmentation:**
  - Unsatisfactory landscape/habitat management.
  - Disaster relief rather than long-term solutions.
  - Land restoration often without consideration for local communities.

## Barriers

- **Knowledge gaps among professionals, weak extension services:**
  - Limited technical capacity to adapt and implement climate change adaptation strategies.
  - Training of farmers and extension workers.
  - Capacity building for local government and civil society organizations on the socioeconomic conditions of farming.

- **Social/low literacy rate insufficient access to information and technology; limited access to markets and distribution of decision-making power:**
  - Limited community extension capacity, especially for women and youth.
  - Scant income-generating opportunities for smallholders.
  - Training of field extension officers on climate change adaptation strategies.
  - Capacity building for local government and civil society organizations on the socioeconomic conditions of farming.

- **Financial constraints; high transaction costs; difficulty in accessing resources for EIA, training, and capacity building:**
  - Insufficient representation within national institutions.
  - Limited recognition of socially marginalized groups.
  - Lack of access to financial resources made available for adaptation activities.
  - Access to sustainable and innovative financing mechanisms for programming EIA in national policies.

- **Institutional limitations; lack of institutional coordination; inadequate representation within national institutions:**
  - Weak intersectoral coordination.
  - Limited recognition of socially marginalized groups.
  - Insufficient capacity for mobilizing resources for climate change adaptation.
  - Access to sustainable and innovative financing mechanisms for programming EIA in national policies.

- **Limited access to markets through public-private partnerships (PPPs):**
  - Lack of market-based incentives for farmers to adopt EIA. Tapping into grants from private sector organizations is not an effective adaptation strategy.

## Target Areas

(See Annex 2 for additional details)

The target areas and activities are identified based on climate vulnerability defined as a function of climate change, social factors, and climate adaptive capacity (see Annex 2). The degree of climate exposure is also considered.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

## COUNTRY OVERVIEW (see Annexes 2 for details)

* **Mali** has ratified the UNFCCC, the Kyoto Protocol, the Paris Agreement and most recently the Glasgow Leaders' Climate Summit.
* The National Forest Policy 2015 and the National Agricultural Investment Plan 2017-2032 put agriculture and climate change at the center of national development strategies. These include the National Adaptation Strategy 2017 and the National Forest Policy 2015 (which emphasizes the importance of forests for soil and water and promotes sustainable forest management).
* The Government has also launched the National Land Degradation Neutrality Strategy, which aims to restore 100 million hectares of degraded land in Mali by 2030.

## THE NATIONAL CLIMATE CHANGE POLICY 2015 AND THE NATIONAL ADAPTATION PLAN 2030 see climate change as a major impediment to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and poverty reduction.
* UNFCCC, climate change is one of the largest threats to this agriculture-dependent country and climate resilient agriculture and food security are key priorities for Mali. The National Adaptation Strategy 2017 considers gender equity and equality essential, and the preparatory exercise for NAP identified inclusion of women in decision-making as a priority.
* Gender equity and equality is important in the lives of women, and gender inequality aggravates deforestation impacts.

## THE UPDATED NATIONAL DEVELOPMENT CONVENTION 2021 includes in its priorities the development of adaptation plans and strategies to address climate change. The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for Mali, covering the period up to 2035, includes the following priorities (amounts in parenthesis indicate conditional funds required for the entire implementation):
* Water management: water harvesting (USD 1 million), water conservation and soil fertility improvement (USD 10 million); integrated land use management (USD 6.7 million).
* Soil conservation for drought resilience (USD 20 million); soil conservation, soil and soil fertility improvement (USD 10 million); crop diversification (USD 5 million); agroforestry (USD 3 million); sustainable agricultural practices (USD 4 million); integrated land use management policies (USD 2.5 million).
* Water harvesting: water harvesting (USD 1 million), water conservation and soil fertility improvement (USD 10 million); integrated land use management policies (USD 2.5 million). EAFUND will directly contribute to these priorities.
* For agriculture, the NDC includes USD 6 million in conditional financial resources (for which the NAP proposes USD 6 million in conditional financing).

## SYNERGY WITH OTHER PROJECTS (see Annexes for details)

* **Mali Water Services Improvement Project (MIWASIP)** will co-finance with other GCF-funded activities in Mali (Scaling up the use of Modern Climate Information and Early Warning Systems in Mali (2015-2030, USD 16.9 million)).
* **Water Services Improvement Project (MIWASIP)** will collaborate with other GCF-funded activities in Mali (Scaling up the use of Modern Climate Information and Early Warning Systems in Mali (2015-2030, USD 16.9 million)).

* **GCF FRAE (MIWASIP)**, Scaling up the use of Modern Climate Information and Early Warning Systems in Mali (2015-2030, USD 16.9 million).

## B (2.) Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1989 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

### 20. The theory of change is based on the presented future scenarios described in the climate analysis. The later evidenced: (i) the impacts of current (observed) and projected climate change (increased temperatures and aridity, changes in precipitation patterns, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events), which will affect crop production and livelihoods, and (ii) the degrading impacts of climate change on landscapes and watershed ecosystems. These two factors will combine to increase vulnerability and reduce resilience of agricultural systems and livelihoods. The main climate effects will alter the onset of the rainy season, translate into limited and modified water availability, and lead to increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (droughts, floods, heatwaves). This will have a negative impact on livelihoods and the overall rural socio-economy. As a consequence, climate-change puts smallholders and their communities under pressure to adapt, with limited capacity for adaptation, unsustainable ecosystem use and management, further constraining ecosystem capacity to deliver services.

### 20. The push factor to transform this baseline trend, and promote a more virtuous dynamic of climate-resilient socio-ecological systems, is the adoption of an integrated land management approach (ILM) that combines Ecosystem Approach (EA) with Integrated Land Management (ILM). This will be achieved through the implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation interventions as described in section B.3. Overall goal, outputs, outcomes and activities are presented in the theory of change diagram below.

#### 20.1 Component 1: The objectives of this component are to reduce GHG emissions from improved watershed ecosystems (outcome 1.1) and to increase climate resilience of watershed ecosystems (outcome 1.2). These outcomes will be achieved through the adoption of an integrated land management approach that combines Ecosystem Approach (EA) with Integrated Land Management (ILM), as described in section B.1. The component will implement the ILM approach which involves use of biodiversity and ecosystem services to improve livelihoods, food security and climate resilience at scale. This is a paradigm shift brought by EA & ILM. The component will indeed use the ILM approach which involves use of biodiversity and ecosystem services to improve livelihoods, food security and climate resilience at scale.

#### 20.2 Component 2: The objective (outcome 2) of this component is “more climate-resilient production systems and food systems at local level”. This will be achieved by increasing the efficacy of food & feed systems through the adoption of EOA at all scales, including production systems, and an enhanced and sustainable access to markets. This will increase the resilience of smallholder farmers in terms of their livelihoods and food security, hence making their livelihoods more resilient in the context of climate change. By combining the provision of EOA through Farmers’ Associations (FAs) with the creation of a conducive environment for market access, this component will also contribute to increasing the efficacy of food & feed systems at local level.

#### 20.3 Component 3: The objective (outcome 3) of this component is to enhance the enabling environment in terms of financial and institutional support, replication and scale-up of climate-resilient watershed ecosystems and agriculture practices, ultimately facilitating mobilization, blending, and discharging of catalytic climate finance, and ensuring that these are effectively used for climate change adaptation. This will be achieved by strengthening national institutions (National Climate Change Funds) to ensure the effective use of climate funds at local level. The component will also leverage private sector experience on carbon credits as part of the exit strategy (output 3.1), complemented by improved

## Component 1 protects and restores public goods, such as ecosystem services, hence improving (i.a. water infrastructure) the livelihoods of communities in Malawi. This component will also make farming systems resilient. Farmers, having access to knowledge, climate-resilient inputs, markets, technology, and extension services, will be able to adopt climate-smart agriculture practices that provide better services to ecosystems (reciprocal relationship between farmers and nature) and thus contributing to component 1 and 2. Component 3 is the enabling environment for components 1 and 2. It includes strengthening investment flows towards climate resilient watershed ecosystems, and resilient livelihoods and food systems because of the interlinkages among them.

## The Goal Statement is “if all landscapes and watershed ecosystems are sustainably managed with the involvement of vulnerable groups; (i) farmers’ capacity to implement climate-resilient agriculture and to access markets is enhanced; (ii) water resources are protected, improved, and used efficiently for both domestic use and irrigation; then a new sustainable equilibrium for ecosystem, livelihoods and agriculture across Malawi will be attained. This will lead to the achievement of Sustainable Development Goals such as No Poverty (SDG1), Good Health and Well-being (SDG3), Gender Equality (SDG5) and Zero Hunger (SDG2).

## These results and goals are subject to the following assumptions: in village and watershed committees embrace integrated landscape management; (ii) farmers (including women and youth) adopt ecosystem based adaptation (EBA) practices, including climate-smart agriculture (CSA); (iii) extension services provide technical support and training new practices; (iv) extension workers effectively read FFS to (v) financial service providers willingly serve clients because of the interlinkages among them. These results are subject to the following risks: (i) no political will; (ii) no major socio-political shocks occur; and (iii) recovery from COVID-19 is confirmed. Results are also conditional to risk factors described in Section 7.5 of the Funding Proposal, and highlighted in the TCG dialog box below.

## The project interventions respond to the multifaceted barriers (see p.13.) to climate change adaptation in Malawi. These include:
* Planning barriers: climate-resilient landscape management for watersheds is not well integrated into Village-Level Management Committees (VLMCs) and Watershed Management Committees (WMCs).
* Institutional barriers: lack of capacity of Village Natural Resources Management Committees (VNRMCs) to prepare EBA-based Watershed Management Plans (CMPs) or Village Level Action Plans (VLAPs) through EBA, as set in the TCG.

## B

### Technical Knowledge: technical capacity to adapt to the changing climate is limited among farmers and extension workers. This barrier will be addressed under activity sets 2 and 2.1, through the implementation of VLAPs and EEA agreements, except support through GCF.

### Social and institutional adaptation: access to finance for low access to information, markets, services and technology required for climate resilient livelihoods, especially among women and other vulnerable groups. This barrier will be addressed under activity sets 2 and 2.1, through the implementation of VLAPs and EEA agreements, FFS and 4Ps, and through the improvement of financial inclusion with community-based savings and loans associations (Community-based financing organisations - CBFO) village savings and loans association (VSLA).

### Financial: Farmers have poor access to financial resources and their managerial capacity in finance is limited. This barrier will be addressed under activity sets 2 and 2.1, through the implementation of VLAPs and EEA agreements, FFS and 4Ps, and through the improvement of financial inclusion with community-based savings and loans associations (Community-based financing organisations - CBFO) village savings and loans association (VSLA).

### Institutional: Institutional capacity is limited for climate mainstreaming, mobilizing innovative finance and integrating climate change into national policies. This barrier will be addressed under activity sets 2 and 2.1, that will result in (i) more sustainable and innovative public and private climate financing through NCFD and local funds being in place; and (ii) EEA and ILM integrated in national policies and investment plans.

### Project description (max. 2500 words, approximately 8 pages)
The project aims to increase the climate change resilience of the most vulnerable rural communities at watershed level in Malawi. This objective will be achieved through the implementation of three interlinked components. The proposed project is expected to directly benefit around 270,630 individuals (about 1.4% of total Malawian population).

42. **Component 1: Integrated Landscape Management:** The objectives of this component are (i) to reduce GHG emissions, increase carbon sequestration, and enhance biodiversity; (ii) to improve the resilience of degraded ecosystems/outcomes T.2). This component will contribute to GOFC-ARSA’s Health, well-being, food and water security, and climate change adaptation goals. The landscape management activities will be implemented in a way that reduces the negative climate impact pathways from increased temperatures and rainfall variability leading to droughts and floods. **All of these components are designed to improve ecosystem services and enhance biodiversity, which is critical for build climate resilience**, which will be firmly integrated in local planning through integrated landscape management, at watershed level. The activities will also contribute to GOFC-ARSA’s Health, well-being, food and water security goals and the local residents, and increase stakeholders engagement, including women, youth and other vulnerable groups, to plan watershed restoration and climate-change adaptation interventions based on ESA and landscape approach (sub)

## Component 1: Through the implementation of integrated management plans (sub-component 1.2), the project will contribute to restoring and strengthening the functionality of public goods, in particular well-functioning watershed ecosystems and their associated services.

### 43. The component addresses the planning, knowledge, social and institutional barriers: the planning barrier by assisting watershed management planning, the knowledge barrier by capacity building necessary for planning, formation of technical committees, and stakeholder engagement, the social barrier through gender and social inclusion, and the institutional barrier on technical and social issues, including gender and social inclusion, as well as increasing social capital through participatory processes.

### 44. Activities under the component are innovative in that they accompany local communities through establishing integrated landscape management plans (ILMPs) at watershed level (holistic; participatory and integrated in local planning) to watershed managed with EIA as a technical guide. The holistic approach is based on the concept of ecosystem services, which recognizes the interdependence between water resources, land use, biodiversity, and human well-being. This approach will be applied across all levels: basin-wide (WRLU), sub-catchment and WRU micro-catchment levels. Integrated landscape management is holistic, participatory and integrated in local planning and deals with both private and communal lands. It promotes equitable and inclusive tenure in local communities.

### 45. Activities under the component build on the legacy of past projects for more sustainability. They bolster the watershed managed structures and processes defined by the government (i.e., Forestry Act 1997, Water Resources Act 2003, Watershed Management Act 2006, Forest Resource Management Act 2008, National Water Resource Management Committee (NWRMC), respectively) as Table B.3.1.1). The project will establish them where they do not yet exist and strengthen those that are already in place. These include the Watershed Management Committees (WMCs), Watershed Management plans (Sub-Catchment Management Plan - SCMP; and Village Level Action Plan - VLAP, respectively) as LMTPs, Watershed Resource Unit (WRU), Watershed Resource Unit (WRU), sub-catchment and WRU micro-catchment levels. Integrated landscape management is holistic, participatory and integrated in local planning and deals with both private and communal lands. It promotes equitable and inclusive tenure in local communities.

### 46. The component will be based on the engagement of ILM Facilitators. A total of 60 ILM Facilitators (a team of two persons for each targeted sub-catchment; one ILM Facilitator who is a national expert on community development and one ILM Facilitator who is an expert in watershed management) will be recruited to support the implementation of the project. The facilitators will be engaged through contracts with NGOs to accompany ILM stakeholders through sub-component 1.1, leading to the establishment of integrated landscape management plans (ILMPs). These include Sub-Catchment Management Plans (see sub-Activity 1.1.4), VLAPs and Village Level Action Plans (VLAP; LM plan of catchments); and SCMC, WMC, WRU, sub-catchment and WRU micro-catchment levels. Integrated landscape management is holistic, participatory and integrated in local planning and deals with both private and communal lands. It promotes equitable and inclusive tenure in local communities.

## Identification and selection of catchments within selected districts will follow a rigorous process (see Feasibility Study for more details). The stages of the targeting are:

1. **Identification of Water Resources Units (WRRUs) that cover the targeted Districts**.
2. **Selection of WRRUs that have high potential to deliver results in the Project**.
3. **Delimitation of selected WRRU catchments into WRRU sub-catchments based on hydrology and in line with the subsequent delimitation of WRRU micro-catchments based on climate vulnerability, hydrological, ecological and social considerations**.

The three stages are interlinked and will be carried out simultaneously. The process is illustrated in Figure B.3.1 for ensuring participation of local stakeholders, interest and consent.

## Activity 1.1.1: Targeting and Phasing of Sub-Catchments and Micro-Catchments

This activity will support the identification, validation, and phasing of sub-catchments and micro-catchments as described in Section B.3.3 for phased implementation. The aim is to identify a total of 111 micro-catchments contained within the targeted WRU sub-catchments for village-level intervention. The delineation will be based on the catchment boundaries, which are already available from the FAO-funded KULIMA Project. The activities will create opportunities to benefit from lessons learned under the project. The Activity will comprise of the following three sub-activities:

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.1: Identification and assessment of 80 sub-catchments and 111 micro-catchments (financed by GCF, executed by MoDLR/C)

The sub- and micro-catchments where ESM will intervene will be those that are most vulnerable to climate change impacts on water resources and socio-economic factors under the leadership of MoDepartment of Land Resources Conservation (DLR), with support from the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS). The project will engage with local communities, Water User Associations. ILM Facilitators will be engaged as observers. A rapid GIS-based assessment using GPS/GIS data on the ground will be conducted by a team of experts led by GCF and supported by MoDLR/C. This will be carried out in collaboration with an international GIS and water-resources-management expert (consultant), with the support of MoDLR/C offices, local communities, Water User Associations, and other relevant stakeholders (e.g., farmers) under the FAO-funded KULIMA Project. An average micro-catchment can contain about 10 villages.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.2: Free, prior informed consent (FPIC) and environmental and social assessment for 30 sub-catchments and 50 micro-catchments (financed by GCF, executed by MoDLR/C)

The project will finance FPIC implementation for all potential beneficiary communities to ensure their strong engagement and commitment in the project. The project will also conduct an environmental and social assessment of each identified sub- and micro-catchment. Catchments facilitators will be trained and engaged for this purpose. An assessment of environmental and social risks will be conducted by a team of experts led by GCF, with support from MoDLR/C offices, local communities, Water User Associations, and other relevant stakeholders (e.g., farmers) under the FAO-funded KULIMA Project.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.3: Validation and phasing of 30 targeted sub-catchments and 111 micro-catchments (financed by GCF, executed by MoDLR/C)

Target sub- and micro-catchments will be validated under the leadership of MoDLR/C with support from DCCMS. The project will engage local communities, Water User Associations, and other relevant stakeholders to validate each targeted catchment for inclusion in the project implementation in micro-catchments as follows: 10 start in year 2, 70 in year 3 and 30 on year 4.

## Activity 1.1.2 Capacity Development of ILM Stakeholders on Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (financed by GCF, executed by MoDLR/C)

This activity will support capacity development for ILM stakeholders on technical and social matters for effective ILM on EBA for MoEA. For this same purpose, it will support capacity development of ILM facilitators in each targeted sub-catchment to guide the landscape stakeholders of micro-catchment through planning and plan implementation. The project will provide training for ILM facilitators on Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EBA) concepts as well as climate trends; (ii) water resources management, (iii) ecosystem services and ecosystem-based adaptation, (iv) land use planning, (v) payment for ecosystem services, (vi) participatory approaches to landscape governance. The training will be conducted by experts from the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS), who has been supported in this role by GCF. Training will also be provided on the following tools: (i) Ecosystem-Based Adaptation Systems Model (EBAS-MoEA); (ii) GCF-FP; and by FAO in the Modeling System for Agricultural Impact of Climate Change (MAICCC). The training will be conducted through various trainings and planning. They will also ensure active participation of women and youth. The Activity will consist of two sub-activities:

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.1: Capacity Development for EBA Implementation

ILM Facilitators training as trainers will be provided by national experts in each of the following fields (i) EBA, including landscape demarcation, resources assessment and prioritization, stakeholder engagement, planning and implementation; (ii) climate change trends; (iii) water resources management; (iv) ecosystem services and ecosystem-based adaptation; (v) land use planning; (vi) payment for ecosystem services. The training will be conducted by experts from the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS), who has been supported in this role by GCF. Training will also be provided on the following tools: (i) Ecosystem-Based Adaptation Systems Model (EBAS-MoEA); (ii) GCF-FP; and by FAO in the Modeling System for Agricultural Impact of Climate Change (MAICCC). The training will be conducted through various trainings and planning. They will also ensure active participation of women and youth. The Activity will consist of two sub-activities:

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.2: Capacity Development for EBA Implementation

ILM Facilitators training as trainers will be provided by national experts in each of the following fields (i) EBA, including landscape demarcation, resources assessment and prioritization, stakeholder engagement, planning and implementation; (ii) climate change trends; (iii) water resources management; (iv) ecosystem services and ecosystem-based adaptation; (v) land use planning; (vi) payment for ecosystem services. The training will be conducted by experts from the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS), who has been supported in this role by GCF. Training will also be provided on the following tools: (i) Ecosystem-Based Adaptation Systems Model (EBAS-MoEA); (ii) GCF-FP; and by FAO in the Modeling System for Agricultural Impact of Climate Change (MAICCC). The training will be conducted through various trainings and planning. They will also ensure active participation of women and youth.

## Sub-activity 1.3.2.3: Gender and Social Inclusion

Vulnerability to climate change originates from social exclusion, which engenders lack of access to resources and lack of assets and economic opportunities; the very factors that are often associated with gender inequality. The objective is to ensure that women and other vulnerable groups are at the root in order to create strong ownership of the Project, and promote meaningful participation of stakeholders in technical and policy decisions.

The sub-activity will establish a social organization structure at village level for facilitating collective actions, called the Community Development Committee (CDC). This committee will be responsible for ensuring that women participate meaningfully, with emphasis on gender equality. The CDC will also ensure that all community members are involved in decision-making, relations, decision-making and share of workloads related to households) to examine decision-making power parity at the household level. The sub-activity will also establish a Gender and Social Inclusion (GSI) focal point who will act as facilitators of Dimitri Club and household methodology. ELM Facilitators’ training are trainers will be provided by international organizations, such as IUCN, to train local facilitators in the following areas: (i) gender equality and social inclusion; and (ii) gender and social inclusion. A particular focus will be put on: gender and sex, violence and harassment (VAH), including sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV); gender and sexual and reproductive health and rights (SRHR), including masculinities and patriarchy; Free Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) process; and the role of women in decision-making. The training will be conducted by ELM Facilitators, who are trained in community development and welfare, including food and nutrition security. Some religious, traditional leaders and other influential members of society will also be involved to ensure that all voices are heard.

The sub-activity will focus on Gender and Social Inclusion will promote Dimitri Clubs in all their villages of intervention, with the target group being women and girls aged 18 years or older.

### Activity 1.1.3 Strengthening/Formation of Village Natural Resources Management Committees (VNRMCs) and formulation of EBA-based Village Level Action Plans (VLAPs)

The VNRMCs will be formed in line with the guidelines provided by MoE, as well as the existing Village Natural Resources Management Committees (VNRMCs), or strengthen their functions where VNRMCs already exist. In order to ensure that the VNRMCs are representative of all stakeholders and have a strong voice at the local level, in collaboration with MoDRLRC and participation from the Department of Forestry (DoF) - will support mapping of existing VNRMCs and their functions, as well as the development of EBA-based VLAPs. The VNRMCs will be responsible for the formulation of EBA-based VLAPs and obtaining their approval from the local authorities, i.e., Village Development Committees (VDCs) at the village level; District Development Committees (DDCs) at the district level; Province Plans, and to higher administrative levels: the Area Development Committee (ADC) at the TA level; and finally to the District Executive Committee (DEC) at the provincial level. The VNRMCs will be responsible for implementing the VLAPs and manage the common natural resources. For such groups of villages, one VNRMC will given one group. VLRP elaboration will be done in collaboration with MoDRLRC and MoE, and within 30 to 50 years.

### Sub-activity

#### Sub-activity 1.3.3.1: VNRMC charter revision and membership selection

Under this sub-activity, the VNRMCs will start with a review of existing VNRMC charters (if any) and ELM Facilitators will provide guidance and advice to stakeholders through VNRMC establishment (charter elaboration and member selection - average about 18 meetings per village). The revised charter will be submitted for approval by the local authorities. In addition, gender and social inclusion, including SEAH and GBI, will be integrated in this process. Discussions among VNRMC members on the revised charters will take place five times over four consecutive weeks at each landscape or multi-cultivation. ELM Facilitators will oversee these discussions to ensure that all voices are heard.

#### Sub-activity 1.3.3.2: VNRMC Capacity Development for EBA Implementation

Training for VNRMC members will be provided by ELM Facilitators, who will train the VNRMC members in the following areas (each of them in the following fields from a strategic point of view (hence, different in content from Sub-activity 1.3.2.3): (i) climate change and its impacts on ecosystems and ecosystem-based adaptation (detailed information on EBA and its techniques are given as follows: Ecosystems and Ecosystem-Based Adaptation); (ii) gender equality and social inclusion; (iii) Free Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) process, and (iv) Gender and Social Inclusion (including FPIC and a special focus on SEAH and GBI) considerations will be mainstreamed into the trainings with the support of the Gender and Social inclusion specialist, and ELM facilitators.

## Sub-activity 1.1.3.3: Drafting and Finalizing EbA-based VLAP Under this Activity, ILM Facilitators and a national expert (ECA) will draft the VLAP document on ScMCM terms to identify strategic investment opportunities for water restoration and business development based on climate vulnerability, biodiversity, ecosystem services, and social inclusion. The VLAP process will be guided by knowledge on EbA and local socioeconomic and cultural conditions. The VLAP preparation process will bring together stakeholders from various sectors (e.g., government, civil society organizations, private sector) to discuss the following: (i) decisions on Pillars (i.e., vision, goal and objectives) and Elements of VLP in an EBA Strategy; (ii) development of a draft VLAP document based on the above discussion; (iii) review and consolidation of VLPs and endorsement of VLP by local government. In-between focused discussions, water restoration and business development opportunities will be identified for each Pillar and Element. The ILM Facilitators and ECA members will facilitate to share the conclusions of meetings, validate them and agree on the way forward. The final draft VLAP document will be shared with ScMCM members and VNRMAC members as well as other landscape stakeholders. The direct beneficiaries are about 8,000 local stakeholders.

### Activity 1.1.4: Strengthening Formation of Sub-Catchment Management Committees (SCMC) and Formulating EbA-based VLAP for Water Restoration and Business Development

This Activity will establish operational watershed management structures (SCMCs) at sub-catchment level, in line with the vision and goals of the EBA Strategy. The SCMCs will be established by ILM Facilitators in collaboration with local government officials and other partners – see Part 1 of this Facility Study. SCMP will propose strategic interventions for effective management of large catchments (e.g., water resources, land use, biodiversity, ecosystem services) at sub-catchment level where village communities cannot fully cover and address at lower level. It will also address upstream-downstream linkages of ecosystems and the environment. The SCMCs will be established in a phased manner to ensure that they are more effective at a higher catchment planning level. SCMP will improve the enabling environment, and allow scaling up replication of EbA-based VLAP for water restoration and business development in sub-catchments. The SCMCs will cover an area of about 267,500 hectares. ScCMP elaboration will be in phase, in accordance with the phases of project implementation.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.4.1: SCMC charter revision and member Selection

Under this sub-activity, that will start with a mapping of existing SCMCs (carried out by ILM facilitators). ILM Facilitators will oversee and guide landscape stakeholders in the selection of SCMC members (e.g., representatives from government, civil society organizations, private sector members per committee). Where a SCMC has already been established, ILM Facilitators will guide landscape stakeholders to review and revise its charter. The SCMCs will be established through a transparent process: the elaboration/review/revision and selection processes for transparency and fairness; awareness raising on gender and social inclusion issues; and capacity building of members. The SCMCs will be led by a chairperson, with ILM Facilitators to coordinate SCMC members and facilitate inputs on Charter and selected SCMC members from VNRMAC members.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.4.2: SCMC Capacity Development for EbA Implementation

Ten-days training for ScMCM members will be provided by national experts (ECA) staff for climate change, and consultants for the rest of themes. The training will cover the following topics: (i) climate change; (ii) water resources management; (iii) ecosystems and ecosystem-based adaptation (detailed information on EbA); (iv) biodiversity conservation and sustainable use of natural resources; (v) ecosystem services (ESs); and (vi) adaptive management. Gender and Social Inclusion Considerations (including FPC and a special focus on women and youth) will be integrated in the training, with a specific focus on gender and social inclusion specialist, and ILM facilitators focusing on Gender and Social Inclusion. The location will be each targeted sub-catchment area (SCMC), as far as feasible, to ensure that all ScMCM members are trained by VNRMAC members.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.4.3: Drafting and Finalizing EbA-based SCMP Under this Activity, ILM Facilitators and a national expert (ECA) will draft the SCMP document on ScMC terms to identify strategic investment opportunities for water restoration and business development based on climate vulnerability, biodiversity, ecosystem services, and social inclusion. The SCMP preparation process will bring together stakeholders from various sectors (e.g., government, civil society organizations, private sector) to discuss the following: (i) decisions on Pillars (i.e., vision, goal and objectives) and Elements of VLP in an EBA Strategy; (ii) development of a draft SCMP document based on the above discussion; (iii) review and consolidation of VLPs and endorsement of VLP by local government. In-between focused discussions, water restoration and business development opportunities will be identified for each Pillar and Element. The ILM Facilitators and ECA members will facilitate to share the conclusions of meetings, validate them and agree on the way forward. The final draft SCMP document will be shared with ScMCM members and VNRMAC members as well as other landscape stakeholders in the sub-catchment in question (ScMCM members, VNRMAC members, TAs and district officials) who engage in

EIA interventions funded under this sub-component will be carried out in the context of the implementation of VIAPs (as described above) and will be targeted at the communities covered by the 111 VIAPs in the 30 WRU sites. ### Activity 1.2.1 Preparation of VIAP implementation

Under this activity, relevant expertise necessary to support the implementation of VIAPs will be mobilized, looking ensuring continuity with the support provided under sub-component 1.1. This will include the preparation of a planning process for the overall implementation of VIAPs at local level. This step will also be critical to ensure the necessary inputs for VIAPs implementation are available in time and that the implementation is carried out in an efficient, effective and timely manner. The Activity will consist of the following Sub-activities:

- **Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 Planning process to initiate VIAP implementation (financed by GCF, executed by MoLCC).** This sub-activity will be completed within six months from project start-up. Following the completion of VIAP preparation, MoLCC will accompany the preparation of the planning process, with support from facilitators and the CGI team. The planning process will include a detailed work plan for each VIAP, including a prioritized list of required inputs to be compiled for submission to the National Local Government Finance Committee (NLGFC). This sub-activity will also involve the development of a monitoring framework for VIAPs, along with stakeholder meetings with respect to work involved in VIAP implementation, and in line with arrangements foreseen during the CGI process. The planning process will be completed by the end of the first year of project execution.

Training providers (international and national NGOs), for example NGOs from the Mekong Climate Smart Agriculture Alliance (MCSA), which has been engaged by MoLCC, and other organizations such as the World Agroforestry Centre have regularly collaborated with MoLCC. 56. **Component 2: Resilient livelihoods and food systems** The objective (Outcome 2) of this component is to stabilize productivity and farmers' incomes thanks to more resilient livelihoods and food systems. Combined with

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FINANCE PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

### Component 1: Building (ecosystem resilience) and building on the VLAPs developed under component 1.1, this component – which will directly benefit at least 16,200 ha of farmlands - will contribute to GCF ARMT "Most Vulnerable People" objective by supporting smallholder farmers in Malawi (sub-component 1.3) to adopt climate-smart agriculture (e.g., food and water security), and MRRA ("Forestry and land use"). By enhancing extension services through farmer field schools, the component will support the development of resilient agricultural systems that are better adapted to changing climatic conditions and enhance their capacity to integrate ESA into their farming systems. The component will support the scaling up of climate-smart agriculture practices (e.g., crop rotation, intercropping, agroforestry) through a system to access markets (sub-component 2.2) and finance – such as village savings and loans associations, micro-finance institutions, etc. - to provide smallholder farmers with the necessary inputs and services for climate-smart agriculture interventions, by boosting nature-positive food production and building resilience to vulnerable shocks and stresses; will contribute to GCF ARMT "Most Vulnerable People" objective.

#### 57. The Component addresses the planning, knowledge, social and financial barriers: the knowledge barrier by enhancing capacities necessary for adopting ecosystem-based adaptation in the production systems through FFS (sub-component 1.3); the social barrier by promoting gender equality and addressing social issues such as land tenure on technical and social issues, including gender and social inclusion (sub-component 2.1) and the financial barrier (sub-component 2.2).

#### 58. Activities under this sub-component are innovative in that they support smallholder's resilience to climate change through a food system approach. Project interventions will engage a wide range of stakeholders of the food system, including farmers, extension workers, private sector actors, and policy makers, to promote the adoption of ESA-based production systems, as well as actors supporting the value-chain functions, such as financial service providers. Through this project, smallholder farmers in Malawi will be supported to adopt climate-smart agriculture practices that create positive production systems, where a variety of climate-resistant, native and well-adapted crops are associated. Through this project, smallholder farmers in Malawi will be supported to adopt climate-smart agriculture practices that create positive production systems with public and private market operators engaged on national or international value-chain through a public-private partnership.

#### 59. Activities promoted are sustainable and replicable: The integration of the ESA in the extension system will allow replication of the approach at regional, national and local levels – with a strong scaling-up potential. Activities are sustainable because they are based on the use of locally available resources (e.g., seeds, livestock breeds adapted to weather, enhances biodiversity, reduces maladaptation and strengthens watershed/landscapes functions) and improves food security and nutrition in Malawi. The activities will be replicable as they can be easily scaled up through public services to ecosystems. Such as reciprocal relationships between farmers and nature will therefore enhance environmental sustainability and resilience of the ecosystem.

### 60. Sub-Component 2: Promotion of ESA-based production systems (Output 2.1): EEA-modes and inputs are promoted among farmers: EEA is knowledge intensive, so farmers' learning will be at the core of the investment approach. The project will promote the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices through farmer field schools and targeted technical visits and exchanges. Combined with an enhanced access to varied and adapted genetic material, FFS will contribute to the development of resilient agricultural systems that are better adapted to changing climatic conditions and the restoration of ecosystem services.

#### 61. Activity 2.1.1 EEA agriculture extension support through FFS: At the level of targeted micro-catchments, EAM will be promoted by means of Farmer Field Schools (FFS). These have proven a feasible education way for uptake of climate-resilient and ecosystem based practices through its participatory and context specific methodology (see Box 2.10). The roll out of FFS will be done in two phases: the first phase is expected to cover 3,657 farmers and the second phase of FFS implementation is expected to cut off 240 MFT 675 farmers trained and 1,080 FFS implemented, impacting 27,000 ha of land.

#### 62. The topics covered will include: (i) Conservation agriculture practices (manure management, zero/reduced tillage and crop residue management); (ii) Water management practices (e.g., water harvesting, mulching, intercropping with legumes to boost soil fertility and moisture), (iii) Agrobiodiversity and the importance of varied and adapted genetic material for climate-resilient agriculture; (iv) Climate-smart agricultural practices (e.g., crop rotation, intercropping, agroforestry, maturing, nutrient sensitive, drought-resistant and climate-resilient crops [based on Malawi-specific climate models projections], etc.); (v) Soil health management practices (e.g., composting, organic manure application), (vi) Climate-smart farming systems and practices of agrometeorology and phenoclimatic. The training will also include climate and weather information analysis and interpretation, early warning system for climate-related shocks and stresses, and the use of modernized climate information and early warning systems in Malawi" (GCF FP052). This project will be built on and build from the PICSa approach and tools (PICSa field manual), which has promoted a collaboration between
