### Sub-activity 2.1.1 Master Trainer's Course (MTC) and Training of Facilitators (ToF) (financed by GCF, executed by FAO)

The MTC will be carried out for the first time in FY 4. During FY 4, 4 MTCs will be carried out for Agriculture Extension Development Officers (AEDOs) and Agricultural Extension Service Managers (AESMs). The MTCs will cover the same topics as those that have been carried out during FY3.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2 FFS Implementation (financed by GCF, executed by FAO)

The FFS will be implemented in FY4 for the remaining targeted districts and sections where MTC were carried out in FY3. The FFS will focus on the following areas: crop production, livestock production, and practices, including the importance of agroforestry. In FY 4, after identification of potential commercial farmers, a group of 1000 farmers will be selected to participate in the FFS.

### Sub-activity 2.1.3 ITPS Implementation (financed by GCF, executed by FAO)

The implementation and scaling up of ITPS will continue during FY4 with activities from Component 1. The FFS groups will be gender-balanced, and women-specific FFSCs may be established in some cases to ensure that women farmers are included.

### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1 MTA Support for FFS roll out (financed by MoA, executed by MoA)

The Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) will provide technical support to the implementation and scaling up of ITPS extension staffs' (AEODs and AEDCs) time to technically support FFS and monitor their implementation. The Department of Extension Services (DES) will be responsible for providing training venues for the MTC and ToF courses, which include study plots as well as training centres.

### Box 2 Lessons from Farmer Field Schools

Farmer Field School (FFS), a flagship innovation, being implemented in more than 90 countries, and it is estimated that 20 million farmers globally have participated in FFS since they started. Makerere is not an exception and the extension team has been implementing FFS for over two decades. The FFS model was developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) and promoted by the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) through the National Agriculture Extension Policy as all the main extension services in Uganda are under MoA.

### Activity 2.1.2 Knowledge and Innovation (financed by GCF, executed by FAO)

EBA transition and practices adoption is knowledge intensive. Part of its adoption success and bigger outreach relies on supporting more access to technical information and knowledge. The FFS model has been used in Uganda as a platform for training extension staffs and trainees to enable different actors to have a first-hand experience and awareness on proposed EBA innovations, methods, and practices. The FFS model is also used by the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) as an entry point to reach out to farmers on one hand help enhance the technical knowledge promoted through the FFS (Activity 2.1.1), and on the other sensitive practices that are not covered in the FFS, such as climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies. The FFS model is also used by the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) as an entry point to reach out to farmers on one hand help enhance the technical knowledge promoted through the FFS (Activity 2.1.1), and on the other sensitive practices that are not covered in the FFS, such as climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies.

## Department of Agriculture Extension Services (DAES) The expected output is 5,200 people participating to visits, exchanges and short trainings, and 196,000 people accessing climate-and technical-information.

### Sub-activity 2.1.1.1 Knowledge generation and sharing
This sub-activity will support the project in the generation of knowledge and its sharing among stakeholders. A series of workshops and training sessions will be developed aiming at connecting and motivating different project stakeholders to generate, use and share good practices and lessons learned from the pilot activities. The first workshop on knowledge generation will be developed during Y1; by a national consultant that will be supporting the process of knowledge generation and sharing among stakeholders. This consultant is also responsible for the dissemination which is spearheading the Agroecology network and generating scientific and applied EAh related-knowledge.

### Sub-activity 2.1.1.2 Visits and exchanges
EAM will promote visits and exchanges on EAh approaches and practices for different types of stakeholders, with a beneficial purpose: providing real success examples in sustainable agriculture and climate change adaptation. The visits will be organized during the training sessions. Targets include (i) MIA frontline extension and research staff; (ii) NGOs present in the area; (iii) farmers’ organizations; (iv) local government officials; (v) private sector representatives; (vi) FAS facilitators (facilitators and farmers); and lead farmers-instructors; (v) visits and short trainings to project Knowledge Networks (KNs). The KNs will be organized in the following thematic areas: (i) Farmers’ groups; (ii) MIA staff and some decentralized staff to institutes that are part of the Agroecology hub network (iii), 500 farmers from different regions, who will be selected by the KNs. These farmers will be trained by specialized institutes from the PharmacueticalAgroecology Network and support them in the process of becoming multipliers.

### Sub-activity 2.1.1.2 Digital extension and climate information
The project will build on the legacy of the GCF-funded “Scaling Up the Use of Modernized Climate Information and Early Warning Systems in Malawi” (SUCEWS) to provide farmers with access to climate information, including weather data, and enable farmers and FAS facilitators to benefit from climate information and better develop climate adaptation strategies. The project will also build on the experience gained by the SUCEWS project for leveraging weather data information for it to be conveyed through SMS and community radios. The development of a Community Radio Network (CRN) is planned in the project area, where farmers will receive weather information through SMS. Community radios will be used to broadcast key weather forecasts and climate change adaptation information. Community radio stations are expected to play an important role in the implementation of the Dimaclu 2018.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2 Agroecology promotion
Agroecology—a way of life—EAh will be firmly integrated into the VLAAS (including its components) and the mainstreaming of agriculture in the context of climate change. This activity, which will replicate and scale-up seed production interventions promoted under the FAO/GEF-3 project, will focus on the development of a multi-stakeholder platform for agroecology promotion (led under component 1) and farms in order to enhance EAh and ecosystem services through agroecology. The expected output is 500 farmers participating in agroecological practices.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1 Availability of adapted genetic material financed by FAO, executed by FAD
In partnership with the different institutes specialized in genetic resources and with the capacity to provide base seed materials for EAh, FAD will finance the production of a wide range of improved crop varieties including lost crops, native and exotic trees multiplications and dissemination for multiplication (details on the display of the project activities are provided in the annex). The project will also support the implementation of the Sustainable Landscapes Impact Programmes (SPLI IP; see B.1) to launch the promotion of endangered and native seeds and the seedlings for Margolis District.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2 Initial seed and seedling availability financed by FAO, executed by FAD
As soon as year 1, EAM will identify (existing or new) seed multipliers producers associations through a participatory approach in order to establish a network of multipliers for the promotion of adapted genetic material. The multipliers will be accompanied and trained by the MIA and a specialized institute (MII) and will adopt different strategies depending on their location, such as training sessions, visits, demonstrations or setting up seed production units. These include locally adapted and native crops, as well as improved and well-adapted drought resistant varieties. The project will support community seed bank groups to evolve into a seed multiplication

- **Sub-activity 2.1.1.4 Community/individual nurseries and seed banks support (financed by GCF, executed by FAO)**: This Sub-Activity will provide technical assistance to community/individual nurseries and seed banks at a rate of $50 per ha/year. Initially, ESAM will provide technical capacity building and initial material for their establishment. The resources provided under this Sub-Activity will be used to support the communities in preparing their future viability to commercialize their products in all project areas, thus supplying the implementation of the proposed activities. This Sub-Activity will also contribute towards the development of farm-level production activities at VAP level (sub-component 2.1 and 2.4). Technical support will be provided by ESAM to community/individual nurseries and seed banks through a combination of training from specialized institutions for tree nurseries (e.g., ICRIF) and from Research Department from Mek for the seed banks. The Sub-Activity will also provide technical assistance to women-led organizations, with specialized institutions for tree nurseries (e.g., ICRIF) and from Research Department from Mek for the seed banks. This activity will target women mainly as they are the main seed carriers, highly involved in seed storage, selection, and distribution.

65. **Sub-component 2.2 Market access and entrepreneurship development (financed by GCF, executed by FAO)**: Private sector is incentivized to invest in climate-resilient agriculture; and better farmers’ access to market will be promoted through the establishment of a public-private partnership (PPP) model for the value chain of the food system. It will establish financially sustainable and diverse local EIA-based agro-food businesses, such as seed production, processing, and marketing, with the support of private sector actors. This Sub-Activity is aligned to the World Bank-funded AGCOM (see Annex 2) and micro, small and medium enterprises (MSMEs). Activities will also promote food security and nutrition by increasing access to nutritious foods for vulnerable groups through improving the relationship between food quality, nutritional traditional diets and healthy local food produced in resilient ecosystems under climate change.

66. A Market Study (annexed to the Feasibility Study) provides details on marketing opportunities for a set of selected crops and products. While the EIA approach will result in a multitude of crops being produced by smallholder farmers, the study identifies those that are most likely to be marketed successfully: cereals, pulses, vegetables, horticulture, cereals, and non-timber forest products (NTFPs) which demonstrate marketing potential for 4P commercial enterprises. These crops will also contribute towards food security and nutrition, as well as sustainability of the climate of change.

67. **Activity 2.2.1 Public-private partnership (PPPs) establishment (financed by GCF, executed by FAO)**: This Sub-Activity is focused on facilitating establishment of PPPs through a four-step process, technical and institutional development support, as well as facilitation of linkages with sub-components 1.2 and 2.4. The PPP model will be based on the following principles: (i) co-investment in the value chain; (ii) long-term sustainability and a robust business model; A4P is a structured commercial partnership between a public entity, business agencies, and private sector actors that are aligned with the project’s goals and objectives. The PPP model will define roles and responsibilities, and sharing benefits, resources and competencies. The level of capacity, formality and transparency in the PPPs will be determined by the type of enterprise involved (e.g., a formal or informal business arrangement). A4P is an entity that is engaged in a formal 4P business arrangement that can be guided by criteria described in sub-activity 2.2.1. The creation of A4Ps will be based on the following principles: (i) co-investment in the value chain; (ii) long-term sustainability and a robust business model; (iii) alignment with project goals and objectives, and (iv) facilitation of linkages with sub-components 1.2 and 2.4. The PPP model will be based on the following principles: (i) co-investment in the value chain; (ii) long-term sustainability and a robust business model; A4P is a structured commercial partnership between a public entity, business agencies, and private sector actors that are aligned with the project’s goals and objectives. The PPP model will define roles and responsibilities, and sharing benefits, resources and competencies. The level of capacity, formality and transparency in the PPPs will be determined by the type of enterprise involved (e.g., a formal or informal business arrangement) that can be guided by criteria described in sub-activity 2.2.1. The creation of A4Ps will be based on the following principles: (i) co-investment in the value chain; (ii) long-term sustainability and a robust business model; (iii) alignment with project goals and objectives, and (iv) facilitation of linkages with sub-components 1.2 and 2.4.

68. **Sub-Activity 2.2.1.1 Expression of Interest**: The focus of this Sub-Activity, under the leadership of a National Expert, is to conduct biannual calls for Expression of Interest, followed by selection of an appropriate partner (e.g., private sector) and respective due diligence process in order to establish a formal ESA farmer organization.

69. **Sub-Activity 2.2.1.2 Capacity-building for partners in the process of negotiation of 4P agreement and operators**: This Sub-Activity, under the leadership of an Institutional Development Expert, will focus on capacity building for partners involved in the process of negotiating a formal 4P agreement with operators. The Sub-Activity will be conducted through the following: (i) producers to integrate into a commercial entity (e.g., F5 Annex Market Analysis for detail on types of commercial entities and (ii) private to establish supplier liaison capacity, which are a key prerequisite to the sustainability of a potential business relationship.

### Activity 68: Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) development

#### Sub-Activity 2.2.2.1 Identification of common economic/competitive interests of farmers and enterprises
The focus of this activity will be to identify common economic/competitive interests of farmers and enterprises through the Market Analysis (see Annex A - Gender Assessment and project level action plan). ### Activity 69: Market development through "EBA product system" brand creation

The focus of this Sub-Activity, under leadership from a Brand development consultant and in collaboration with the existing EBA buy-blander brand (see FAS Appendix 2: Market Analysis for details on branding), is to create an ESBAR brand message that clearly identifies the link between food tradition, food sustainability, and biodiversity. (ii) offer EBA products in a more attractive way.

The focus of this Sub-Activity led by a local PR marketing firm will be to create an ESBAR brand message that clearly identifies the link between food tradition, food sustainability, and biodiversity. This activity will also include a campaign on public awareness of the relationship between food quality, the production ecosystem, human health and climate change. ## Sub-Activity 2.2.3.1 Linking EBA produce to traditional cuisines
The focus of this Sub-Activity will be:
(i) the promotion of traditional foods and products, the product for which has been produced under Sub-Component 2.2.2.1; (ii) the creation of demand for these traditional foods and ingredients and thus create demand for production under EBA; (iii) to provide and promote the social/cultural components of the value chain, including the promotion of the cultural heritage of the EBA producers from restaurants and end users to producers and consumers and in doing so strengthening knowledge-sharing and existing networks; (iv) to support the development of a market for traditional foods and ingredients as part of traditional cuisine; (v) to highlight seasonality of production and seasonality of recipes, especially in the context of climate change; (vi) to promote the use of local produce. These activities will be led by 10 farmers’ groups that enhance local economy and food diversity.

## Sub-component 2.3 Access to finance for climate resilient investment solutions
(Outputs: 2.3.1 Financial services; 2.3.2 Capacity building)
The objective is to provide access to financial services, including microfinance, to the beneficiaries identified for access to finance by the project beneficiaries to sustainably invest in the EBA climate resilient production processes and value chains.
About 85,000 beneficiary households including 50% women and 20% youth will access conditional financial services.

## Sub-component 2.3.1 Consolidation / expansion of Community Based Financial Organisations (funded by MoF executed by MF)
The objective of this activity is to strengthen existing Village Savings and Loans Associations (VSLAs) or create new VSLA groups in the EBA areas, with a focus on women and youth, to provide financial services beneficiaries to simple and adapted financial services. VSLAs will also be supported to link with FIs to access additional microfinance products and services. The VSLA model is being used as an entry point for MF’s membership. These VSLAs may also allow VSLA members to open formal savings accounts, which contributes to a higher level of financial inclusion in the EBA areas. This activity will be implemented by MF through its partnership with the VSLAs and insurance, mobile banking and digital services.

## Sub-Activity 2.3.1.1 Strengthening of existing VSLAs and establishment of new VSLAs in EBAIM areas
Implementing Partners which are local and international NGOs with experience in supporting CSOs/NGOs to establish and strengthen VSLA groups will be selected by MF through a competitive process led by EBAIM beneficiaries, to enable them to determine the number of VSLAs to strengthen and the number of new VSLAs to establish. The implementing partners will:
(i) provide technical assistance to existing VSLA groups; (ii) provide technical assistance to non-members; and (iii) provide technical assistance to the VSLAs in various thematic areas based on a capacity assessment.
The objective will be to study various sustainability models implemented in neighboring countries and, based on this analysis, develop a model that can be adapted for use by MF. The model should also contribute to the visibility of the VSLA and its services to their members, including EBAIM beneficiaries. MoF will provide technical assistance to the implementing partners to establish and strengthen these modules. These modules will be disseminated to project beneficiaries by the Implementing Partners, which will participate in training sessions.

## Sub-Activity 2.3.1.2 Technical backstopping and implementation support; knowledge management and dissemination
The objective of this activity is to provide technical backstopping and implementation support to MF’s Implementing Partners based on outcomes of its monitoring and evaluation and supervision missions. EBAIM beneficiaries will be involved in the selection process for implementing partners, as well as in the identification of new VSLA groups. The lessons learned from the project will be disseminated through various platforms including publications, conferences, workshops, seminars, training sessions at national and international conferences. MF through FARMRE will also organize at least one knowledge dissemination workshop with the participation of EBAIM beneficiaries, Reserve Bank of Malawi, Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs, Ministry of Agriculture and of NGOs and Projects involved in rural and inclusive finance development, to further share and scale-up lessons

## Component 3: Enabling Institutional and Financial Environment

The objective (Outcome 3) of this component is to enhance institutional capacity to resiliently manage climate risks in smallholder communities and agriculture practices. This will be achieved by leveraging public and private climate finance (sub-components 3.1, 3.2, 3.3, and 3.4). The component will also address the sustainability and scaling-up of ESA activities and well-functioning watershed management systems (Component 3.5).

This component will address the institutional barriers as per the theory of change, these include: (i) insufficient institutional capacity to mobilize innovative public and private climate finance and (ii) limited integration of ESA in national

### B

**policies**, it will be responsive to low and volatile investments in integrated landscape management and address the need to propose innovative solutions to attract more sustainable climate finance.

#### 7. Activities promoted under this component are sustainable and innovative. The Project will build on Malawi’s vision for a green economy, as expressed through the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) and MEED Act (2013). Together, the activities will facilitate the mobilization of catalytic climate finance, and contribute to sustaining, replicating and scaling up climate change adaptation and mitigation actions. The proposed technical assistance component is innovative because it will leverage a private sector experience on carbon credits (Climate Asset Management – CAM) and other market-based instruments (e.g., REDD+). It will also build on the lessons learned from existing national policies and investment plans, and bring the EEA agenda at sectoral policy levels. By integrating EEA in national policies, the Project will contribute to the development of a conducive regulatory and scaling up of EEA through other projects and programmes – for massive-scale impact and sustainability.

#### Sub-component 3.1. Leveraging public and private climate finance (Output 3.1): Catalyze and innovative public-private partnerships (PPPs) in support of Malawi’s NCCF, as well as the National Environment Fund (NEF), the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) (activity 3.1.1) and local national conservation trust funds (Malawi Environment Conservation Trust Fund – MECTF) (activity 3.1.2). The Project will support the NCCF in developing innovative interventions, based on EEA (activity 3.1.2); it will also leverage private sector experience on carbon credits in Malawi as part of a broader strategy to catalyze public-private partnerships and to improve an enabling environment, more particularly on enhancing access to climate finance and instruments.

#### Activity 3.1.1: Support to Malawi’s National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) initiated by the GFCS, executed by the EEA. The NCCF is a multi-stakeholder fund that will be established in accordance with the MEED Act. It will focus primarily on ecosystem restoration, watershed resilience and climate-resilient agriculture, based on EEA. The Project will support the NCCF to develop innovative interventions for climate change adaptation and mitigation under the EEA under components 1 and 2. The Project’s proposed technical support to the NCCF, which will start on year 2, will make use of existing experience in Malawi and elsewhere in Africa, as well as lessons learned from other vulnerable watersheds, for increased impact. In line with the GFCS’s Standard Operating Procedures (SOP) proposed technical assistance component, the Project will support the NCCF to:

- **Sub-activity 3.1.1.a: Support to resources mobilization:** The objective of this sub-activity is to support the NCCF to mobilize climate finance. The Project will notably assist the NCCF to meet GCF accreditation requirements, including the development of a business plan and a project proposal. The NCCF has already established well-established and experienced. Direct access is properly identified in the NCCF SOP. Under a longer-term horizon, the Project will support the NCCF to develop innovative private sector financial instruments – which could include for example green bonds.
- **Sub-activity 3.1.1.b: Support to pipeline building:** The NCCF has two priority investment areas for climate change adaptation. These include (i) integrated watershed management, and (ii) climate change community resilience. In the first area, the Project will support the NCCF in developing a portfolio of projects in vulnerable watersheds. Areas EAM will finance investment strategies, with the objective to build a pipeline of quality adaptation projects. The second priority area is climate change community resilience. These include (i) the development of criteria for targeting water users; (ii) climate vulnerability, ecosystem services degradation and biodiversity loss; (iii) identification of potential adaptation options; (iv) assessment of their effectiveness/feasibility; (v) a list of potential projects to be funded; including EEA-based beneficiaries (communities, households, etc.); (vi) development of an investment strategy; (vii) the establishment of IMAs; (viii) monitoring, verification and reporting framework (MVFR). EAMS’s SMART outcomes/deliverables indicators and reporting framework will be used to monitor progress.

- **Sub-activity 3.1.1.c: Capacity development on EBA:** Training on the main EBA techniques listed in the previous sub-activities will be provided by EAMS, as well as other relevant training modules. The Project will support the NCCF, for better investment financing decisions. Training will include (i) deep dive into the EBA techniques (as described above), and (ii) a review of the lessons learned from existing national policies and investments and benefits, based on EAMS’s experience. Training – conducted by FAO staff and consultants – will also build on the evidence-based studies prepared under sub-component 3.2 on policy dialogue.

#### Sub-component 3.1.1.a: Capacity development on EBA (Output 3.1.1): The Project will support the NCCF priority area of a capacity development plan and knowledge transfer). EAMS will provide targeted capacity development on climate vulnerability analysis. Training (see Feasibility Study for details), conducted by FAO staff and consultants, will

## Sub-activity 3.1.1.5: Capacity development on carbon balance analysis.

In line with NDC priority area 4 (Capacity development and knowledge transfer), ESAM will deliver trainings to NSCF on GHG accounting for FAO projects, using the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model (GLEAM) tools, which are built on the latest scientific evidence. The training will cover the use of GLEAM to assess the carbon balance and potential of projects financed by NCF in the agriculture, forestry and other land use change (AFOLU) sectors.

### 81. Activity 3.1.2: Support to National Conservation Trust Funds (funded by GFCS; executed by FAO).

This intervention will support NSCFs in their operations, and implement their mandate to yield positive environmental and natural resource conservation, in the context of NDCs.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.2.1: Technical assistance for national entities in accessing GCF resources.

This intervention would support MEET in accessing, for example, the GCF Project Specific Assessment Approach (PSAA) and the GCF Project Design Document (PDD). The Project will also provide technical assistance to NSCFs in applying for GCF as a priority for Malawi’s National Designated Authority - NDA (Environmental Affairs Department - EAD). The Project will support MEET in developing a project proposal, including the engagement of stakeholders (government and social safeguards), together with the institutional and capacity assessment of MEET. See Part 2 of the Financial Plan.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.2.2: Feasibility studies to develop innovative financial instruments for ecosystem restoration.

To support implementation of MEET’s Resources Mobilization Strategy (RMS, 2019–2023), the Project will conduct feasibility studies on innovative financial instruments for ecosystem restoration in Malawi. One instrument identified during design are debt-for-nature swaps (DNS). The Project will support MEET in developing a DNS proposal and conducting a DNS feasibility study.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.2.3: Capacity development on Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E).

The Project will support the Conservation Trust Funds to develop indicators for the Fund’s monitoring, verification and reporting framework. This will include capacity building of NSCFs in M&E, as well as developing a framework that would contribute to the Trustee & MSAs’ synergies. With activity 3.1.1 it will also be explored for ‘Trust funds staff’ to become part of the MEET team.

### 82. Activity 3.1.3: Leveraging private sector experience on carbon credits in Malawi as part of the exit strategy (funded by the GCF; executed by FAO).

This activity will establish the enabling environment to leverage private sector experience and expertise for the development of a carbon credit market in Malawi, financed by Climate Asset Management (CAM) – a joint venture between GFCS/IBRD Asset Management and Prolifikon. The resulting carbon credits would be used as an incentive for private sector investment in climate-smart land restoration practices (use donor mapping in the feasibility study). GEA, through the leadership of CAM, will support NSCFs to develop a market-based approach that will provide incentives for private sector land restoration activities with access to carbon credits. CAM is investing in the Restore Africa Programme against the future revenue from carbon credits generated by the project. The Project will also support NSCFs in engaging with communities allowing them to benefit from the carbon revenues. Carbon credits will be more and better diverse than those currently available on the market, as they will cover land restoration activities over a period of 30 years or longer (up to 100 years – allowing for continuous and long-term ecological impact). The Project will also support NSCFs in engaging with private sector entities. Based on the following two criteria: (i) projects that have been approved by ESAM for financing locally, with the sale carbon credits as a mechanism to attract further financing similar to the Restore Africa Programme.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.3.1 Gap analysis and baseline.
This sub-activity will aim at performing a gap analysis and baseline study on the potential of private sector engagement in carbon credit markets in Malawi, with the objective to assess potential for proposing carbon credit in ESAM’s areas (preferably in one or two districts that Restore Africa Programme is already active). The gap analysis will also allow to identify possible additional element to the Project’s exit strategy. The gap analysis will allow to assess whether technical solutions (in terms of potential for promoted trees and shrubs to sequester carbon, above

## Sub-activity 3.1.2: Continuous learning for possible replication.

Under this sub-activity, EASAM will enhance the capacity of the countries involved in the Restore Africa Programme to consolidate the learning process on Restore Africa Programme activities in Malawi and Mozambique. This will be achieved through a series of training workshops involving the involvement of other EASAM partners (NCFCT, MEET, Shire BST, MFIs), etc., using EASAM intervention sites as case studies.

### Sub-component 3.2: Scalability in national policies.

(Drop-out 3.2.EA integrated into national policies). This sub-component will provide the enabling environment by promoting EASAM as a key instrument for climate change adaptation and mitigation, through policy briefs, capacity building workshops, and training of trainers. The inclusion of EASAM principles by embedding its practices into policies, sectoral planning and financing – including government budget – hinges on the development of a set of policy briefs that will provide an overview of the benefits of EASAM. A series of sectoral strategies can secure the financial support and human resources needed for it to become viable in the long term.

### Activity 3.2.1: Preparation of evidence-based studies required by the GCF, executed by EASAM

The project will conduct a baseline study on the socio-economic performance of EASAM using the FAO Tool for Agricultural Performance Evaluation (TAFE). The study will be conducted in collaboration with the CGIAR Research Program on Water, Land and Ecosystems. These studies will play a key role in proposing policy changes. Results of the cases will be compiled into recommendations (briefly) with the objective to inform the GCF.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1.1: Policy briefs based cost-benefit analysis (CBA).

As part of the M&E system, the project will conduct CBA – which will be done on farms and communities that have benefited from EASAM support. The CBA will include a detailed description of the project activities, the expected benefits, the net present value (NPV), internal rate of return and annual cash flows. The project will finance policy briefs to highlight the efficiency of EASAM in terms of cost-benefit analysis.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1.2: Policy brief TAFE analysis.

Impact of EASAM on production systems will carry out analysis of the socio-economic performance of EASAM, using the FAO Tool for Agricultural Performance Evaluation (TAFE). The study will be conducted in collaboration with CGIAR Research Program on Water Land and Ecosystems system with regard to various dimensions of EASAM (environmental, social, economic), in the targeted water management systems. This study will provide a basis for policy recommendations (briefly) within component 3.1. This study will evidence the wider benefits of EASAM in relation to sustainable development, in terms of poverty reduction and food security.

### Activity 3.2.2: Policy Assessment and preparation of a policy influencing plan (PIP), financed by EASAM

For this activity, The Project – through the GEF-managed GEFP project - will collaborate with the CGIAR Research Program on Water Land and Ecosystems to develop a policy influencing plan in line with the direction, inter-ministerial coordination (including MCA, MoCCNRI, MoF) and policy guidance in relation to Malawi’s national priorities for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The PIP will be presented at the National Seminar that will be held on 20th October 2017.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.2.1: Policy Influencing Plan (PIP).

Through the TADGEF-7 DGP Project, EASAM will partner with the Centre for Environmental Policy and Advocacy (CEPA) to carry out a policy review process. The aim of this policy review process will be a Policy Influencing Plan (PIP) (i.e. a policy advocacy plan) including the identification of key stakeholders involved in land restoration, integrated landscape management and biodiversity conservation into the national policy framework.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.2.1: Dissemination of the PIP.

The PIP will be presented at a National Seminar that will be held on 20th October 2017. The PIP will also be disseminated through workshops involving policy-makers, civil servants, research/academics, extension organizations, farmer organizations, private sector, development partners and NGOs. PIP will also be disseminated through the production of


---
**Page 45**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Activity 3.2 Implementation of the PIP (financed by GCF; executed by FAO)

### Sub-activity 3.2.1 Policy dialogue

Based on the PIP proposed by the NACC/FAO DSS Platform and using existing policy dialogue mechanisms (e.g., national-level studies) under activity 3.2.1, the Project will launch an iterative series of 5 policy dialogue events led by NACC/DAC at national level to provide information about existing policies and strategies related to ILM and LRM with GCF support, as well as to discuss how these can be integrated with EIA regulated policy dialogue events that will target the following stakeholders: (i) policy makers; (ii) civil servants; (iii) farmers’ representatives; and (iv) other relevant stakeholders. The Project will also engage with the Parliamentary Committee on Agriculture and Natural Resources who can lobby during budget sessions for more funds to support ILM.

### Sub-activity 3.2.2 Update of policies and strategic frameworks

These include the Agrobiodiversity Policy, the National Agriculture Investment Plan (current NAP covers 2017–2023), National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP) (2015–2024), and the National Land Use Management Strategy (NLUMS). The Project will support the implementation of ILM with EIA integrated. The Project will organize public events to help make an advocacy case for ILM, and will also provide training on ILM to key stakeholders in order to encourage individuals to publicly advocate for the proposed changes.

## B.4 Implementation arrangements (max 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

### Accredited Entity

#### FAFI will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for this Project. As such, FAFI will be responsible for the overall management of the Project and will ensure that the Project is implemented in accordance with the GCF policies and guidelines, including the following: (i) ensuring that all project activities are carried out within the scope of the approved Project Document; (ii) ensuring that all project activities are carried out in a manner consistent with the GCF’s (v) project closure and evaluation. FAFI will ensure these responsibilities in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAFI and GCF.

#### Sub-Regional Office for Southern Africa located in Harare, Zimbabwe and the Country Representation Office in Lilongwe, Malawi.

### Roles to fulfill by the AE functions

FAFI will set up a dedicated Project Task Force (PTF) in line with GCF project cycle guidelines. The PTF will be composed by the Budget Holder (BH), the Lead Technical Officer (LTO), (funding Lien Officer) (fLO), and the Country Representative (CR).

### The PTF will remain independent from the Executing Entity functions also performed by FAO

In line with the GCF policy on fees added through GCF Board Decision 18/09, the above-mentioned segregation of responsibilities between the AE and the EE will be respected. This is in accordance with the AE functions listed in the GCF General principles and indicative list of slight costs covered under GCF fees and project management.

## Project co-financing

### Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (MoFE), the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), and FAO will provide co-financing to the project, respectively in the form of cash (grant) for MoF; in-kind for MoA; and cash (grant) for FAO. The co-financing provided by MoF and MoA is subject to the availability of funds from their respective national budgets in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the GCF policies as well as AMA, Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) between FAO and GCF and the Project Agreement (Subsidiary Agreements signed between FAO and the Government of Malawi, etc.

## B

**MA and MoF are responsible for executing and managing their co-financing under the coordination of the Project Implementation Unit (PIU) - see below- and through the Project Steering Committee - PSC (see Project execution section below).**

* **The Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs will provide in-cash (grant)-co-financing through the FARMIE Programme, for a total amount of 6.4 million USD. Activities co-financed are described in sub-component 23 of this funding proposal.**
* **The National Department of Agriculture will provide in-kind co-financing for a total amount of $95 million USD (in the form of: i) salary/taxes of the Head of District for agriculture, who will contribute to the Project during the DFS; ii) the following services provided by the District Agricultural Officer (DAO): training and mentoring of farmers (training coordinator - AEDC); who will contribute to the Project during the DFS and iii) training venues (training centers for extension officers).**

* **FAO will provide in-cash (grant)-co-financing through the GEF-7 Sustainable Forest Management, Dyland Sustainable Landscapes Impact Programme, DSLI, for a total amount of $23,000 USD and FAO’s Technical Cooperation Project 19845, “Support to Malawi’s National Forest Policy and Strategy Development, Forest Assessment and the preparation of a policy influencing plan; on production of seeds and seedlings of native plant species in the context of the implementation of the National Forest Policy and Strategy (NFP/S) under the GEF-7 Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) consultation and an environmental and social assessment. Through this contribution, FAO will contribute to the GCF-GEF linkage, and long-term vision for complementarity.**

## Execution Entities

92. The project will be executed by FAO and the Government of Malawi acting through: (i) the Ministry of Agriculture - MoA (specifically the Department of Land Resources Management - DLRC), (ii) the National Local Government Secretariat (NLGS), and (iii) the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) under the GEF-7 Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) Programme(s), in a co-execution model to deliver the project activities funded by GCF process (see below for details). Additional support will be provided by the Government of Malawi acting through MoA and MoF.

93. FAO will act as EE and will ensure strong country-driven execution of project activities and will be in charge of the selection of activities funded by GCF processes based on its comparative advantages. This will allow Malawi to benefit from the technical expertise, experience and resources available within the United Nations system (UN), while providing opportunities for the government to increase their capacity through technical assistance and development of institutional capacities under the three components. FAO will execute the activities contained in the FAO managed GEF-7 project.

94. Flow of funds and legal arrangements: After the signature of the MoU, FAO will enter into a Project Agreement (Subcontract) with MoA to implement the activities listed on the Project Implementation Plan for the project, including MoA (DLRC), NLGS and MoF (FAWE). The Project Agreement will include 1) provisions on the Contractual Arrangements between the parties; 2) the terms of payment and disbursement of funds to be provided by the Government of Malawi acting through MoA and MoF, and 3) the respective roles and responsibilities of the parties. Funds disbursed under this Project Agreement will be subject to the financial management and project implementation arrangements outlined above. GCF proceeds received by FAO in its capacity as Accredited Entity will flow to the Project Account established with the Government of Malawi acting through MoA and MoF for the listed activities (figure B.4.1), a summary of key activities also illustrating the flow of funds is presented in table B.4.1 below. More details on the financial arrangements are provided in annex 2, which includes the terms and conditions of GCF responsibilities, as well as EE’s and CoE’s, as well as NLGCS responsibilities as EE, together with all financial details, will be included in the Project Agreement, which will also serve as subsidiary agreement, signed by the Government of Malawi.

## Guidance of the project and project technical committee (PTC)

The PTC will be responsible for ensuring overall efficient management, coordination, implementation and monitoring of the project through the effective implementation of the annual work plans and budgets (WPBs). The PTC will consist of four members: (a) a representative from MoA (e.g. MoA Agriculture & Food Security Specialist – NRM specialist), (b) a representative from MoF (e.g. MoF Agricultural Extension Specialist – AES – part-time 5%), (c) a national safeguards specialist, and (d) an M&E / GIS and/or xii) and (v) xiii) and (vi) Knowledge Management Specialist (part time). All PTC members will be appointed by MoA and will be responsible for overall project management and coordination with project stakeholders. The PTC will also provide guidance on new project risks, agree on possible countermeasures and management actions to address specific risks, review the project progress, and provide direction.

## Project Coordination Unit (CPU)

The CPU will coordinate closely with the two Regional Project Implementation Units (RPIUs), which will be established in Bishnupur (Southwest Region) and Mizoram (North Eastern Region). RPIUs will supervise the day-to-day project implementation. The CPU will consist of four members: (a) a representative from MoA (e.g. MoA Agriculture & Food Security Specialist – NRM specialist), mentioned above, (b) a representative from MoF (e.g. MoF Agricultural Extension Specialist – AES – part-time 5%), (c) a national safeguards specialist, and (d) an M&E / GIS and/or xii) and (v) xiii) and (vi) Knowledge Management Specialist (part time). All CPU members will be appointed by MoA.

## Other Project Partners

### In addition to the above EE, the project will engage relevant government counterparts from MoA (e.g. Gene Bank within the Research Services), Ministry of Climate Change and Natural Resources – MiCCNR (e.g. DC/CCS), National Agricultural Research Institute (NARI) (e.g. NARI), Department of Agriculture (MoA) (e.g. Department of Agriculture and Natural Resources – LUNAR), training centres (Paradise Permaculture Centre, Kumaon Permaculture Development Centre, etc.), non-governmental organizations (NGOs) (e.g. CARE International, World Vision, etc.), service providers and the private sector (e.g. Malawi Industrial & Agricultural Investment Corporation – MIACIC).

### In addition, several partners will be engaged in the project either to ensure complementary with their activities or to provide specific services. These partners include: (a) a representative from MoF (e.g. MoF qualified and experienced service providers NGOs) from the Malawi Climate Smart Agriculture Alliance (MiCSAA), which have extensive and recommendations to ensure that the agreed deliverables are produced satisfactorily according to plans (i.e., review and agree with annual work plan) and provide necessary strategic guidance for its implementation; (iv) appraise the annual project progress report, including any adjustments made in the annual work plan, and approve the revised annual work plans to build on achievements and address shortcomings; and (v) provide ad hoc direction and advice for executing agencies as required.

101. The PSC will be expected to meet formally at least once every 12 months. Formal meetings will be scheduled and arranged by the National Project Coordinator in consultation with, and at the request of PSC members (with tentative dates for the next meeting being agreed upon). Any other meeting of the PSC can be requested by any of its members.

102. The PSC will be supported by the Project Technical Committee (PTC), which will review and, where needed, advise on technical aspects of the project implementation. The PTC will also be chaired by the GCF Representative for Conservation and FAO representation in Malawi, who will be the Secretariat for both the PSC and the PTC. Other members of the PTC will include representatives from the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR), Planning Services, Animal Health and Livestock Development, and Agriculture Research Services (including the Gentele Centre). The PTC will also have representation from relevant CSOs and NGOs in the sector such as FUMI, FUNMI, NURVA, and relevant CSOs and NGOs. The PTC will: (i) technically oversee activities in their sector; (ii) ensure a full alignment of project activities with the work plan of their agency; (iii) provide technical advice to the PSC on links between the project activities and the work plan of their agency; and (iv) facilitate the provision of co-financing to the project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Making a difficult choice: How can a country meet its needs (through debt finance) in terms of climate finance for a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient practices and land restoration?

105. The Government of Malawi requests maximum concessionality to support vulnerable farmers to adapt to increasing temperatures, more frequent droughts, floods, and other extreme weather events. These interventions will play a catalytic and transformative role in (i) unlocking additional investment in climate-resilient agriculture and resilient ecosystems; (ii) covering the cost of adaptation measures for smallholder farmers; (iii) supporting the integration of climate change adaptation change in their landscapes/watersheds and production systems; (iv) repair ecosystems affected by climate change; and (v) ensuring that the investments made are sustainable. The proposed interventions will also ensure that there is a sufficiently stable revenue base for continued investment in adaptive responses to climate change.

106. The NDC 2021 of Malawi puts the estimated funding requirements for climate change adaptation at USD 1,23 billion (USD 859 million from the public sector and USD 374 million from external sources). Of this amount, USD 818 million is to be supplied by external sources for 2020-30. For agriculture, livestock and fisheries, roughly USD 600 million from external sources are required over the period of 2020-30. The NDC also estimates that USD 55 million would be required per year in climate change adaptation investments to support smallholder farmers’ livelihoods and improve their resilience to the size of the problem. With respect to mitigation, USD 4.21 billion is necessary for 2020-2030, of which 2.55 billion is to be supplied by external sources over this period. The NDC estimates that USD 67 million per year in climate change crop management requires some USD 11 billion during 2020-2040, half of which is to depend on the international community for funding. In total, the NDC estimates that USD 583 million (USD 495 million from external sources) over this period is to come from external sources. References to these data are found in the endnotes.

107. A number of investments (funded by e.g., the World Bank, the GEF or the EU) have been made and are being implemented under the NDC 2021. These include: (i) the Malawi National Forests Investment Plan (NFIP), which covers a range of interventions; The NFIP (2017 report) that reports that (ii) up to 3.7 million hectares (35% of Malawi’s total area) have an opportunity for and suitability for land restoration, reforestation and afforestation activities. (iii) Up to 48% of Malawi’s total area) have an opportunity for soil and water conservation. The NDC for Malawi (from June 2021 also includes a component on climate change adaptation in the form of a National Adaptation Plan (NAP). However, COP proceeds will, through the EBM project, contribute to addressing this financing gap.

108. Co-financing with other initiatives has been thoroughly identified, including funding by the Malawi Rainforest Fund (MRF) and the World Bank’s Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF). The Project also secures national co-financing from the Ministry of Agriculture (2.95 billion) and co-financing from FAO (5 million USD). Government co-financing will be used to support the implementation of the project activities, including the creation of new financing opportunities, which include the Restore Africa Programme (under preparation), financed by Climate Asset Management (CAM) and the World Bank’s Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF). The Project also seeks co-financing from the Malawi Agricultural and Industrial Investment Corporation (MAIC) – as mentioned in sub-component 2.3 (in section B.6).

## Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project design includes several features to enhance sustainability. Its support for the DCA (UNDP), UNICEF, and institutional strengthening of the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) will ensure that the Project is sustainable beyond its implementation period. The Project also supports the development of a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) under the NDC 2021. The Project’s support (more particularly under component 3) for the establishment of an institutional framework to manage and monitor land use change (land tenure management) will be included in VAPs, and expected to be covered through local governments (districts) funding; sustainable financing mechanisms such as carbon credits, payments for ecosystem services, and other innovative regulatory frameworks in the field, more particularly the Decentralization Act (which devolves funding from central Government to districts).

109. The financial inclusion of smallholder farmers, FOBs and SMEs with local finance institutions, together with partnerships with financial instrument providers (e.g., MCAF) developed (and enhanced by) the MoF-CARMA (co-financing arrangements for climate change adaptation), will ensure that the Project is sustainable beyond its implementation period. In addition: (i) support to the Malawi Climate Change Fund (NCCF); (ii) linkages with national conservation initiatives such as the Restore Africa Programme; and (iii) the development of a National Adaptation Plan (under preparation) to leverage experience on carbon credits, will facilitate mobilization of climate finance, and contribute to the sustainability of the Project. The Project also seeks co-financing from the Malawi Agricultural and Industrial Investment Corporation (MAIC), which is expected to expand the scale of investments promoted by the Project under component 1 and 2. Interventions will mainstream and integrate deep-root ESA in national policies and investment plans, and bring the ESA agenda at sectoral policy levels.

## C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)

### C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building development/transfer?

Yes ☑️ No ☐

**110.** Community members and farmers learning on EIA are at the core of the investment strategy - and will amount $5.53 million of GCF funding. For this reason, capacity development activities will be mainstreamed across the three components of the project. These include (i) strengthening the institutional framework for EIA, i.e. technical knowledge, planning, social, financial and intellectual capability. Under component 1, capacity development focuses on building the sustainability of the project and the communities through capacity measures, gender and social inclusion with a special focus on FPIC and SEAM and GBV; and will target (i) the formulation and implementation of EIA-based VLPAs, and (ii) CMCs for the preparation of EIA-based CMPS. Under component 2, capacity development focuses on building the sustainability of the project and the communities through capacity measures, gender and social inclusion with a special focus on FPIC and SEAM; and will target (i) targeted trainings, (ii) capacity development to SMEs for business plan preparation and (iii) capacity development to local authorities for EIA-based VLPAs. Under component 3, capacity development focuses on institutional capacity building for the sustainability of the project and the communities through capacity measures, gender and social inclusion with a special focus on FPIC; and will target (i) policy makers. These capacity development activities, combined with an enhanced access to finance to undertake EIA-based VLPAs, will enable the communities to positively adapt, safeguard the natural resource base, and provide better services to ecosystems.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section reviews the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

1.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project aims to reduce the vulnerability of the most vulnerable rural communities at watershed level in Malawi, where climate change accelerates the vicious cycle of low agriculture productivity and poverty. The project will support these communities to adaptively manage their land resources so that they can anticipate, absorb, accommodate or recover from extreme weather events and natural disasters resulting from climate change. This will be achieved by improving soil health and water retention in agricultural systems, thereby increasing livelihoods in the face of threats that impact agriculture and hence the economy, food security and nutrition (adapted from GCF’s Initial Investment Framework).

1.16. EEMF will support vulnerable Malawian rural communities to positively adapt to the impacts of climate change and improve their livelihoods through the adoption of EAE measures, including low-emission climate-resilient agricultural practices, such as agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and improved soil management in functioning ecosystems surrounding ecosystem-type farms bring multiple benefits for producers, including higher yields from more productive soils, increased resilience to drought and floods, reduced erosion, enhanced soil organic matter and soil structure, which are continuously deteriorating under conventional agriculture. Improved soil health also leads to better water retention capacity of the land, which is critical in mitigating against floods. Farms are also resilient when strengthened as ecosystems; diversity means that the farm as a whole is less vulnerable to climate change impacts than a monoculture farm (adapted from GCF’s Initial Investment Framework). It has been proven to increase productivity in developed countries. [2] Further, an ecosystem-based approach to production and land management can be more cost-effective for smallholder farmers compared with chemical fertilizers. In Malawi, it has been shown that EAE agriculture generates higher benefits in agriculture (higher yields) than conventional agriculture. However, the adoption of EAE practices is not yet widespread in Malawi’s agroecology; its precise effects are subject to socioeconomic, cultural and environmental factors of each farm.[3] For example, the adoption of EAE practices by smallholder farmers depends on their access to extension services, productive farms support farmers to safeguard the natural resource base and improve their income and nutrition, and vulnerability. The project will provide training in agroecology and other related skills through a combination of on-farm demonstrations, field visits, and trainings. It will also provide financial incentives for farmers who adopt EAE practices and access finance. Through its interventions, the project will contribute to GCF ARPA “Aim at Vulnerable Peoples and Communities” (with emphasis on women) and GCF ARPA “Ecosystems and Ecosystem Services” and GCF ARPA “Forestry and Land Use”.

1.17. The project is expected to be implemented in 2019/2020, with a total of 35,846 hectares under climate change (1.42% of the total area) within 11 pre-defined districts. Within each of the pre-targeted districts, the project will target 70% of the households in the targeted communities and 30% of the households in the non-targeted communities, with a total of 1,115 villages. The project investment logic is based on an integrated landscape development approach, with the aim to improve livelihoods through climate change adaptation and mitigation. The project will be scaled up from the pilot phase (2018-2019) in the Chitipa District, which has been identified as a priority area for scaling-up. The project is expected to have an impact potential based on the number of people and households being part of the villages in the targeted communities. The project will be implemented through the Malawi National Forest Service (MNFS), with support from the Feasibility Study (section 5.4). Based on use data disaggregated data at district level data from the 2018 household census, the project expects to reach 94% of villages in the targeted communities.

1.18. The project will target households within the pre-defined micro-watersheds but belong to the overall selected sub-catchments as indirect beneficiaries. These indirect beneficiaries are individuals who do not live within the targeted communities and whose livelihoods depend on the ecosystem services provided by the targeted catchments (SCMAs and EEA-based SCMAs developed under component 1). The total number of indirect beneficiaries is then 69,100 households (30% of the total).

1.20. FACI-Area Based Balance Tool (SIA-Tool) has been used to estimate in detail in eddies (in CO2eq per year) the mitigation impact of carbon sequestration and avoided deforestation. Edi results (see Annex 22 for details on methodology) suggest a mitigation potential in the range of [170,325-323,869] CO2eq/year over 20 years or [137,518-137,518] CO2ee/year.

1.22. Paradigm shift (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will contribute to a paradigm shift in the Malawian agricultural equilibrium for ecosystems, livelihoods and agriculture across Malawi, from their baseline state of very high vulnerability—in terms of high exposure, high and low adaptive capacity to more frequent and intense extreme climate events—to an alternative paradigm in which


---
**Page 57**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 53

121. To achieve this, the project will promote the locally-led EEA approach combined with integrated landscape management (ILM) to ensure that landscapes are managed in a way that promotes climate-resilient agriculture and other livelihoods to become resilient to climate change. The EEA approach – which is the core component of the project – is based on the principles outlined by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Paris Agreement, and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (2017). The latter recommends: development of adaptation planning tools; ecosystem-based adaptation; integration of climate change considerations into land use planning; promotion of sustainable agriculture and agroforestry; sustainable forest management; and riparian restoration. Ecosystem-based management of landscapes is a key component of the project, which will be implemented through the Integrated Landscape Management (ILM) approach to promote resilience in both terrestrial ecosystems and water resources and watersheds under the projected climate change scenario.

122. The paradigm shift brought forth by the project relies on applying EEA through ILM to fundamentally transform landscapes into climate-resilient systems that are more productive, sustainable, and resilient. This will enable them to positively adapt, safeguard the natural resource base, and provide better services to ecosystems (reciprocal relationships) and people (socio-economic benefits). The project will focus on developing a landscape-level (watershed) level large enough to facilitate climate change adaptation at scale. In the context of Malawi’s water resources, this will be achieved through the development of Local Adaptation Plans for Watersheds (LAPWs). As a result of the Project, EEA will be firmly integrated in these local plans and processes. ILM allows the combination of ecosystem services with land use planning to ensure that landscapes are managed in an integrated way relevant to socio-economic needs. ILM is already implemented at a smaller scale in the FAG/GEF-7 Project in Malawi. It involves long-term landscape management, which is more likely to be successful and sustainable than short-term interventions. As such, ILM is the ideal vehicle for plan and implement EEA. This contributes towards achieving the project’s overall objective of promoting climate-resilient landscapes that are both sustainable and resilience – hence delivering on GCF ARAT, ARAZA and MRA and MRE under the Project with respect to the GCF. The project will ensure that all stakeholders have a say in the planning process. The planning and implementation process will be highly inclusive, with meaningful participation of vulnerable groups (e.g. women, youth, people living with disabilities) and other key actors such as local government officials and community level units. Local and traditional knowledge in ecosystem management will be incorporated.

123. Interventions under EEA are comprised of actions to restore and strengthen ecosystems (see Appendix 2). These interventions are designed to ensure that the landscape is managed in a way that biodiversity and ecosystem services are maximized, and climate-change adaptation is most effective. EEA allows “holistic solutions” for landscapes, which can be tailored to local conditions and needs. The project will focus on ILM as it boosts ecological resilience by strengthening, restoring and sustainably managing ecosystems. EEA Resident Expertise (RE) will provide technical support in the development of LAPWs and other planning tools that cover a range of climate situations than other adaptation strategies. EEA combined with ILM generates benefits short- and long-term. In the short term, it provides immediate benefits such as improved water availability and resilience to climate change adaptability through grey infrastructure when economic, social and environmental benefits are included in the project design. These include reduced vulnerability to droughts and floods; increased food security; avoided loss of biodiversity; and enhanced ecosystem services. In the long-term, EEA can avoid maladaptation. Healthy watersheds ecosystems better absorb shocks resulting from floods and droughts, thus reducing the risk of climate change impacts on livelihoods. The project will also provide benefits such as improved agricultural productivity through EEA lessens flood damages, makes water available year-round, sustains food security in times of drought, and provides a more stable supply of water for livestock. In addition to food security, EAA is more accessible to the rural poor than other types of adaptation and can bring socio-economic benefits.

124. The project will boost the efficacy of food systems through an enhanced and sustainable access to markets, business opportunities, as well as finance. This shift will support farmers to stabilize and increase their income from agriculture. The project will also contribute towards achieving the objectives of the European Union Programme under the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (which will co-finance the EEA Project), and as an Enabling Environment for Private Sector Investment (EEPSI) in Malawi. In addition, the introduction of private sector finance will allow the adoption of EEA, and shift away from a pure grants-based approach. In addition, the project will contribute to achieving the objectives of the European Union Programme under the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs, which ultimately facilitate mobilization, blending, and disbursing of catalytic climate finance, and contribute to sustaining, replicating and expanding the scope of the Project (climate-resilient investments). Similarly, the introduction of PES at the

### D. Sustainable development (mass: 500 words, approximately | 1 page)

#### 127. One key benefit that the Project will generate is enhanced food sector resilience (SDG2 - Zero hunger). EBA practice within the region has shown that smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable to climate change (section B.1; climate resilience). For example, studies in Malawi reported that maize yields could increase by more than 40% under traditional management systems. However, this potential is not being realized because of a lack of access to knowledge and services. The Project will ensure that farmers have access to the knowledge, extension services and trainings on sustainable adaptation to climate change (Action A.1). Additionally, it could contribute to poverty alleviation (SDG-1 - No Poverty) through their potential for increased household incomes.

#### 128. Its approach via USAID’s EBA model will ensure that farmers and other consumers who will grow GM crops have access to best practices in the use of these technologies. The Project will also extend training on climate-resilient agricultural practices, including improved water management techniques, which will contribute to SDG-5 (Land on Land). As highlighted in section B.1; climate change will negatively affect the health of the population and increase food insecurity. In view of expected increased climate change adaptation, the Project will also contribute to SDG 3 (Good Health and Well Being).

#### 129. The Project will contribute to gender equality and women empowerment (SDGS), as the National Gender Policy and Action Plan (NGPAP) recognizes that women are particularly vulnerable to climate change. The Project will ensure that women-in-FIFS on climate-resilient agriculture, and mainstream gender in the training. The Project will train extension workers on gender-sensitive agricultural practices and provide them with tools for integrating gender into their work within the community. Project will incentivize women’s access to labor saving technologies, nutrition sensitive and early maturing crops, and other inputs that are more climate resilient than traditional varieties. It is important that conservation agriculture (CA) alone can reduce the labour demand for women farmers by about 34 days.

#### 130. Sustainable adaptation to climate change is unattainable if interventions exclude the vulnerable groups or do not fully cater to them. The vulnerable groups are characterized by scant access to knowledge, resources and

### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 131. Current climate change impact pathways put communities under increased pressure to respond to a vicious cycle of droughts and floods, which can lead to food insecurity, poverty, conflict, and migration. These impacts are likely to lead to interpersonal conflicts, food insecurity[28] and malnutrition as well as degraded health conditions. Without a significant increase in the resilience of smallholder farmers in agriculture sector, the vulnerability of communities will remain high.

#### 132. Shortages of vital ecosystem goods and services will trigger agricultural production shortages. Production of the main staple crops in Malawi may experience major annual decreases, as much as (i) 770,000 tons for maize (maize), (ii) 450,000 tons for cassava (manioc), (iii) 128,000 tons for sorghum and millet, and (iv) 369,000 tons for some legumes (pea/green pea). Hence creating food security and livelihood challenges for the targeted communities. The total value of these crops is estimated at US$54 million per year for maize; US$28 million per year for cassava[29]; US$13.2 million per year for millet[30]; 84.2 million USD/year for cassava; 86.5 million USD/year for green pea/green peas.

#### 133. The vulnerability to climate change at individual level can be said to originate from social exclusion, which enhances the risk of exposure and sensitivity to climate change impacts. This is because it increases their vulnerability to various shocks and stresses[30]. In recent years, economic inequality has significantly worsened[29], in particular among women and youth. The most vulnerable groups are those who are already exposed to multiple risks such as maternal deaths, sexually transmitted infections and gender-based violence.[21] As of 2020, discrimination and violence based on sexual orientation and gender identity (SOGI) has increased[31].

#### 134. The vulnerable groups – who are particularly susceptible to climate change by social exclusion – are women (52% of total population), the youth (30% for age 15-30 years and 50% for the poor). Eighty-three percent of the rural population in Malawi is female[32]. The Project will support implementation of the National Gender Policy, which aims to reduce gender-based violence. The Project will provide meaningful solutions tailored to the vulnerable populations (see more details in the Gender Action Plan). The Project will also promote women’s empowerment and participation through capacity building combined with increased stakeholders’ engagement, including women, youth and other vulnerable groups to plan and benefit from climate change adaptation interventions on ECA, as well as promoting alternative livelihoods on ECA for improved access to markets and finance.

#### 135. The proposed GCF grant, combined with identified cofinancing and parallel funding is the most appropriate to implement the planned project and investments. According to the World Bank[20], Malawi has a large stock of public debt (48% of GDP) and high levels of poverty (67%). The country also faces significant economic vulnerabilities including from response to the COVID-19 pandemic, debt service costs and unbudgeted arrears. Malawi is often at risk of natural disasters.

### D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 136. As described above in section B.1., the Project is aligned to the MDGs II (2007-2020), the NDC, the NAP, the NAP 2017-2023, the NULRS (2017) and the National Resilience Strategy 2018-2020. The NDC puts emphasis on ECA in its implementation of climate change adaptation interventions. The Project will support the adoption and dissemination of conservation technologies for smallholder farmers in the agriculture sector. These interventions are in line with Malawi’s NAP[34] (currently under preparation) to reduce vulnerabilities to the adverse impacts of climate change.

#### 137. In addition, the Project is aligned to the National Water Policy (2018), the NAP, and the NULRS. The Project will support implementation of the Decentralization Act (1996), which devolves powers and funding from the central government to district councils. This will enable them to manage their resources more effectively for sustainable development of natural resources, facilitate restoration of ecosystemic conditions and preservation of biodiversity, and promote community-based conservation initiatives. The Project will also support the implementation of the National Water Policy as a mechanism for coordination, allocation and delegation of responsibilities among stakeholders (CMCs) for the protection and integrated management of water resources. The Project will also support the new National Disaster Risk


---
**Page 60**
---

Management Plan (MP) (2019), which includes the issue of building climate resilience to disasters and shocks, and implementation of the National Resilience Strategy (2018-2030). The Project's gender-responsive activities will contribute to the goal of achieving gender equality in agriculture.

### 1.3. Broad stakeholders consultations processes were conducted during preparation to promote public ownership and ensure that the needs of the recipients were taken into account into the proposal (See Annex 7). The National Agricultural Policy Development Committee (NAPDC) was established as a steering committee for this collaboration with the FAO Country Office. Through national workshops, community engagement meetings at district level, and consultations with key stakeholders, the Project has been able to identify the needs of farmers and other large range of stakeholders, which includes: i) the Director of the NDA; GHG emissions using the low social carbon price. Using the high social price of carbon, the project EIRR increases to 54.6%. Without the value of avoided GHG emissions, the project has a much lower, albeit still positive, EIRR of 12.7%.

### 144.
The sensitivity analysis further shows that the project remains economically viable in different scenarios, including a 30% reduction in the social carbon price and a 50% increase in the discount rate of 2%. The cost per beneficiary (197 USD/person) is comparable to/consistent with recent GCF funding proposals.

### 145.
These ex-ante results are confirmed by other analysis in the country. For example, landscape restoration interventions conducted within the Malawi National Forest Landscape Restoration Program (NRLP) and involving improved crop management, tree planting and soil and water conservation practices (e.g., terracing and contour ploughing) to enhance watershed management through improved crop management, tree planting and soil and water conservation practices are estimated to have an EIRR of 27.5% (10). Similarly, catchment management interventions conducted within the SRIMP project in Malawi are estimated to have a high EIRR of 34%. Enhanced watershed management through improved crop management, tree planting and soil and water conservation practices is estimated to have an EIRR of 27.5% (10).

The cost-benefit analysis conducted within the Malawi NRLP shows that agriculture-based restoration interventions (e.g., agroforestry) generate positive NPVs (10). Similarly, catchment management interventions are estimated to generate positive NPVs as community plantations and private woodlots generate positive NPVs (10). Igracule et al. (2021) find that the IRR of agroforestry in Malawi is about 34% compared with a traditional maize monocropping system, which has an IRR of about 15%, as opposed to 15% of maize monocropping (which is the traditional way of growing maize in Malawi) (10). Moreover, the cost-benefit analysis conducted within the SRIMP project shows that agroforestry generates positive economic benefits in terms of NPV and BCR (10).

The economic and financial analysis (EFA) is presented in more detail in Annex 3.
