# Sustainability

## Long-term planning for climate change adaptation is rare at the community level due to lack of data and information, which is aggravated by weak long-term management systems. There is a lack of knowledge among the stakeholders on climate change impacts and risks. Small-scale farmers who are vulnerable to climate change have limited access to finance and markets, hampering the sustainable development of market-based and economically inclusive solutions.

### Ecosystems have been shown to reduce the impact of floods and droughts and limit water pollution exacerbated by agricultural runoff. They also enhance productivity through EBA (Enhanced Biological Accumulation) which makes water available year-round, enhances biodiversity, strengthens watershed functions, and improves soil quality. This project provides a blueprint for future public and private investments in ecosystem services that is suitable to the Malawian context.

Under the paradigm shift supported by the project, a sustainable natural resource management system will be established with local communities, ecosystems, livelihoods and agriculture as interdependent components of this equilibrium vulnerable smallholders will have access to information technology support, and finance they need for diversification into new income, and pursue the replication of best practices in their own landscape level, ensuring their sustainability. At the same time national policies, institutions, and private partners will mainstream ecosystem services in their work facilitating its diffusion throughout the country.

The EAM project holistically addresses critical drivers of sustainability:
- **Ecosystems**: By supporting CMC and VNRMS to firmly mainstream EBA into local development plans, this project will develop medium to long-term strategies for scaling up and mobilizing more climate finance towards climate change adaptation in degraded watershed ecosystems beyond the project’s investment horizon. This decentralized and participatory approach will also advance the policy and regulatory frameworks in the field.
- **Socio-economic**: Ownership and equitable access are key for behavioral change – are


---
**Page 65**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 81

E

embedded in the project, notably through its participatory and collective learning approaches. Environmental sustainability is ensured by the inclusive inclusion of smallholder farmers. For example, the development of food systems (i.e., markets/business opportunities for smallholders) as well as local financial services (e.g., private savings accounts developed by the MoF-FARMSE), will contribute to income-generating schemes and enhance the sustainability of ecosystem-based conservation interventions. In addition, support to The Green Climate Fund Carbon Trusts, as well as partnerships with CMA in the development of their own experience on carbon credits, will aim to secure a long-term financial viability of the landscape restoration investments.

* Environmental EIA approaches will be used for all projects guaranteeing their sustainable provision and benefits for the communities whose livelihoods depend on them.
* Technical: the soundness and comprehensiveness of the measures supported under the project are extensively discussed in the FS.

### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

146. In this role as Accredited Entity (AE), FAO will oversee project implementation according to the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between FAO and GCF. The AE will ensure that the project is implemented in a transparent manner and in accordance with the required standards.
The M&E unit will use the data from the MIS to prepare the draft performance reports, which will be sent to the PSC/PTC. As per the GCF Monitoring and Accreditation Framework (MAF), the AE will also submit an annual Project Implementation Report, and an independent Final evaluation report. FAO will also provide semi-annual and annual Financial Reports throughout project implementation.

147. Project monitoring will be conducted by the M&E unit composed by a National M&E specialist with GIS skills, and two M&E Associates (one in regional hubs). The unit will be complemented by the M&E unit of the PEF/ARMES Programme. FAO will ensure the existence of a well-designed, operational, and effective monitoring system that is aligned to the project’s objectives and outputs. Monitoring data will be collected through regular visits to the field sites by the Central Project Implementation Unit (CPU) and relevant Technical Units in FAO HQ. The project will be implemented based on the annual workplan and budget (WP/B), which will be developed for each year of implementation, taking into account the results achieved during the previous year as well as in relation to the achievement of higher-level project results and overall GCF goals. Monitoring data will be stored, compiled and displayed in a dedicated module within the MIS. The M&E unit will also ensure that the project is aligned with the GCF’s gender equality strategy, including its objectives, outcomes and targets in the results framework. It will also track implementation of the project’s Gender Action Plan and Environmental and Social Management Plan.

148. As per the PIP policy on evaluation, the FAO Office of Evaluation (OE) will conduct a final evaluation of the project, to be launched within six months prior to the actual completion date. I will aim at identifying project outcomes, their sustainability and actual or potential impacts. It will also have the purpose of indicating future actions that could be taken by the GCF, the Government of Nepal, and other stakeholders in relation to the project’s results, lessons learned from the donor, and share with them the evaluation report, which is a public document. An independent mid-term evaluation (MTE) will be carried out at project mid-

### Terms of expenditure and/or overall project duration, tentatively in the first quarter of project year 4. The GCF will arrange an independent MTE in consultation with the Project Steering Committee (PSC), Project Technical Committee (PTC), the Project Implementation Unit (PIU), the lead technical office (LTO) and the FAO-GCF Coordination Unit at FAO headquarters. The MTE will be conducted to review progress and effectiveness of implementation in terms of achieving project deliverables, including an assessment of the extent to which the project has contributed to the Fund's higher-order goals. The evaluation will also focus on project progress in the achievement of expected results against budget expenditures. It will highlight replicable good practices and key issues faced during project implementation. The MTE will be conducted by a team of independent experts with relevant experience in the field, and the methodology for the evaluation must be consistent with GCF requirements as outlined in the GCF Evaluation Policy, Evaluation Standards, and Evaluation Operational Procedures and Guidelines.

148. The overall evaluation cost for each evaluation is included within the project budget, consistent with global GCF standards. # F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max: 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Political (collaboration between Government stakeholders)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Limited collaboration between stakeholders from different ministries (e.g. Ministry of Agriculture - MoA, Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources - MERNR) in Malawi on landscape restoration activities. This low collaboration may negatively affect project implementation as it will result in a lack of shared understanding on climate-resilience practices and technologies, and ultimately impair the overall long-term project impact.

#### Mitigation Measures

All actors (MoA, MoCNR) will be engaged to participate in the Project Steering Group (PSG) and Project Technical Committee (PTC) to ensure that government organizations work hand-in-hand. At the local (district) level, all activities will be coordinated by the District Agriculture Officer (DAO). The DAO will also be responsible for coordinating efforts to ensure collaboration between all government stakeholders. These actions will lower the probability of risk to low.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Political (changes in the development priorities of the Government of Malawi)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium/Select |

**Description**

Low enforcement of development priorities, strategies and international commitments preventing from fully engaging in ecosystem-based adaptation - EBA. Typhoon-related climate change risks and institutional arrangements beyond the control of the project lead to a volatile environment that hampers the long-term success of the work.

#### Mitigation Measures

Project priorities are aligned with the development priorities of the GoM and with the most recent national strategies, policies and legislation. As mentioned in section B.1., the Government of Malawi has enabling favorable policy conditions for landscape restoration (e.g. National Forest Policy, National Land Use Policy, National Tree Planting and Restoration Strategy - NTLRS 2017) that promote ecosystem-based adaptation. The project's implementation arrangements are aligned with these policies and legislation. The Project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Government of Malawi. This includes a multi-sectoral high-level representative (at least at Permanent Secretary level) and we will debate together on how to address any potential risks or challenges that may arise during the project implementation phase, including its impact on the project, its activities and its implementation modalities. The FAO Representative in Malawi, which has been engaged as a key stakeholder in the project, will provide technical support and advice for the project team through dialogue with the Government counterparts to anticipate potential changes in policies and strategic directions and to propose the necessary measures. We aim to inform the GoM of any case of major changes in the government policies and strategies that may impact on the project implementation.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: other (exogenous risks)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Exogenous risks include: (i) climate risks; more frequent extreme events impacting negatively all landscape restoration efforts; (ii) COVID-19 and other sanitary crisis disrupt implementation. These risks would translate into


---
**Page 84**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 80

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The project seeks to restore and enhance the resilience of the degraded ecosystem that support sustainable and resilient livelihoods. EBA will support investments in ecosystem restoration, ecosystem-based adaptation that include diverse agroforestry systems, and climate-smart agriculture practices. The proposed mitigation measures under all scenarios consider the best climate-adapted to Malawi. Additional tree enhancement in farmland through agroforestry and other climate-resilient agricultural practices will be implemented to enhance livelihoods and food security resilience. During the course of the formation of EBA-based Village Level Action Plans (VLAPs) under option 1, 11, and 12, communities will be involved in identifying the most appropriate adaptation measures and risk reduction interventions, e.g., terraces or dams are required to reduce erosion / water flow. Activities 3.2.32 will support the development and delivery of innovative climate adaptive financial services (which may include agriculture insurance to mitigate risks).

Risks mitigation strategies against the COVID-19 pandemic will be included: engaging stakeholders' engagement plans, adopt higher flexibility and adaptive management and use remote communication whenever possible; (ii) maintaining social distancing by reviewing and adjusting implementation and stakeholder engagement arrangements to compensate staff shortages.

These actions will lower the input of risk to medium.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: other (Maladaptation Behaviours)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | High |

### Description

Communities resist to short-term and maladaptive practices and discourage adopting EBA.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Community buy-in will be maximized by the (i) participatory nature (including women and youth of the development of VLAPs, (ii) community-based learning and training activities, (iii) accompanying mentoring development actions), (iv) access to inputs (seeds, planting materials, equipment) and (v) access to finance. In addition, the project will provide a strong focus on capacity building, including awareness raising, technical assistance and financial support. Therefore, the project will place a strong focus on community learning and participatory planning (sub- option 12). The project will also ensure that women are fully involved in all aspects of EBA implementation and adopt EBA. It will ensure capacity development, knowledge generation and learning at local level through FFS and other training activities. The project will provide a strong focus on community learning and participatory planning by well qualified master trainers and facilitators, with the support of experts from public and private organizations. We will use a gender transformative approach, the Household Methodology that promote the participation of all community members in decision making processes.

Selected Risk Factor 5: other (Resistance to Women's Participation and Empowerment)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description

The project may encounter passive or active resistance to women's participation as well as when working to strengthen women's empowerment in the communities.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project targeting mechanisms including direct, community-based and self-targeting mechanisms will promote women's participation including vulnerable groups such as female-head of household, female youth and girls, and persons with disabilities. The project will use a gender transformative approach, the Household Methodology that promote the participation of all community and


---
**Page 85**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 81

## Household members including women, men, female and male youth as well as boys and girls. Religious, traditional and community leaders and other key figures will be trained in the Dimtra Clubs facilitation and household meetings. The training will cover gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) approaches. GESI-based approaches will allow to openly discuss gender-sensitive topics such as: (i) gender and sex; (ii) advantages and disadvantages of different gender roles; (iii) gender stereotypes and discrimination; (iv) sexual exploitation and harassment (SEAH), including masculinities and patriarchy; (v) origin/ontology and merits/demerits of social inclusion and exclusion; (vi) gender-based violence (GBV); (vii) gender, age, disability, ethnicity, religion, discrimination and social exclusion; and (viii) gender and social inclusion in community development and welfare. The training will also cover the following: (i) the importance of GESI in achieving the project’s objectives; (ii) the design process to monitor the progress made on GEWE and take necessary corrective actions as appropriate.

### Selected Risk Factor 6: Money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism (ML/TF)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Low |

## Description

Risks of money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

FAQ includes in the project agreement signed between FAO and the Government of Malawi clauses related to AML/CFT, as follows:

- a) The Government shall comply, and shall require all persons and entities engaged in its activities under the Project to comply with, all internal anti-money laundering, counter-terrorism financing laws, rules, and regulations.
- b) The Government confirms it has obtained sufficient undertakings from all persons and entities involved in its activities under the Project that they will not engage in any activity which is likely to facilitate or assist in money laundering or terrorist financing. The Government also confirms that it will comply with the substantive objectives of the GCF’s Policy on Prohibited Practices.

- (C) The Government shall ensure that all persons and entities engaged in its activities under the Project are aware of their obligations under Chapter VII of the UN Charter, Article 68 of the UN Charter, and the relevant provisions of the FATF Recommendations. The Government and FAO are firmly committed to the international fight against terrorism and, in particular, against the financing thereof. The Government shall ensure that all persons and entities engaged in its activities under the Project do not directly or indirectly: provide support to individuals or entities; i) associated with terrorism, as included in the list maintained by the UN Security Council pursuant to Chapter VII of the UN Charter; ii) listed on the FATF’s blacklist; or iii) that are the subject of sanctions or other enforcement measures promulgated by the United Nations Security Council. The Government shall ensure that all agreements that may be concluded with third parties for the implementation of activities under the Project.

During project implementation FAO, at AE, will ensure close monitoring and supervision through its offices in the regional office and HQ in order to ensure that the activities are implemented in full compliance with the signed project agreement.

G. OCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

100. During preparation of the environmental and social management framework was developed – see the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) in Annex A. An initial risk assessment was carried out against the ESMF criteria to identify potential risks and opportunities related to the project’s design and implementation, gender, social, and environmental aspects, and identified priority design elements to proactively address these. A detailed risk assessment will be conducted during the project preparation phase (PP) in close collaboration with local authorities, relevant technical institutions, to NGOs and CSOs, among others, as outlined in Annex 7A per the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). The ESMF is structured around four main themes: environmental and social risk categorization. The ESMF (Annex A) identifies the project as Category B (Moderate Risk), a project with significant potential for adverse effects on people, communities, or the environment, and/or significant direct and indirect impacts that individually or cumulatively, have few, generally site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed adverse impacts.

151. Overall, the project is expected to bring about major positive impacts, thanks to the socially inclusive participatory process, with a strong focus on women, youth and vulnerable groups, and the major environmental co-benefits resulting from the project’s approach of promoting agroforestry systems, which are more resilient to climate change than monoculture. Even with these expected positive effects, the project has been classified as Medium Risk (Category B) because: (i) the project will operate in areas where there is a lack of local knowledge associated with genetic resources that is held by local communities and/or farmers; (ii) crops may be introduced into areas where they are not currently grown, which could lead to competition for land or water use or management of forests; (iii) the project will operate in sectors or value chains that are dominated by subsistence production systems, which are more vulnerable than commercialized systems to climate change and other shocks such as “working poverty”; (iv) the project will operate in situations where youth work mostly as unpaid contributing family members and, accordingly, have limited access to land and resources; (v) women tend to work predominantly as unpaid contributing family members or subsistence farmers, have lower skills and opportunities for income generation than men, and are more vulnerable to climate change impacts such as droughts, floods, more precarious contracts and higher informal rates); these elements correspond to FAO Environmental and Social Risk Factors 1.2 and 3.2, respectively; (vi) the project will operate in areas where there is a lack of local knowledge associated with genetic resources that is held by local communities and/or farmers.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 83

## ESSS - Plant Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture

- **YES** (Standard 3 is triggered; project supported activities prioritized within VLVP may include: (i) the conservation, protection, and sustainable use of plant genetic resources (seeds/planting material) for cultivation; and/or (ii) establishment or management of germplasm banks)

To manage adverse risk associated with the handling and supply of seeds for local consumption, the project will:

- Ensure that the seeds and planting materials are available in sufficient quantities to meet the needs of local consumers, even if they have been forgotten and will need to be restocked;
- Ensure that the seeds and planting materials are stored according to norms; and
- Ensure, according to applicable national laws and/or regulations (e.g., Plant Variety Protection Regulations [PVPRs]), and over associated traditional knowledge are respected, along with the principles of EABM.

## ESSS 4 - Animal Livestock & Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture

As a mitigation measure, the Project prioritizes the use of native species in its activities, aligning with the principles of EABM agriculture. The team took further steps to ensure that the project is not listed as non-eligible under the ESMF non-eligibility list.

## ESSS - Pest and Pesticide Management

The project aims to promote integrated pest management (IPM) and climate resilience practices, including the use of biopesticides. The Project is committed to promoting sustainable and EABM agriculture practices.

The project has been explicitly excluded through the ESMF non-eligibility list. While integrated pest management (IPM) and climate-resilient practices are being implemented in a manner that minimizes environmental and social risks, the project will not be implementing any new or additional use of pesticides; the project aims to safeguard against unintended consequences without compromising its overall objectives.

## ESSS - Involuntary Resettlement and Displacement

The project is committed to ensuring that all stakeholders are aware and agree with the project's approach. The preparation of VLVP will guarantee that all local stakeholders are aware and agree with the project's approach, which does not entail physical or economic displacement; whether full or partial, permanent or temporary.

## ESSS 7 - Decent Work

- **YES** (Standard 3 is triggered if the project operates in sectors or value chains that are dominated by subsistence producers and other vulnerable informal agricultural workers. This includes situations where women work mostly as unpaid contributing family members, lack access to land, credit, inputs, markets, and technology; and/or situations where women tend to work predominantly as unpaid contributing family members or hired laborers with low pay, poor working conditions, no social security benefits, low wages, less representation and voice in producers' and workers' organizations, more vulnerability to exploitation and abuse.)

Specific measures and mechanisms will be introduced to empower the most vulnerable/disadvantaged categories of rural workers such as small-scale producers, women-headed households, landless agricultural workers, and other informal sector workers. Complementary measures have been included in the design of project

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 84

#### E588 – Gender Equality

**YES**

*Standard it is required by the GCF since the project may face either passive or active opposition to women's involvement and efforts to enhance women's empowerment.*

As per GCF requirements a gender assessment and action plan has been developed, which includes mitigation measures such as utilizing leveraging mechanisms like direct, indirect, and/or hybrid approaches.

Mitigation measures include utilizing leveraging mechanisms like direct, indirect, and/or hybrid approaches. Mitigation measures include targeting vulnerable groups such as female-headed households, female youth, girls, and persons living with disabilities. The project will also ensure that women are included in the decision-making process through gender-sensitive participatory processes. As per the Gender Methodology, the project will promote gender equality through open discussions on gender issues, including sexual harassment, gender-based violence (GBV), child marriage, and Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAR). Training for religious leaders, community members, and local government officials will be provided to increase their capacity to mobilize communities and promote gender equity. The project will prioritize the inclusion of women in all aspects of the project design and implementation process using the Gender Action Plan (GAP) to ensure alignment with project objectives.

#### E589 – Indigenous Peoples and Cultural Heritage

**YES**

The project focuses on integrated landscape management and ecosystem-based approaches, which directly identify indigenous communities' territories and traditional practices.

Mitigation measures for ESSS include obtaining Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC) from all affected indigenous peoples. The project will ensure that the ESMP plans for the development of the project-level Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and implementation monitoring plan (IMP) are fully aligned with the requirements of ILO Convention 169 on indigenous rights, identify and protect cultural assets, and involve communities in decision-making processes related to land use. The project will also ensure that it respects indigenous peoples' cultural heritage by providing training for all beneficiary communities in recognition of their rights akin to those of the IPs.

The ESMP establishes a framework that guides the screening and categorization, level of impact assessment, recommendations, and mitigation measures for each sub-component. All ESMP plans for sub-components 1.2 and sub-component 2.1 are fully screened against environmental and social safeguards. The ESMP will be developed in accordance with ILO Convention 169 and other relevant regulations adopted prior to the implementation of the proposed activities. ESMP plans will be implemented by EPIC facilitators.

The project's ESMP has entailed careful planning and includes a range of management controls to ensure that post-appraisal social and environmental due diligence takes place in a timely manner. It describes the required interventions, including those related to indigenous peoples' rights, and ensures compliance with the requirements of IAO's ESMS and Malawi regulations.

*Institutional arrangements: Overall compliance with the project’s ESMP will be ensured by the Environmental and Social Management Unit (ESMU) and national and regional specialists within the CPU. They work closely together with the Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist (who will oversee the GAP) and the National Project Coordinator.*

**Page 89**

108. In addition, there will be zero tolerance of sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment (SEAH), and the project will ensure that subsequent ESAM as well as implementation safeguards documents will mainstream SEAH mitigation, in accordance with the FAO ESAM. The project will support gender sensitization and trainings for project staff and partners on the prevention and response to SEAH, including through the use of a dedicated online training module during the implementation of the project. Specific procedures to minimize SEAH risk will be developed for the project GMA, to ensure that all project staff are aware of their responsibilities in preventing and responding to SEAH (including confidential reporting), and to facilitate linkages to related services and referral for anyone affected by SEAH.

157. Grievance and Redress Mechanism (GRM). The grievance redress mechanism (GRM) is an integral project component that will ensure the effective implementation of the project, including its gender equality objectives and performance. The mechanism is based on FAO requirements and most importantly, it is based on existing, community-driven mechanisms in the project area. The GRM will be implemented through a two-tiered approach: at the project level, the GRM works through culturally appropriate local-level project GMA; at the regional level, the GRM will work with the national FAO office and other stakeholders to ensure that the project is aligned with national policies and strategies. The GRM has a strong link to FAO Mission’s complaints officer to ensure the right application of GRM in line with FAO’s Complaints Mechanism. The GRM will be strengthened by ensuring that all project staff are aware of their responsibilities under the GRM, and will also be strengthened to include a procedure for handling SEAH that is inclusive, survivor-centered and gender-sensitive.

158. Gender Analysis: An analysis of the gender-related aspects of the project was also undertaken during the development of the ESAM and Gender Action Plan (GAP) (Annex 6). Although the Government of Malawi ratified most of the international conventions on women’s rights in 2007, there are still many challenges to achieving gender equality in these areas. These inequalities are due to entrenched social norms including the co-existence of patriarchal and matriarchal systems, which have been reinforced by religious beliefs and practices. Women continue to be marginalized in decision-making processes. They must also bear the consequences of HIV/AIDS high prevalence rates as well as other diseases such as malaria and tuberculosis. The project will address these challenges through its gender strategy, which includes: (i) increasing women’s access to productive resources; (ii) improving women’s economic opportunities, men dominate activities and sectors of high productivity and profit margin, and even when women are involved in the production process they do not have control over the proceeds from their labor; inferior access to improved agricultural inputs and technology, market difficulties to access markets and finance the business; (iii) increasing women’s access to education and training, including technical and vocational knowledge and work capacity to conduct catchment and landscape management into integrated planning process.

159. The ESAM gender strategy and action plan aims at reducing inequalities and removing the above-identified barriers that prevent women from participating in decision-making processes. It will also aim at empowering young men and women, youth-in-the-development process: in order to promote gender equality and youth transformation in the longer term. The project will focus on four key areas of intervention: (i) increasing access to education and training for girls’ youth empowerment; (ii) promoting equitable and inclusive sharing of decision-making power at household and community levels through women’s participation in decision making, including their participation in domestic and public life; and (iv) economically empowering the vulnerable particular women and youth.

160. For these purposes, EMAL will rely on the use of the Dimbula Clubs and Household methodology (also referred to as the “DCHM”) which is a participatory approach that aims at increasing women’s empowerment through engagement, social inclusion, collective action, gender equality and equitable sharing of decision-making power both at household and community levels. The project will also support the development of a youth club in each village for mobilization and engagement in the planning and implementation of ILM. Women and youth’s capacity will be strengthened to ensure that they are able to participate effectively in the planning, implementation and monitoring that will allow women, men and youth household members understand and change gender norms, while at the same time increasing their access to resources and services. The project will also support the development of Microfinance Institutions (MFIs) which will put a particular focus on GVA and SEAH. Under component 2, this project will strengthen women, men and youth’s capacity for sustainable livelihoods through the establishment of Village Savings and Credit Associations (VSCAs) (MSMEs) as will be supported to strengthen their marketing skills and access markets. EMAL will also support women and youth in accessing microfinance services from MFIs and other financial institutions, including those provided by FFIs. Under component 3, NCCE and national conservation trust funds will be supported in developing their gender strategy and action plan; the project will support them to consider gender principles in their interventions and to promote

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 161. Coordination of the implementation of the Gender Action Plan will be the responsibility of the PPU Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist. The Specialist must ensure that project planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation are gender-sensitive and consider the needs of women and girls. The logical framework should include measurable indicators related to women and girls that allow assessing the sensitivity of projects' components to gender equality and the extent to which they contribute to reducing inequalities between men and women disaggregated by age.

### G.3.1 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 162. Financial management and control and procurement processes will be implemented as per FAD rules and regulations, which were certified as acceptable to the GCF in the accreditation process. FAD has an Administrative Manual Section 400 that provides guidance on financial management and control. The FAD Intranet provides access to this Manual and to other procedures, information and guidance via the "TACO" Handbook.

#### 163. FAD has deployed an Oracle-based ERP system called the Global Resources Management System® (GRMS) in its operations. GRMS is a comprehensive financial management software that supports all aspects of accounting and finance functionalities. Using GRMS improves the flow of financial information, supports financial monitoring and reporting requirements, and provides a single source for all financial data. GRMS also includes a project accounting module which is used by the whole organization and that allows for a separation of income and expenditure by donor and project and facilitates internal management and external reporting requirements.

#### 164. FAD will ensure that procurement processes are in accordance with the GCF's policies, procedures and guidelines to facilitate internal management and external reporting requirements. Procurement and Letters of Agreement Services are based on "Best Value for Money" principles as embodied in the Manual Section 502 and the Manual Section 503.

#### 165. As mentioned in Section C.4, FAO as the Accredited Entity of the GCF will have overall responsibility for quality assurance and oversight of co-executing entities, and shall assume fiduciary responsibility in accordance with FAO standards and procedures. The accredited entity is responsible for ensuring that all projects are implemented according to FAD rules and regulations mainly contained and detailed in the FAD Handbook (including those referred to financial management and control).

#### 166. During implementation, FAO will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures. This may include monitoring missions, spot checks and participation at project governance meetings. The procurement process is subject to the GCF's rules and regulations.

#### 167. FAD Country Representative in Malawi (FAOR) is responsible for financial monitoring and is supported by a management team comprised of a Program Assistant, Management Assistant, and a Strategic Operations Officer. Such team will interact and support the work of the PPU.

### G.4 Disclosure of project

#### No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed fully to the GCF; this information is being provided in confidence.
#### With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF; as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is responsible for ensuring that all parties involved are aware of and comply with this confidentiality agreement.

4. **The tobacco industry in other countries (e.g., Kenya) has recommended that tobacco farmers make it as many as 48 applications for subsidies under the National Tobacco Subsidy Scheme, which was launched by the government to support tobacco farmers and reduce poverty.**
5. **Tobacco is usually planted in a single or monocrop, contributing to soil erosion, and water erosion, which leads to reduced soil fertility and increased vulnerability of smallholder farmers to climate change.**

## References

1. FAO, 2016: "National Agriculture Policy," Uganda: Ministry of Agriculture, Irrigation and Water Development.

The total cost is valued at $257,483 hectares. However, it has been decided to round the figure to 267,500 hectares. And these values use a discount rate of 10%.

The project will be implemented through the following components:

* The PIGCA module involves agriculture extension staff working with groups of farmers ahead of the agricultural season to fully analyse National Agricultural Extension Service (NAES) data on climate change impacts. This will help them understand how their crops are affected by current circumstances. Then soon before and during the extension season staff and farmers consider the practical implications of seasonal weather forecasts, including expected rainfall patterns and temperatures.

Dear Madam, Sir,

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) regarding Ecosystems-based Adaptation for resilient Watersheds and Communities in Malawi (EBAAM)

We refer to the project titled "Ecosystems-based Adaptation for resilient Watersheds and Communities in Malawi" (Project No. GCF/18/MW/02), the content of which we acknowledge have received.

a) The Government of Malawi has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal by Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO).

b) The project is included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and policies of Malawi

c) In accordance with the relevant environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for accertaining no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been followed. We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.


Kind regards,

Mrs. Tavengi Mhale-Luka

# Secretariat's assessment of FP238

## Proposal name: Ecosystems-based Adaptation for resilient Watersheds and Communities in Malawi (EbAM)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country: Republic of Malawi

Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- The EbAM project targets the most climate-vulnerable watershed areas in Malawi, a Least Developed Country, by employing an ecosystem-based approach that combines integrated landscape management and restoration. This addresses immediate ecological and agricultural needs but also sets a foundation for long-term sustainability at scale.

#### Points of caution
- The upcoming 2025 general election in Malawi presents a risk to project stability and continuity due to potential political shifts and changes in the governance and regulatory environments.
- It is anticipated to have a wide-reaching impact through planned investments in 30 sub-catchments for restoring, conserving, restoring, and sustainably managing approximately 267,500 hectares under EbA (Ecosystem-based Adaptation) and SCMPs planning including $1.4 million under EbA management.
- It includes technical and financial innovations, including supporting the country's long-term climate change adaptation strategies. The project aligns closely with Malawi’s national priorities as outlined in its updated national strategy for sustainable development 2035 (NSD).
- This includes promoting ecosystem-based adaptation practices such as integrated land use management, and sustainable agricultural practices ensuring the project supports national policies and enhances policy coherence.

The project's success is dependent on collaboration by the project team with government counterparts and other stakeholders to secure the necessary support and financing; managing these relationships effectively will be key during the implementation phase.


---
**Page 109**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background

1. The objective of “Ecosystem-based Adaptation for resilient Wetlands and Communities in Malawi” (the EBAM project) is to enhance the climate change resilience of wetland ecosystems in Malawi through a comprehensive approach that integrates EBA with ILM.
2. Targeting 11 vulnerable districts chosen due to their high climatic vulnerability, the project aims at reducing the negative impacts of climate change, including increased rainfall variability, extreme rainfall, and the frequency of climate-induced disasters such as droughts and floods.
3. The EBAM project is strategically designed to enhance ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) for wetland ecosystems in Malawi and improve livelihoods, with a significant emphasis on involving vulnerable subgroups such as women and youth.
4. The 11 districts were selected due to their significant exposure to climate change impacts, including extreme soil loss and land degradation, increased rainfall variability, increased temperatures, and frequent climate-induced disasters like droughts and floods.

6. The EBAM project is structured around three interconnected components:
   - The first component focuses on enhancing the ecological resilience of wetland ecosystems by restoring ecosystem functions at a large enough scale to facilitate adaptation.
   - The second component aims to enhance livelihoods through an Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA) approach, which uses biodiversity and ecosystem services to help communities adapt to climate change. This will be achieved by promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices, improving access to markets, and facilitating financial inclusion for smallholder farmers in the project area.
   - The third component seeks to create a conducive environment for climate resilience by enhancing policy frameworks and mobilizing innovative finance mechanisms.

7. Overall, the EBAM project embodies a paradigm shift towards sustainable ecosystem management and climate-resilient agricultural practices in Malawi. By integrating EBA within local planning processes through Village Level Action Plans (VLAPs), institutional strengthening, community engagement (i.e., under the guidance of National Water Resources Management Committees [NWRMCs]), policy advocacy, financial sustainability, knowledge-sharing (including digital services), and robust monitoring.

The project’s success is crucial for sustaining the project’s impacts beyond its lifetime. The project will be implemented in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment Protection (MoEP) and Village Level Action Plans (VLAPs), institutional strengthening, community engagement (i.e., under the guidance of National Water Resources Management Committees [NWRMCs]), policy advocacy, financial sustainability, knowledge-sharing (including digital services), and robust monitoring.


---
**Page 110**
---

1. This strategy ensures that project initiatives are deeply integrated into local and national frameworks, empowering communities and institutions to continue practices and support essential services and extension support that bolster climate resilience. Through innovative financing mechanisms such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF), Malawi has mobilized additional resources for expanding these practices to other regions, embedding ecosystem-based adaptation into the fabric of Malawi’s development and climate-resilience efforts.
2. The project is funded by GCF through grant agreement 102272, with a total budget of USD 234 million granted from GCF. Co-financing totaling USD 10.6 billion will be provided by the country’s government, the World Bank, the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), and the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO).
3. The budget allocation across the project components is detailed as follows:
   - USD 284.0 million is dedicated to component 1, aimed at integrated landscape management practices that enhance ecosystem services and reduce greenhouse gas emissions.
   - USD 579.6 million is allocated to component 2, focusing on stabilizing productivity and farmers’ incomes through more resilient livelihoods and food systems.
   - USD 118.0 million is designated for component 3, which seeks to enhance the enabling environment for climate resilience and scaling up climate-resilient watershed ecosystems and agricultural practices.
4. Additional allocations include USD 2.32 million for monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities and USD 59.6 million for project management.

10. The project’s risk assessment identifies political factors such as collaboration challenges between government stakeholders and changes in the government’s development priorities as significant risks to achieving project objectives. Other identified risks include limited cooperation between partners in different ministries and departments, which could hinder the achievement of project goals.
5. To mitigate these, the project involves key governmental actors in committees to ensure coherent policy alignment with the project’s climate-resilience and ecosystem-based adaptation focus, which may affect commitment to ecosystem-based adaptation strategies. This is mitigated by the strong alignment with Malawi’s current complementary policies. High-level government officials have been engaged in discussions on the project’s objectives and implementation.
6. Additional exogenous risks like extreme weather events and health crises like the coronavirus disease (COVID-19) are also identified as potential challenges to achieving project goals that support resilient livelihoods and adapt project management practices to ensure continuity despite these challenges.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Community resilience (total cost: USD 234 million, GCF grant: USD 284.0 million)

15. Component 1 of the project empowers ILM in order to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and enhance ecosystem services through integrated landscape management focusing on ecosystem services. It aligns with the Fund’s outcomes related to health, food security, ecosystems, and forestry.
16. ILM employs a holistic, participatory approach that is integrated into local planning, encompassing community-based natural resource management (CBNRM) as well as CBNRM as the principal tool for building climate resilience, incorporating local planning and stakeholder engagement at all levels of implementation. The project involves trained and empowered management committees and local communities, emphasizing the inclusion of women, youth, and vulnerable groups. Through active and inclusive stakeholder engagement at various levels,

The project ensures broad participation in both planning and execution phases. The project’s approach is informed by lessons from past projects to enhance sustainability. A rigorous targeting and selection process is employed for intervention areas, focusing on local stakeholder participation and engagement with regions identified as vulnerable due to their climate and ecological characteristics.

13. Overall, the component demonstrates a comprehensive approach to address climate impacts in food systems through integrated interventions that foster stakeholder engagement and capacity-building to achieve sustainable outcomes.

**Component 2: Ecosystem resilience – strengthened climate resilience of agroecosystems (total cost: USD $8.0 million)**

14. Component 2 focuses on enhancing resilience in livelihoods and food systems, aiming to address productivity and income of farmers through resilient agricultural practices. It addresses the barriers that hinder progress towards achieving these goals, including knowledge gaps, social challenges, and financial limitations. By tackling these barriers, the component aims to contribute to climate-resilient livelihoods, improved food security, and food security using a range of solutions to building resilience in agricultural systems.

15. Moreover, component 2 empowers sustainability by promoting practices such as EBA (see Box 3) that can be scaled up across agroecosystems. By strengthening community systems, the component aims to enhance ecosystem resilience and ensure the long-term stability of livelihoods and food systems through sustainable agricultural practices.

16. This component also focuses on facilitating market access and entrepreneurship development for farmers by providing them with the necessary tools and resources to improve their products and increase their income. This approach not only benefits individual farmers but also contributes to the overall resilience of local food systems and the development of micro, small, and medium-sized enterprises.

17. Furthermore, component 2 promotes financial inclusion by strengthening community-based financial services (CBFS) in agroecosystems. Farmers are enabled to invest in climate-resilient agricultural practices and improve their overall economic stability. The replicability of the approach is a significant strength. By designing interventions that can be scaled up across agroecosystems, this component has the potential for widespread impact beyond its immediate target areas especially with the investment pipeline for National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) in mind. This will enhance the long-term effectiveness of the component’s initiatives.

18. In summary, component 2 adopts a multifaceted approach to building resilience in livelihoods and food systems through productivity, sustainability, market access, and financial inclusion to empower farmers and enhance agricultural resilience.

**Component 3: Climate governance – strengthened climate governance and institutional frameworks for sustainable land management (total cost: USD $8.0 million)**

19. The project supports a range of innovative and sustainable activities designed to foster climate-resilient livelihoods in agroecosystems. By promoting the adoption of national policies, the project enhances the longevity and effectiveness of EBA principles across sectoral planning and financing.

20. Furthermore, the project engages in policy influence and advocacy through policy dialogues and the preparation of evidence-based studies and policy assessments, aiming to foster policy changes that promote ecosystem restoration and integrated landscape management. Additionally, the project builds partnerships and leverages synergies to explore

### Impact potential

The project is considered to have significant impact potential, aimed at transforming the vulnerability landscape of Malawi’s rural communities through comprehensive environmental and rural livelihoods interventions. The project will enhance agricultural yields, improve soil quality, and increase water retention, thereby improving food security.

Primarily an adaptation project, it aims to empower project beneficiaries to effectively prepare for, respond to, and recover from climate-related extreme disasters—focusing on mitigating the cycle of poverty and environmental degradation exacerbated by climate change.

With a strategic focus on ecosystem-based adaptation and integrated landscape management, the project is poised to directly benefit 270,830 people, targeting approximately 30 sub-catchments (for a total of about 257,000 hectares) over six years, and generate mitigation benefits of ~49.3 MTCO2e tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent.

# 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: High

The project aims to catalyse a significant shift towards sustainability in ecosystems, livelihoods, and landscapes of Malawi, which currently face high vulnerability to climate change impacts. It seeks to transition from the current state of vulnerability to one where ecosystems are conserved, restored, and managed sustainably, providing adaptation to climate change, while also improving food security.

As noted above in the project's description, to achieve this, the project will promote a locally led approach called landscape-based adaptation (LBA) with integrated land management approaches (ILMA). LBA focuses on holistic and multifunctional landscapes to maximize biodiversity and ecosystem services, making them more resilient to climate change. ILMA involves the integration of conservation, restoration, and sustainable livelihood objectives. These approaches will be integrated into watershed and village-level plans, engaging marginalized communities.

The project will implement various EBA interventions such as ecosystem restoration and sustainable agricultural practices, aiming to enhance ecological resilience and improve food security in Malawi's rural areas. This will help smallholder farmers and pastoralists, making their livelihoods more resilient. Additionally, the project aims to strengthen the enabling environment for landscape-based adaptation by supporting policy development projects and programmes. It will promote collective learning on EBA through training and participatory planning processes.

Overall, the project seeks to create a sustainable and resilient future for Malawi by integrating EBA into local plans, policies, and investment strategies, while ensuring meaningful participation of stakeholders and knowledge-sharing at various levels.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential

### Scale: High

The project is strategically positioned to contribute significantly to sustainable development in Malawi, aligning with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) [Zero hunger], (4) [Green water and sanitation], (13) [Climate action], and (15) [Life on land].

By promoting ecosystem-based adaptation and integrated landscape management, EBA offers a comprehensive approach to addressing climate change impacts. This approach supports the creation of resilient landscapes that can withstand the effects of climate change while enhancing the strengthening of adaptive capacities to climate-related hazards. Through its commitment to restoring degraded landscapes, improving agricultural productivity, and promoting sustainable land management practices, the project contributes significantly to achieving food security, income stability, and enhanced adaptability capacity to climate change, contributing significantly to the Sustainable Development Goals in Malawi.

## 3.4 Needs of the recipient

The project targets the acute needs of Malawi's rural population and agrarian economy, challenging them with poverty, food insecurity, and water scarcity. Focusing on vulnerable communities, the project aims to improve resilience through sustainable watershed management practices that address soil erosion, erratic rainfall, and increased frequency of floods and droughts, which impact agricultural productivity.

By empowering communities with sustainable resource management skills, enhancing food and water security, and building institutional


---
**Page 114**
---

### 3.5 Country ownership

The project exemplifies strong country ownership, aligning closely with Malawi’s national development priorities for climate change, environmental conservation, and sustainable development. This initiative is deeply integrated into the country's broader efforts to combat climate change and enhance resilience through its alignment with Malawi’s National Development Plan (NDP) and Growth and Development Strategy. By working in concert with local ministries, government agencies, and community organizations, the project ensures that its objectives are in harmony with national priorities.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The project expects enhanced productivity on farms, value addition post-production, market access improvements, significant greenhouse gas emission reductions, and the bolstering of climate resilience. Financial analysis reveals positive net present values and internal rates of return for beneficiaries, highlighting the cost-effectiveness of the project. The project is expected to generate a total economic benefit of US$685 million with an investment of US$230 million, resulting in a net present value of US$455 million and an economic internal rate of return of 12.8 percent.

The project is expected to generate substantial positive environmental and social benefits for communities involved in agricultural interventions, particularly those vulnerable to climate change impacts.

42. Safeguards instrument. The AE has prepared an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF; see Annex 6). The ESMF is the appropriate instrument for this project to address environmental and social risks and impacts. The ESMF will be updated as identified following the development of village-level action plans (VLAPs) and sub-catchment management plans (SCMPs).

43. The project will also support the development of MSMEs, provision of financial services for ESA investments by the financial institutions and building an ESA investment pipeline of Malawi’s private sector.

44. An Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) will be developed to identify and address potential specific risks and impacts at the local level and be incorporated as part of the VLAPs to ensure compliance with the GCF ESMF.

45. Compliance with the GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) standards. The project complies with the GCF ESS standards in all aspects, including:

* ISS1 Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts. The ESMF provides information about the baseline conditions of the physical context, general environmental conditions, social and cultural conditions, livelihood systems, and the indigenous groups and communities in the target project areas. The ESMF also provides a description of the potential risks and impacts associated with each component of the project interventions per the AE’s ESS standards and E&S risk screening checklist. The AE sets out in the ESMF the following: (1) the principles and procedures for assessing the E&S baseline conditions, including the identification of environmental and social risks and their associated mitigation measures. The ESMF requires that each VLAP be formulated with the standard format as set out by the AE.

47. Given that potential conflicts may arise from activities that ensure livelihood improvement and benefit-sharing among the local groups, the AE further elaborates on the AE’s approach to conflict management in the project area. The AE sets out the context in the host country and sets out the process to handle conflict risks during the project implementation phase. The AE also provides a description of the conflict resolution/mediatory approach as part of the integrated landscape management for land plan and land restoration activities, which will be implemented with the participation of local communities in the targeted mini-catchments.

48. ISS2 (Labour and Working Conditions). The AE has identified moderate labor risk on decent work conditions. The project’s activities may operate in sectors or value chains dominated by subsistence producers and other vulnerable informal agricultural workers generally lacking access to education, training, and social protection services. Children and youths work predominantly informally as unpaid contributing family members and lack access to education and health services.

49. The AE will take specific measures, and as part of the project design, to empower the most vulnerable and disadvantaged categories of rural workers such as small-scale producers, contributors to informal value chains, and other vulnerable groups. In accordance with the AE’s guidance on child labour, the project will not engage children under the age of 18 in work related activities that are hazardous or harmful to their health, safety or morals. Where children under the age of 18 may be engaged in work-related activities in connection with the project, an appropriate risk

## ES3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Control)

The project aims to reduce the dependence on the use of pesticides and agrochemicals through promoting EBA and agroecological practices. The project will ensure that all farmers are aware of the project’s non-eligibility list, and the use of pesticides has been explicitly excluded through the ES3 non-eligibility list. While integrating EBA into farming systems is a key component of the project, it should be done in an environmentally sustainable manner. It will be implemented in a manner that minimizes environmental and social risks. By prioritizing agroecological principles and excluding harmful inputs such as pesticides, the project aims to significantly reduce pollution and improve resource efficiency while meeting or exceeding the related standard.

## ES4 (Community Health, Safety and Security)

The project will use a community-centric approach in the establishment and operation of a watershed management structure at the micro-catchment level. The project will form Watershed and Stream Management Units (WSMUs) to develop catchments. Each WSMU will be funded by the project. An estimated total area of 83,240 hectares, including both communal and in-farm areas, will be targeted for EBA interventions under this ES. The specific risks and impacts on community health, safety and security will be identified as part of the sub-project-specific ESMS during implementation.

## ES5 (Land Access and Sustainable Land Management)

The project does not entail physical or economic displacement, whether full or partial, permanent or temporary, as a result of land use change. The project will ensure that all farmers are aware of the project’s non-eligibility list and involuntary resettlement under the project's non-eligibility list. Furthermore, restrictions on resource access, such as farming land, which cannot be adequately mitigated and would negatively impact the livelihoods of local communities or lead to displacement of indigenous and marginalized populations are strictly prohibited. The AE will guarantee the full transparency of land restoration activities in all phases of the project implementation process.

## ES6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources)

The Alt has triggered its relevant standards as the project activities prioritized under VLP-2019 include: (i) promoting the use of indigenous crop species, varieties that are not previously grown, providing seeds/planting material for cultivation, and the establishment of seed banks; (ii) ensuring that all farmers are aware of the project’s non-eligibility list. The project may also involve promoting integrated crop and livestock systems. In addition to the above, the Alt has identified specific measures to manage the adverse risks associated with the handling and supply of seeds for cultivation, including: (i) avoid undermining local seed and planting material production; (ii) ensure that all farmers are aware of the project’s non-eligibility list; (iii) ensure that seeds and planting materials are free from pests and diseases according to norms; and (iv) ensure that the use of genetically modified organisms is avoided. The Alt will also ensure that associated traditional knowledge is respected, along with the sharing of the benefits accrued

from their... Furthermore, the project will prohibit the introduction of non-native and invasive species as included in the ESME non-eligibility list.

The IPPF outlines relevant international standards, including the UNDRIP, and implementation arrangements, monitoring and reporting and grievance mechanism arrangements. It also largely governs the project’s approach to engagement with indigenous peoples and other potential target communities. The safeguarding documents developed during project implementation, based on the screening. The collaborator will ensure that all ESME implementation specialists have a good understanding of associated activities, rather than blinding all sites with the same mitigation measures. [i] Ensure effective communication and disclosure of project information to local stakeholders in their native language through a variety of means; [ii] Provide project updates and information through various media including announcements in community forums, market days, picture-board displays, etc.;

### Gender policy

The AI provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the updated GCF Gender Policy. The gender action plan has been integrated in the project's overall work plan to ensure that the gender mainstreaming approach is fully embedded into all aspects of the project.

The gender action plan includes specific actions for addressing gender equality and women’s empowerment as an add-on. The gender assessment and its accompanying gender action plan address the gendered dimensions of food insecurity, nutrition, and water, sanitation, and hygiene (WASH) in all sectors of the project – agriculture, food security/nutrition, Water, Sanitation and Hygiene for All and policy and governance. The gender action plan includes actions to address the root causes of gender inequality and women’s empowerment, including the lack of access to land and credit, limited resistance to addressing sensitive topics such as hegemonic masculinity and patriarchy is recognized as a separate risk factor, and there is a clearer needs analysis which takes intersectionality into account. The gender action plan has been revised to include indicators to measure qualitative changes related to gender equality and women’s role in society.

However, factors such as a largely cash-based economy and the country’s porous borders with neighbouring countries contribute to heightened money laundering risks.

#### 4.3.2 GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

- In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of the overall single proposal is not material.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP238

Proposal title: Ecosystems-based Adaptation for Resilient Watersheds and Communities in Malawi (EBA4M)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country: Republic of Malawi

Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

This funding proposal1 for a medium-sized cross-cutting project in environmental and social safeguards Category B is submitted by the Government of Malawi through the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) as part of the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Trust Fund.

The two executing entities (EEs) are FAO and the Government of Malawi acting through three state entities: the Ministry of Water Resources, the Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs, and the National Local Government Finance Committee.

1. The total cost of the proposed project is USD 53.22 million, of which USD 42.81 million is proposed to be financed by GEF (see section 1.6 below).

The project objective is to increase the climate change resilience of the most vulnerable rural communities at the district level in Malawi, in 11 districts (out of 32 districts in the country) that have a combination of high exposure and/or sensitivity to climate change with low capacity to adapt.

In these districts, increases in temperature, rainfall variability and intense precipitation events are leading to increased frequency and intensity of disasters such as floods, droughts and landslides. These natural hazards are causing significant damage to infrastructure, reducing livelihood opportunities for rural communities, and reducing agricultural productivity and causing human suffering. The project aims to be a catalyst for change by promoting the adoption of ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approaches at the landscape level in these districts.

The baseline study shows that the 11 districts have high vulnerability to climate change and are considered as high vulnerability to an alternative paradigm in which watershed ecosystems are conserved, restored and managed sustainably. The project will support communities to adapt to climate change and continue supplying services to rural communities at scale.

To achieve this, the project will promote an ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approach2 that includes: forest restoration; agroforestry; soil and water conservation; land management – to repair degraded ecosystems, and promote low-carbon resilient agricultural practices. This involves working through existing local governance structures to develop and implement landscape-level EbA strategies and action plans in the 11 districts. The legislation of Malawi, applying an ecosystem-based approach. The sub-catchment and village plans will be developed with communities and stakeholders to ensure that they are based on sound soil and water conservation for erosion and run-off control (with contours bands, swales, brush fall, gully plugs, etc.), agroforestry, forest restoration, land management – including community forest and woodlot management; pastureland management; riverbank protection;

1 Funding proposal package for “Ecosystems-based Adaptation for Resilient Watersheds and Communities in Malawi”, received from FAO on 3 May 2024.

- **Cost**: $0.641 million
- **Co-finance**: $0.531 million

## Impact Potential Scale: Medium to High

1. The project is cross-cutting, with 90 per cent of GCF resources considered as contributing directly to climate change and 5 per cent towards climate change mitigation.
2. The three targeted adaptation results areas are:
   - Enhancing the resilience of (i) most vulnerable people and communities; (ii) their well-being; and food and water security; and (iii) ecosystems and ecosystem services.

### Adaptation Impact

1. The funding proposal highlights the vulnerability of Malawi to climate change, including increased temperatures, droughts, floods, wind damage and landslides caused by the increasing frequency and intensity of tropical storms.
2. These impacts are expected to be more severe in the dry season, translating into limited water availability at key growing times, and increased water stress, with negative impacts on both cash and subsistence crops, and the overall rural economy.

### Project Benefits

1. The project is expected to directly benefit around 270,820 members of smallholder farm households in the prioritized districts.
2. The project will be implemented through a micro-catchment approach (methodology detailed in feasibility study, annex 2 to the funding proposal). These will be residents of the 111 micro-catchments to be selected according to hydrological, ecological and socioeconomic criteria, with priority given to those located within or adjacent to the prioritized districts.
3. An average micro-catchment will contain 10 villages in a cluster (see Figure 8.2). From this list of 111 micro-catchments, 54 are already identified as areas that can benefit from interventions at the landscape scale.

### Annexes

1. Annex 2 to the funding proposal provides details on how the projected number of beneficiaries was determined and calculated, and extensive water calculation was activated.
2. The assumptions made in these calculations include the vulnerability of these communities to drought and flood damage will be reduced through collective investments in the landscape, and


---
**Page 124**
---

on farm investments to productivity and diversification, with support on value addition and access to markets, will reduce their vulnerability to unpredictable growing conditions.

12. More than 300,000 people in villages that are not part of the targeted micro-catchments, but are included in the selected sub-catchments, are considered to be indirect beneficiaries of the project. These communities are expected to benefit from improved water resources and achieve a measurable adaptation benefit thanks to well-functioning Sub-Catchment Management Committees (SCMCs) that will be established as part of the project’s “Community First” approach. Based on this, the total number of project beneficiaries is estimated at nearly 575,000 people, of which over 300,000 are indirect beneficiaries.

14. The project's adaptation impact has the potential to be significant, but the extent to which this can be quantified, measured and reported remains unclear, since the detailed monitoring and evaluation (M&E) framework will be developed only during project implementation. Furthermore, the M&E indicators and methods have not been defined in the logical framework (funding proposal section E.6) are incomplete. In relation to resilient livelihoods and food security, there is no clear methodology for assessing changes in household income, stability of crop yields, or volume of crop sales, nor any clear methodology for assessing change, other than mentions of records, surveys and evaluations.
