# Mitigation impact

Climate change mitigation through anticipated avoided and reduced greenhouse gas emissions from land use change is estimated at 2,750,323 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO₂ eq) over 10 years, or an average of 173,516 tCO₂eq/year. This will be achieved through community land management interventions and household on-farm interventions, both of which involve changes in forest, grassland and cropland are managed to capture and store carbon in soil and below-ground biomass.

## FAO's own Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (Ex-Act) has been used to estimate in detail the anticipated avoided emissions from land use change and associated avoided degradation, with results and methodology detailed in annex 2 to the funding proposal. In response to a written question from the ITAP about why there is only one sentence about monitoring, reporting and verification of carbon sequestration in the funding proposal process, The M&K Geographical Information Systems specialist in the central Project Implementation Unit will be able to provide more detail on this topic during the upcoming meeting through the M&K system, with an M&K manual detailing the system to be developed and disseminated at inception.

### In response to a question from the ITAP about how precisely the list of landscape and on-farm interventions to be tracked by the M&E specialist and technical experts from non-governmental organizations (NGOs) will be defined, the AE quantified in the ex-ante assessment that the AE developed a more detailed table that is useful in clarifying what should be included in the list for tracking (monitoring, reporting and verification) that is still to be developed. The table shows, for example, how the conversion of high carbon input (business as usual) to cropped land with low carbon input (business as usual) compared to improved management with reduced tillage and higher input of organic material (with project) is derived from Component 1 activities on land use change and associated avoided degradation. The table also shows how technical support for conservation agriculture, manure management, reduced or zero tillage and the use of cover crops are included in the list of landscape interventions to be tracked by the M&E specialist.

### All these linkages in a clear system for tracking and reporting on parcels of land successfully brought under improved management with reduced tillage and higher input organic management practice can be taken as a proxy indicator of enhanced carbon sequestration/reduced emissions, as per the Ex-Act calculation methodology.

The ITAP assesses the impact potential of the project as medium to high.


## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: Medium

The proposed project aims to be a catalyst for a broad shift for ecosystem, livelihoods and agriculture in the country, from their baseline state of very high vulnerability on an alternative pathway towards more resilient landscapes that are economically viable and sustainably managed, thus granting their adaptation to climate change and their supply of services to communities, people and their farming systems.

### Additional written responses from FAO to the ITAP on 24 May 2024.


---
**Page 126**
---

## Upgrading and replication

The project design includes several features to enhance sustainability, including using existing local governance structures and plans as the basis for planning village and catchment-level EIA interventions in Component 1, and integrating village plans into the larger catchment management plans.

### Component 2: Scaling up

Component 2 is focused on scaling up through incentives to partner with EMA projects, involving government extension staff to support and learn from the implementation of agro-ecological approaches and Farmer Field Schools. Component 3 directly tackles the issue of sustainability by providing a package of services that will mainstream EBA approaches and practices into policies, sectoral planning and financing.

### Sustainability of results

Overall, the project relies heavily on temporarily funding a large number of projects through its own implementation, input delivery, and extension support. Although it is positive that these project-based interventions will cooperate and share learning with Ministry of Agriculture extension staff and other officials, it may be difficult to scale up these approaches beyond the life of the project.

### Preparing to access voluntary carbon markets

Preparing to access voluntary carbon markets in future is presented as a part of the project's strategy. The success of this strategy relies on several factors that will depend on many factors beyond the project’s control. The project itself will take only a limited step in this direction by participating in taking an inventory of emissions in the project districts and catchments (including livestock, agriculture, forestry inventories, and soil testing), using an existing methodology developed through the Restore Africa Programme, and sharing findings with relevant stakeholders.

### Leveraging climate finance

Also relevant are the project’s efforts to leverage climate finance through support to the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) and other climate funds. The strategy relies heavily on supporting NCCF to be accredited to GCF, and the Malawi Environmental Endowment Trust to utilize the GCF Specific Project Accreditation Approach modality. Any additional support from other international or domestic financing instruments is welcomed, including debt for nature swaps for the Malawi


---
**Page 127**
---

# Sustainable development potential

## Socioeconomic benefits.

The project’s farm-level EBA or agro-ecological approach through improved crop varieties and practices will increase yields and income for smallholder farmers, which can improve household nutrition levels, and potentially food security. Activities to support the establishment of local seed banks, as well as the promotion of indigenous tree species, should also contribute to poverty alleviation, as they increase household incomes. This is, of course, dependent on the availability of markets supporting farmers to access markets through new offtake agreements and logistics support.

## Environmental benefits.

By scaling up EBA practices in the landscape through activity 12.3 (the establishment of agroforestry systems), it will lead to improved soil quality and water conservation. Revegetation of slopes and riverbanks will reduce erosion, sedimentation, and flooding.

Although not specifically highlighted in section D.3 of the funding proposal, the project will also have significant biodiversity benefits. In response to a question from this on the ITAP at the CEF meeting, it is proposed that the project will contribute to the conservation of native tree species, and landscapes for their conservation and promotion. This will be achieved thanks to (a) the establishment of agroforestry systems in the landscape; (b) the introduction of native plant species, and landscapes, for their conservation and promotion. This will be achieved thanks to (c) the establishment of local seed banks/nurseries at community level (activity 2.1.3) to improve agrobiodiversity in site; (ii) restored or re-established natural landscapes with a high degree of native plant species, and landscapes, for their conservation and promotion.

In addition, it is proposed that the project will contribute to the conservation of native and well-adapted varieties and species to increase biodiversity [in a system currently dominated by maize monoculture] through the establishment of local seed banks/nurseries at community level (activity 2.1.3) to improve agrobiodiversity in site; (ii) restored or re-established natural landscapes with a high degree of native plant species, and landscapes, for their conservation and promotion.

In addition, it is assumed that improved farming practices, including the use of organic fertilizers, will increase soil fertility and microbial biodiversity – critical for soil fertility and agricultural productivity.

## The design of the proposed project has been carried out hand-in-hand with a National Project Preparation Task Team (comprising several government staff and partners), see annex 7 to the funding proposal, with strong stakeholder engagement, and government will be closely engaged in implementation. The project is designed so that the belief in, and behavior change towards sustainability, is embedded in the project, notably through its participatory community engagement approach.

### Policy and strategy alignment

The proposed project is well aligned with the country’s policies and strategies – including the Malawi Growth and Development Strategy II (2017– 2023), the National Forest Policy (2015), the National Land Use Policy (2016), the National Forest Plan 2017-2023, the National Forest and Landscapes Restoration Strategy (2017) and the National Environment Management Act (2014). In addition to these policies and strategies, under the VLPs, the project will support implementation of the Decentralization Act (1998), which devolves power from central government to local governments. It is also aligned with the Environmental Management Act (2013) and the National Forest Policy (2015), the new National Disaster Risk Management Act of 2013 (the bill is referenced in the funding proposal) and implementation of the Climate Change Act.

### For adaptation, the NDC of Malawi (as updated in July 2021) puts emphasis on FdA, integrated catchment conservation and management, and capacity development in resilient agroforestry systems, including climate-smart agriculture and sustainable land management. These interventions are also in line with the country’s national adaptation plan, currently under development.

### The project will support implementation of the Decentralization Act (1998), which devolves power from central government to local governments. It is also aligned with the Environmental Management Act (2013) and the National Forest Policy (2015).

### The Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs (MFEA) are FAO and the Government of Malawi.

Approximately two-thirds of the GCF funding will be spent on activities executed through FAO Malawi, while approximately one-third of the funding will be spent by the Government of Malawi as AE. Approximately one third of GCF funding will be spent on activities to be executed by the Government of Malawi acting through:

- The Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock through its Department of Land Resources
- The Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs: The ministry is implementing the FARMSE initiative (funded by the International Fund for Agricultural Development), the framework of which is utilized by activity 2.3 to support community-based financial organizations, including microfinance institutions, savings groups, and other financial institutions to farmers and small and medium-sized enterprises.

The National Land Use Commission (NLUC). The NLGC is the public body mandated to facilitate fiscal decentralization, financial management and local development in local government. The NLGC will execute sub-activity 1.2.2, providing procurement services for the project through its Procurement Unit.

### Engagement with stakeholders, including civil society

Several stakeholder consultations were conducted during preparation, and are detailed in annex 7 to the funding

proposal which also highlights how stakeholders will be engaged during implementation.

Through national workshops, community engagement meetings at district level and field visits over 2021 and 2022, the wide range of stakeholders engaged included: the Director of the national agricultural research system; representatives from the Ministry of Environment Change and Natural Resources (10 relevant departments); the NLGFC; 8 non-government organizations (including the Consultative Group on International Agricultural Research system); academia and research institutions; private sector representatives (including the Malawi Sugar Board, Lilongwe Sugar Company, Climate Asset Management, and Hongkong and Shanghai Banking Corporation [HSBC]); conservation/environment trusts; savings and credit cooperatives; local government officials; and representatives from the World Bank, Global Environment Facility, National Water Resources Authority; other donor-funded projects; and agribusinesses and retail/distribution companies.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
**Scale: Medium**

a. Of the project's total proposed budget of USD $2.42 million, 80 per cent (USD $1.935 million) is proposed to be a GCF grant; and 20 per cent (USD $481,000) is made up of co-financing to the project, closely woven into the project logic. The co-financing is as follows:

b. The Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs will provide USD $357,600 in grant co-financing for the project. This amount includes contributions from long-term savings associations; linking commercial banks; microfinance institutions; savings and credit cooperatives; and local government officials.

c. The Ministry of Agriculture will provide in-kind or financing for a total amount of USD 2.95 million, including: provision of land plots of agriculture; agricultural extension developers and coordinators; and provision of venues for training of master trainers and facilitators.

d. FMO will provide USD $123,000 of grant co-financing through the Global Environment Facility-Sustainable Forest Management, Dryland Sustainable Landscape Impact Project (GEF-SFM/Dryland SLP) to support the project's environmental management elements serving both projects: a policy assessment and the preparation of a policy influencing plan; development of an institutional framework for forest landscape restoration consultations; and an environmental and social assessment. Through this co-financing, the project will be able to leverage USD $123,000 in long-term vision for complementarity between the Global Environment Facility and GCF.

e. The project also identified two opportunities for further leveraging co-funding during project implementation: (i) a pilot project on climate change adaptation in Malawi (USD 175,000, started late 2021), financed by Climate Asset Management [joint venture between HSBC Asset Management and the World Bank]; and (ii) a pilot project on forest landscape restoration through Catholic Relief Services in Malawi. The second opportunity is funding from the Malawi Agroforestry Project (MAP) and the Global Carbon Partnership for value addition to crops and forest products from the project landscapes.

f. Economic rate of return: The economic analysis conducted by the project proponents (on the basis of a 10-year horizon) indicates that the project will generate benefits higher than the costs of the project interventions. Beneficiaries include: (i) increased productivity in Component 2; (ii) improved access to markets and financial services, including credit; (iii) land support to producers in Component 2; (iv) additional post-production value through access to markets and finance, and entrepreneur development, in Component 2; (v) a significant

## Project as a whole

The project as a whole is shown to have a positive return on investment, with a net present value of USD 68.5 million and an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 32.8 per cent, above the discount rate of 14.2 per cent. The project also has a high social rate of return, providing avoided greenhouse gas emissions using the low social carbon price. Using the high social price of carbon, the project EIRR is still positive at 17.9 per cent.

The sensitivity analysis further shows that even in the most adverse scenario, including a decrease in benefits of 20 per cent and an increase in costs of 20 per cent, the project has a much lower, albeit still positive, EIRR of 12.7 per cent.

## Financial rate of return

The financial analysis considers the project investments in forestland management (including improved land use for agriculture and forest-based honey production) from the perspective of a beneficiary household. Costs and benefits are discounted at an interest rate of 14.2 per cent, which is considered to be the opportunity cost of labour), with models created for each of the two main activity types in the two landscape types, as well as for honey production from beekeeping. Each of the five modes compares the costs and benefits over a period of 30 years.

The analysis presents the internal rate of return (IRR) only for honey production, providing a high rate of return of 62 per cent, "because the investment in year 1 only amounts to producing the bees and hives, which are not included as an initial cost. The IRR is 30 per cent over year 3". This loan is not mentioned in the funding proposal or the feasibility study; thus a proposed "payment-in-kind" arrangement for honey production could be considered.

The project also has a high social rate of return, providing avoided greenhouse gas emissions using the low social carbon price. Using the high social price of carbon, the project EIRR is still positive at 17.9 per cent.

## No IRR is shown

No IRR is shown for the other four production models, with the funding proposal arguing that these are not included in agricultural practices based on annual crops with recurrent and variable costs, rather than a large upfront investment cost, without which "the return on investment would be negative". The project also has a high social rate of return, providing avoided greenhouse gas emissions using the low social carbon price. Using the high social price of carbon, the project EIRR is still positive at 17.9 per cent.

The analysis presents the internal rate of return (IRR) only for honey production, providing a high rate of return of 62 per cent, "because the investment in year 1 only amounts to producing the bees and hives, which are not included as an initial cost. The IRR is 30 per cent over year 3". This loan is not mentioned in the funding proposal or the feasibility study; thus a proposed "payment-in-kind" arrangement for honey production could be considered.

The project also has a high social rate of return, providing avoided greenhouse gas emissions using the low social carbon price. Using the high social price of carbon, the project EIRR is still positive at 17.9 per cent.

## The ITAP rates

The ITAP rates the project's overall efficiency and effectiveness as medium.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP believes that the project "Ecosystems-based Adaptation for Resilient Waterways in Malawi" is a good fit with the priorities of the Green Climate Fund as envisaged by FAO and the Government of Malawi. The project is an important one for reducing the vulnerability of smallholder farmers to climate change and contributes to the country's goals for climate change mitigation in the agriculture, forestry, and land use sectors.

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# PART I: GENDER ASSESSMENT

## 1. INTRODUCTION

In Malawi, the agricultural sector accounts for about 42 percent of the country's Gross domestic product (GDP). Whilst tobacco, tea and sugar represent important cash crops, about 80 percent of the food is produced by smallholder farmers. Smallholder production is characterized by low productivity, limited access to resources, poor infrastructure, and a small compared to crops, and consists mainly of subsistence grazing of sheep, cattle, goats, poultry and pigs. The sector is highly vulnerable to climate change and its associated impacts on climate change. The climate crisis is already triggering more erratic and extreme weather, resulting in chronic food insecurity. In addition, the frequency and intensity of droughts, floods and heatwaves have increased in intensity, frequency, and scale, causing devastating environmental, social, and economic damage. Small-scale farmers are most directly affected, although the influence of climate change on women is often more severe.

Women play a key role in Malawian agriculture. They account for 65 percent of smallholder farmers, perform between 50 and 70 percent of all agricultural tasks and produce 70 percent of locally consumed food. Despite their critical contribution to the sector, they face multiple constraints including limited access and control over land, difficult access to farm inputs and labor-saving technologies, poor infrastructure, lack of education and training, low levels of education levels and limited knowledge of improved agricultural practices, a situation that is worsened by limited access to information, inadequate provision of extension services to support their activities. Women are also less likely than men to own land or livestock. Women's "managed" plots are, on average, 12 percent smaller than those of their male counterparts and 25 percent less productive. This is partly due to the fact that women have less access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers and pesticides, which are critical for increasing farm productivity and improving farming efficiency. The constraints and barriers highlighted above could also be exacerbated by climate change. Since water security is essential for agricultural production with around 9 out of 10 people in Malawi depending on rain-fed agriculture, women's access to water resources at lower levels access to water technologies, such as irrigation, than men; women seems to be more affected by the impacts of climate change.

As it is the case for many countries in Sub-Saharan Africa, gender inequalities persist in Malawi. Given that patriarchy predominates, women have generally held a less privileged position compared with men and are often excluded from decision-making processes on land ownership, access to resources, decision-making authority and economic dependence. Gender roles are defined by traditional and cultural factors that intersect with other social identifiers, such as age, religion, ethnicity, education level, marital status, etc., and the interplay between these roles and relations are the results of social constructs, gender inequalities are reinforced by social norms, particularly in rural areas. The acceptance of male authority over women is transmitted


---
**Page 144**
---

both implicitly and explicitly through various institutions, including in homes, schools, churches and community gatherings.

4. One particular element to be considered in Malawian society is the variation of successions according to whether the district is governed by matrilineal or patrilineal system. In matrilineral system, women in the family inherit land and the man moves into the woman's family. Customary law in matrilineal system only gives women security over their own land. In patrilineal system, men inherit land from one and the woman moves with her husband’s family. Customary law in patrilineral system only gives women security of tenure when marriage fails to subsist. Although women are considered as food producers (especially in matrilineal systems) they often remain the decision makers regarding access and control over land in both matrilineal and patrilineal societies. The matrilineal system is prevalent in southern districts (Nsanje and Chikwawa), and the matrilineral system is prevalent most of the central and Southern Region districts.

5. Assessment of the gender differences is essential to identify and address gender difference in agricultural vulnerability to climate change. The combination of natural resource dependence and gendered terms in assets and resources access shows that women are more vulnerable than men. This means that the current understanding of poverty could be nuanced considering the existence of matrilineal and patrilineal societies, underlining the need for a gender-sensitive approach to understand women’s and men's vulnerabilities and develop appropriate project interventions. Addressing gender inequality in agriculture in the context of climate change requires that project support, such as the promotion of agricultural technologies, training, credit access, and additional solution, responds to the different needs of men and women farmers.

6. The Global Fund (GCF) project, "Ecosystems-based Adaptation at the Watershed Level: Community Based Climate Change Resilience in Malawi", aims to increase climate change resilience of rural communities at watershed level in Malawi. It aspires to be a catalyst for a broad shift from traditional farming practices towards more sustainable and resilient agriculture very vulnerable to an alternative paradigm when which watershed ecosystems are restored, well-functioning and sustainably managed. The Gender Assessment conducted as part of the project design process highlighted that women face many challenges in accessing resources and land. The project is designed to shed light on gender-specific constraints, needs and opportunities for a strategic approach to address these issues. The assessment also highlights that the project area has highlighted a number of climate change adaptation barriers facing women and youth that are of social, technical, commercial, financial and institutional nature; the project will address them in order to make sure that they do not become obstacles to the achievement of the design and implementation process to increase the climate resilience of integrated landscape/watershed ecosystems. The project is designed to (i) build adaptive capacity of rural communities with a view to more resilient livelihoods and food systems, and that (ii) a gender-sensitive enabling environment for women and youth in the project area is created through the design and socially inclusive climate resilient landscape, watershed ecosystems and agriculture practices. To ensure gender mainstreaming is clearly visible in the design and implementation of project's activities, the project will adopt a gender perspective in all phases of planning of project activities and sub-activities from a gender and social inclusion perspective, and includes clear


---
**Page 145**
---

targets, gender design features and measurable indicators ensuring women’s participation and benefits.

7. The underlying theory of change is: the project will recognize the strengths, constraints and needs of the women and youth so that their potential as agents of change will be realized for achieving the best results possible under the proposed project. It will necessarily entail changes in attitudes and discourse on men’s and women’s roles in agriculture, including women and men in the targeted communities. Youth’s participation will also be promoted since they could provide valuable contributions that will complement women’s and men’s inputs.

2. METHODOLOGY

8. The Gender Assessment seeks to understand the situation of women in agriculture, including natural resource management in the context of climate change, in areas targeted by the proposed project namely in the following districts: Karonga, Chitipa, Rumphi, Nkhotakha Bay, Dedza, Neno, Salima, Machinga, Phalombe, Chipinge and Chimanimani. The assessment also covers youth, who constitute another vulnerable social group and are likely to be affected and affect the project.

9. The Assessment has mainly relied on literature review and consultations. The outputs through literature review were discussed with relevant stakeholders and key informant persons through focus group discussions (FGDs) and individual interviews. The main questions and suggestions focused on key issues pertaining to the lives of women, the core questions asked are included in Section 3.1.2. The assessment was conducted in the following districts: Karonga, Chitipa, Rumphi along the 11 targeted by EbAm considering the representativeness of the North, Centre and South regions were visited. In addition, two other districts not targeted by the project were also visited to explore potential issues in terms of gender. Two other districts non-targeted by the project were also visited as they showcase interesting experiences on watershed and natural resource management that could be scaled-up by the project (Table 1).

10. A total of 333 villagers in the target areas were consulted, of which 211 were women and about a hundred were young people<sup>16</sup>. Women's views were collected through women-only and mixed Focus group discussions (FGD). The same applied to youth. Institutional consultations were held with district officials, local government representatives, and other stakeholders in consultation 7.1. The results of literature review combined with consultation related information are summarised below.

11. To the best extent possible, the assessment is based on official statistics and published research results pertaining to the target regions and field consultations conducted in those regions. Where relevant information could not be found, it relies on that of the whole nation. Where no such information exists, the assessment may refer to the general consensus among the professionals in the field, or anecdotes.

## 3. LEGAL AND ADMINISTRATIVE FRAMEWORK

### 3.1 Equality, Inclusion and Protection: Women, Youth and Children

#### 3.1.1 Treaties, constitution and laws

12. The Government of Malawi is a party to most of the International and regional instruments that promote human rights in general and women, youth and child rights in particular. Table 2 below shows the international treaties and conventions ratified by Malawi. These include all kinds of human rights, equal rights of women and men and the protection of women from all form of discrimination. The Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child has also protected the rights of children, except the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on a child's right to participate in decision-making which should have allowed the Committee that monitors States parties compliance with the Convention to receive and consider complaints from a child’s representative from its jurisdiction.

# African Youth Charter (July 2, 2006)

Aims to strengthen and consolidate efforts to empower young people through meaningful youth participation in all spheres of national life and Africa's development agenda.

## Convention on the Rights of the Child, adopted on November 20, 1989

Sets out the civil, political, economic, social, health and cultural rights that should be enjoyed by every child. The minimum age for admission to compulsory education is set at nine years old; the age of majority is attained earlier under national legislation.

## Minimum Age Convention (with the specification that the minimum age is 14 for the child) (1979) (N° 136)

Sets the general minimum age for admission to compulsory education as 9 and the minimum age for hazardous work as 18 (as of 2015). Initially setting the general minimum age at 14 (for light work), it was raised to 15 when educational facilities are sufficiently developed.

## Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the involvement of children in armed conflict (2000)

A signatory state commits not to involve children under the age of 18 in armed conflicts. Ratified on September 21, 2010

## Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the sale of children, child prostitution and child pornography (2000)

Strengthens implementation of the Convention and increases the protection of children from involvement in armed conflicts and from sale, prostitution and pornography. Ratified on October 7, 2009

## Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on a communications procedure (2011)

A signatory state recognizes the competence of the Committee to receive communications from States parties that monitors States' parties' compliance with its provisions and that are submitted by or on behalf of children of a heretofore representative within its jurisdiction. Not signed

## African Charter on the Rights and Welfare of the Child (1999)

Highlights the issues of greater importance to the child, such as the right to education and the right to work, while building on the same basic principles as the Convention on the Rights of the Child.

### Status for Malawi
Ratified in 2010

Sources:
- http://www.africanchild.org/malawi

The Constitution of the Republic of Malawi of 1994 promotes equality between women and men. In its fundamental principles, it recognizes the dignity and worth of each human being and guarantees the protection of their basic rights, according to its terms: "the inherent dignity and worth of all human beings; the equal and inalienable rights of all individuals, irrespective of race, colour or creed; respect for the inherent dignity and worth of all human beings and afford the fullest protection to the rights and views of all individuals, groups and minorities whether or not they are entitled to vote." It also prohibits any discrimination based on


---
**Page 149**
---

on *race*, colour, sex, language, religion, political or other opinion, national, ethnic or social origin,
disability, property, birth or other status or condition*.

14. The Constitution includes a bill of rights primarily in its sections 23 and 24 which invalidate any practice that discriminates against women, and enshrines gender equity, inheritance and
guidelines on family and marriage. Section 24 stipulates that *“1. Women have the right to full and equal participation in political life and public affairs. 2. Women shall not be discriminated against their
gender or marital status which includes the right: (a) to be accorded the same rights as men in civil law, including marriage, divorce, adoption and inheritance; (b) to be protected from any law that
discriminates against women on the basis of gender or marital status shall be invalid and legislation shall be passed to eliminate customs and practices that discriminate against women,
particularly those which deny women their rights to education, employment, participation in work, business and public affairs; and (c) deprivation of property, including property obtained by
inheritance.”*

15. The Amendment of Malawi’s Constitution in 2017 emphasized on raising the age of the child from 16 to 18 and testified the will to step towards ending child marriage. The Amendment followed the effort to harmonize all laws on the age of the child given that Malawi adopted in February 2015 the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the early
age of marriage at 18 for boys and girls.

16. In addition to the Constitution, and in order to accelerate progress towards meeting the Millennium Development Goals as well as the SADC Gender Protocol targets, the Malawi Government has reviewed, enacted and implemented several laws that lead to gender equity outcomes (Table 3). The laws essentially promote and protect women and children rights at all
levels.


*Malawi Constitution - Chap. IV (Human Rights) - Section 20 (Equality); A1.
*Malawi Constitution - Chap. V (Basic rights) - Art. 24 (Rights of women).

The act provides for sex discrimination, harmful practices and sexual harassment. Provides for public awareness on promotion of gender equality and provides for registration of complaints. |
| Marriage, Divorce, and Family Relations Act 2015 | Makes provision for marriage, divorce, and family relations between spouses and their children in relation to custody, guardianship, maintenance, and that of their children. |
| Deceased Estates (Will, Inheritance and Protection) Act 2011 | Provides for the registration of wills; the devolution of property under a will; the inheritance to the estates of persons dying without valid testamentary disposition or intestate succession; the administration of deceased estates; the prosecution of offences relating to deceased estates; and other matters incidental thereto, including those in relation to deceased estates and for other connected matters. |

Source: Malawi.org.uk/malawi

17. Considered as the most relevant national legislation relating to our assessment, the Gender Equality Act 2013 is essentially the domestication of the Maputo Protocol. It aims at empowering women in all spheres of life by ensuring their equal participation and equality.

Part II of the act related to “Sex discrimination” prohibits discrimination of women on the ground of sex in any matter relating to marriage, divorce, family relations or succession. The law treats a woman more favourably than he she would treat a person of his her own sex; or (art. 4.b) applying to the other person an exclusion, distinction or restriction which applies or would apply equally to both sexes.

The act also provides for penalties and sanctions in respect of any person found guilty of engaging the commission of any harmful practice. Sanctions for contraveners are also specified therein namely imprisonment not exceeding five years and/or a fine.

18. The Gender Equality Act 2013 with sexual harassment, an act that is considered part of gender-based violence. Section 6.1 of the act defines sexual harassment as “any form of


---
**Page 151**
---

17

unwanted verbal or physical conduct of a sexual nature in circumstances in which a reasonable person, having regard to all the circumstances, would be anticipated that the other person would be offended, humiliated or intimidated and announces the corresponding sanctions. The Act also proposes measures (art. 7) that the Government shall take to make sure employers provide a safe working environment free from sexual harassment and other forms of virtual harassment in the workplace. They consist, among other things, of "entitling all persons who have been subjected to sexual harassment or other forms of virtual harassment at work to redressance and be guaranteed that appropriate disciplinary action shall be taken against perpetrators", "obliging the person in charge of the work place to implement the policy and procedures and impose sanctions on those found guilty" and "establishing a procedure for investigation and five internal sexual harassment procedures before prosecution of the offence can be commenced or civil proceedings instituted."

19. In terms of access to services, while the Act seeks to promote equal rights for girls' and boys' access to education and training (Sections 14 to 18), articles related to fairness in public service appointments could be questioned with the quotas setting "of no less than forty per cent and no more than sixty per cent" of women's representation in public service. The Act also includes and exceptions related to the application of the quotas (Section 11). Part VI of the Act describes the rights of women in sexual and reproductive health, and Part VII promotes public awareness of women's rights.

20. The Prevention of Domestic Violence Act 2006 shows the commitment of the State to eliminate violence against women. It is a comprehensive law that seeks to ensure that through the provisions of the Act apply to both males and females; this law provides provisions for safeguarding and offer redress to women, who are the main victims of domestic violence in the country.

21. The Deceased Estates (Wills, Inheritance and Protection) Act 2015 makes provisions related to intestacy, succession, administration of estates, wills, inheritance and protection in Malawi. It applies to the administration of estates of all persons dying and residing, or leaving property in Malawi at any time after the commencement of this Act. The Act provides that as provided for in this Act, no person shall be entitled under any other written law or under customary law to succeed to an estate by intestacy or otherwise than as provided in this Act on the date of his or her death. The Act provides with respect to making of wills, inheritance in case of intestacy, powers of court, protection and administration of (small) estates, execution of wills and related matters. It also makes provision for the rights of women and girls to inherit land and women legal authority to inherit land if widowed and provides women and girls with equal inheritance rights as male family members. Land grabbing is also considered as a criminal offence by the Act.

22. The Marriage, Divorce, and Family Relations Act 2015 makes provision for marriage, divorce, and family relations between spouses and ex-married couples, their welfare and maintenance, and of their children. It seeks to bring law in line with international best practice toward preventing child marriage, and showed its willingness to protect girls from the abuse and exploitation that can result from early marriage. The Act also provides for the protection of boys and girls; the new Marriage Act consolidates all laws on marriage and divorce. It includes a new requirement for couples wishing to marry or divorce to register their intention with the court, including civil, religious and customary marriage by repute or permanent cohabitation. This law also contains strong protections for married women who may find themselves in a situation with no


---
**Page 152**
---

right to succession or inheritance or share of property, maintenance or even custody of children upon termination of family relationships.

23. The Marriage, Divorce and Family Relations Act was at the origin of the Constitution amendment that came into force in parliament in 2017. The amendment removed the provision allowing children between the ages of 15 and 18 to marry with parental consent.

24. The Child Care, Protection and Justice Act 2010 is aiming at preventing violence against children and protecting them from all forms of abuse, exploitation, neglect, child marriage, and harmful practices. Although the Act refers to the Constitution in its Section 3, the definition of "child" that Acts consists of a person below the age of sixteen years**, it is not in line with what the Malawian Constitution defines as a child (Section 21). The Act also does not define the age of eighteen years**.

25. In its Part II, not only does the Act provide details about child care and protection by the family, but it also reflects situations that are considered with substantial risks for children and which result in children needing care and protection. These include child neglect from parent or guardian (Part II, Section 1), sexual abuse of a child (Part II, Section 2), and so on. On one hand the Act makes provision for child protection (Part II), it also includes on the other hand measures dedicated to children who are suspected to have committed offenses (Part III), the Act provides for the establishment of a special court for children (Part IV) and for the imposition of criminal responsibilities (Part IV); and to child legal representation (Part V). Mechanisms related to Child Protection, including those that provide for review boards (Part VI) and referrals centers and safety homes (Part VII) are also described.

26. The Education Act 2013 governs Malawi's education system and provides the promotion of educational access and quality as well as the duty to promote education for all people in Malawi; irrespective of race, ethnicity, gender, religion, disability or any other discriminatory characteristics**, some provisions that are entirely consistent with what is stipulated in the Constitution. The Act also provides for free primary education and equal educational opportunity for all Malawians by identifying and removing barriers to achievement** (Part II, Section 5). Equality of access to education is a constitutional right, but no specific mention is made in relation to the access of girls and boys. The Government promotes a generalized access to education by providing free and compulsory primary education to every child between six and eleven years old.

27. The Trafficking in Persons Act 2015 focuses on the prevention and elimination of the issue and complements the provisions related to human rights. It pays particular attention to the protection of children from being trafficked, including by prohibiting the recruitment, transporting, transferring, harbouring, receiving or obtaining a person, within or beyond the territory of Malawi, for the purpose of exploitation (Part I). The Act also prohibits fraud or deception; (a) abuse or threats of abuse of power or position; (e) abuse or threats of abuse of position of vulnerability; (f) abuse or threats of abuse of the law or legal process; or (g) giving or receiving a bribe or other form of remuneration for the purpose of obtaining or refusing to obtain, for the purpose of exploitation that person** by The Act. The law distinguishes different types of offences and provides for penalties in accordance with the gravity of each offence committed. The penalties for trafficking in persons and trafficking in children are respectively 14 years and 21 years of imprisonment without the option of a fine. Section 30 of the Act stipulates that a person

**31. The purpose of the National Gender Policy 2015 is to strengthen gender mainstreaming and women’s empowerment at all levels in order to facilitate attainment of gender equality and equity in Malawi; and the policy’s goal is “to reduce gender inequalities and enhance participation of women, girls and boys in socio-economic development processes.” The Policy has targeted areas such as: (i) health and nutrition; (ii) education and skills acquisition and training; (iii) economic development and employment; (iv) social protection and safety nets; (v) environment and climate change management; (vi) gender equality and GBV in GBV as well as the capacity of the national structures involved in the implementation of the gender policy in terms of outcomes, the Policy aims at the following.**

20 National Gender Policy, 2015 (Second Edition), Ministry of Gender, Youth and Community Service, Foreword

19


---
**Page 154**

- Increased meaningful participation of women, men, girls and boys in decision making,
- wealth creation and poverty reduction,
- Reduction gender-based violence at all levels;
- Enhanced gender mainstreaming across all sectors; and
- Enhanced coordination within the Gender Machinery

32. The Policy recognizes that climate change management is important in the lives of women. It considers as new major challenges: HIV and AIDS; GBV; human trafficking; increased environmental degradation; extreme weather events, and higher temperatures.

The policy aims to ensure for women and other vulnerable groups access to control over agricultural resources, technologies and markets for cash crops. Under natural resources management, women are often excluded from decision-making processes. The effects of deforestation are amplified by gender inequality in decision-making power and access to information on climate change adaptation and mitigation measures.

33. The Ministry responsible for Gender is the one a charge of coordinating and providing oversight regarding the implementation of the Gender Policy across all sectors.

b. The National Plan of Action to Combat Gender-Based Violence (2014-2020)

34. Considered as the most recent National Plan of Action (NPA) and developed in the framework of the National Gender Policy, the NPA to Combat Gender-Based Violence 2014-2020 has followed the same approach as its predecessors: the 2008-2013 Strategy to Combat GBV and the 2008-2013 National Response to GBV. and provided comprehensive response to GBV in all sectors, including health, education, justice, law enforcement, men and children. The NPA draws heavily on lessons learned from the implementation of its two predecessors and identified the remaining challenges in GBV programming across all sectors, namely:

- Lack of Sufficient Data and un-coordinated research, monitoring and evaluation of outputs and outcomes of GBV interventions;
- Communication and gender change communication to prevent GBV;
- Limited dissemination and outreach;
- Weak results analysis and lack of results based approach in the implementation of activities
- Failure to include the private sector as a partner with established programmes on gender equality and women empowerment.
- Failure to address violence against children which has risen to unprecedented levels.

35. The 2014-2020 NPA aimed to provide a strong framework for sustainable intervention to prevent and respond effectively to GBV, in order to reduce the incidence of GBV, improve quality and to improve the quality of services responding to GBV by 2020. The five priority areas of the NPA have been: (i) the prevention of GBV by addressing the root causes and promoting transformation of harmful social norms; (ii) strengthening the capacity of service providers for early detection, response and thus reduces its impact and continuation; (iii) the creation of an effective response mechanism supporting survivors of GBV; (iv) the establishment of a strong monitoring system to track progress of the NPA; and (v) research, data collection, monitoring and evaluation. In order to overcome the weak coordination among stakeholders in the GBV sector, the NPA has focused on strengthening the importance of activity coordination and results based approach. The implementation of the Plan relied on the strong leadership of the Ministry of Gender, Children, Disability and Social Welfare (MoGCDSW) and the National Council for Women's Affairs (NCWA). Joint collaboration was mentioned on the use of the Gender, Children, Youth and Sports sector Working Group: Joint


---
**Page 155**
---

Sector Strategic Plan (SSP) 2013-2017 and the non-governmental organisations (NGOs) coordinated by the NGO Gender Coordination Network (NOGCON). The MoCDCSW was also expected to lead on the General Technical Working Group for the National HIV/AIDS and Integrated Community Development at district level and the existing Government recognized community structures.

c. National HIV/AIDS Policy (2003)

36. The National HIV/AIDS Policy 2003 comes as a response to the identification of HIV/AIDS as one of the key development challenges of Malawi as per the UNDP Country Development Report in 1998. The policy recognizes that HIV/AIDS is an issue engaging strong cross-sectoral collaboration and sustained action that the HIV/AIDS challenge requires. Among others, the Policy recognized in its Preamble: (i) the unequal position of girls and women in society; and that women are disproportionately affected by HIV/AIDS; (ii) that HIV/AIDS has a greater impact on women due to biological, social, cultural and economic factors; (iii) the discrimination and marginalization experienced by people living with HIV/AIDS; (iv) the importance of individual and collective well-being, development and human security; and (v) the strong influence culture and religion have on lifestyle and choices.

37. The Policy's goal was therefore: "to prevent HIV infections, to reduce vulnerability to HIV, to improve access to treatment, care and support for people living with HIV/AIDS and to mitigate the socio-economic impact of HIV/AIDS on individuals, families, communities and the nation". The policy had the following objectives:

- Prevent HIV Infections
  - Improve delivery of prevention, treatment, care and support services;
  - Mitigate the socio-economic impact of HIV/AIDS on individuals, the family and communities.
- Reduce individual and societal vulnerability to HIV/AIDS through the creation of an enabling environment;
- Strengthen national leadership and multi-disciplinary institutional framework for coordination and implementation of HIV/AIDS programmes in the country".

38. Apart from strengthening and sustaining a comprehensive multisectoral response to HIV/AIDS, including HIV/AIDS prevention, treatment, care, and support and impact mitigation; the Government in Chapter 4 made some provisions related to the rights of people living with HIV/AIDS stipulating that:

- Government shall respect, protect and fulfill the rights and dignity of people living with HIV/AIDS;
- Government and partners shall ensure a conducive legal, political, economic, social and cultural environment for the full enjoyment by people living with and affected by HIV/AIDS are respected, protected and fulfilled;
- Government and partners shall ensure an effective participation of people living with HIV/AIDS in all decision making in relation to the formulation, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of HIV/AIDS related policies and programmes.
- Government and partners shall ensure that people living with HIV/AIDS are not discriminated against in access to health care and related services and that respect for privacy and confidentiality is maintained;
- Government and partners shall ensure that HIV/AIDS, whether suspected or real, is not used as a reason for denying an individual access to social services, including health care, education and employment opportunities.


---
**Page 156**
---

- Sector policy makers, including labour, corporate and social service sectors shall ensure that sectoral policies are put in place that effectively address discrimination on the basis of HIV status and take steps to effectively eliminate stigma and discrimination in their institutions and in the implementation of their sectoral mandates.
- Government and other institutions shall provide access to justice for all persons whose rights have been infringed, have access to independent, speedy and effective legal and/or administrative procedures.
- Government and other institutions shall establish mechanisms and services to protect those who choose to disclose their HIV status at family, community or national levels, as well as in the workplace.
- Government shall ensure that orphans living with HIV are not discriminated against access to benefits and services provided by the State or through private or philanthropic initiatives.

39. Chapter 5 of the Policy also made specific provisions towards certain vulnerable groups that are considered “underprivileged socially, culturally and economically or legally less able to fully access education, health care and social services and means of HIV prevention and less able to protect themselves from HIV infection.” These groups include women and girls; these groups included among other women and girls, orphans, widows and widowers, children and youth with disabilities, older persons and people with disabilities. Policy statements related to women and girls were as follows:

- Government shall ensure that women and girls, regardless of marital status, have equal access to appropriate HIV prevention services, including information on and means of preventing and health services including women specific and youth friendly sexual and reproductive health services for all women of reproductive age, including women living with HIV.
- Government will protect the rights of women and girls to have control over and decide freely and responsibly about their sexual and reproductive health and rights in relation to their sexuality, including sexual and reproductive health services.
- Government shall ensure that women and girls are protected against violence, including sexual violence, rape and other forms of coerced sex, as well as against traditional practices that affect the health of women.
- Government will ensure equal access for women and girls to legal equality and capacity within the family, in matters such as divorce, inheritance, child custody, property and employment rights, in particular with regard to HIV prevention and care. Women and girls shall have full access to responsible positions, measures to reduce conflicts between professional and family responsibilities and protection against sexual harassment in the workplace.
- Government will ensure that women and girls are provided with information on the benefits of scientific and technological progress so as to minimise risk of HIV infection;
- Government will ensure that women and girls living with HIV have access to life skills education, which addresses unequal gender relations, to enable them to protect themselves from HIV infection or live positively with HIV/AIDS if they are already infected.
- Government and partners shall develop and implement gender sensitive HIV/AIDS care programs for women and girls in the context of care at home, hospital, clinic, community and family or household and hospice.”

40. One important point covered by the Policy is the consideration of how many practices can increase a woman’s risk of HIV infection. These practices include “polygamy, extra marital sexual relations, marital rape, first aid to snake bite victims, ear piercing and tattooing and customary practices such as widow and widower inheritance, death cleansing (kupida kuta), forced sex for


---
**Page 157**
---

young girls coming of age (18), new born baby cleansing (kutenga mwanu), circumcision (jandhokwana), ablation of dead bodies, committal adultery for childless couple (his), wife and husband exchange (chimwanamwe), temporary husbandship (mukha), and the use of lalitso (mphini)[1]). Provisions are made on the involvement and contribution of Government, traditional and religious leaders in the prevention and HIV. The Policy also clarifies the implication of Traditional Healers and Traditional Birth Attendants.

41. Several plans and strategies have been developed to support the implementation of the Policy:
- The Malawi HIV and AIDS Extended National Action Framework (NAF), 2015-2012.
- The National Strategic Plan (NSP) for HIV and AIDS 2015-2020.
- The National HIV Prevention Strategy 2015-2020.
- The National HIV Testing and Counseling Policy 2015-2020.

42. Despite that the NSP 2015-2020 had by far fallen short to achieving its prevention goals of reducing new infections from 58,000 in 2010 to 11,000 in 2020 (with 33,000 new infections estimated for the year 2014), the country has made significant progress towards the NSP 2015-2020. This included among others the tremendous progress towards the 90:90:90 testing and treatment target[2], the expanse of the condom and lubricant programming, the availability of antiretroviral drugs (ARVs) in all public health facilities, the expansion of the Blantyre and Bintumbe districts which is considered as an additional prevention intervention, the delivery of a comprehensive package of services to pregnant women and children under five years old, the structural inequalities that impact both Adolescent Girls and Young Women (AGYW) and Adolescent Boys and Young Men (ABYM), the availability of HIV interventions focusing on Adolescent Girls and Young Women (AGYW), the Elimination Maternal to Child Transmission (e-MTCT) program, and improvements in the health system.

43. The goal of the NSP 2020-2025 is to contribute towards ending AIDS as a public health threat in Malawi by 2025 through: (i) reducing new HIV infections from 33,000 in 2019 to 11,000 in 2025; (ii) reduce HIV and AIDS related morbidity and mortality; (iii) reach 95- 95-95 targets for testing, treatment and viral suppression[3]; and (iv) strengthen the health and social welfare systems for effectively responding to the HIV and AIDS epidemic. The Plan has the National AIDS Commission as one national coordinating authority and features a unique agreed monitoring and evaluation system.

d. Sexual Reproductive Health and Rights Policy

44. The adoption of a comprehensive and integrated approach to Sexual Reproductive Health and Rights (SRHR) in Malawi constitutes a necessary response to expanding needs that includes increasing demand for family planning services, the growing number of young people sexually active, and a growing burden of reproductive ill health[4]. The National Health Policy of 2012 presents a framework that articulates issues that are central to the development of health delivery systems in Malawi. The Policy serves as a point of reference in the provision of sound foundation for the

[3] The policy also provides for the elimination of gender-based violence, including sexual and domestic violence prevention and personal HIV risk determination, social asset building, back to school support, and village savings and loans programmes. These interventions are aimed at reducing the incidence of new HIV infections among adolescents and young people in the epidemic control. National AIDS Commission.

---
**Page 158**
---

successful provision of a comprehensive range of health services, which includes the sexual and reproductive health services.

45. In Malawi, the National Sexual and Reproductive Health and Rights (SRHR) Policy was formulated in 2002 and revised in 2017. The development of the ongoing National Sexual Reproductive Health and Rights (SRHR) Policy (2017-2022) built on the success of the 2008-2016 SRHR Policy and response to emerging challenges. The policy is aligned with the following key components of SRHR namely: Basic Emergency Obstetric and Neonatal Care (BeEmONC), Community Based Neonatal Care (CBNC), Family Planning, Prevention and Treatment of Sexually Transmitted Infections (STIs), Voluntary Male Medical Circumcision (VMMC) and Prevention of Mother to Child Transmission of HIV (PMTCT). In terms of legislative framework, the Policy is aligned to the Health Sector Strategic Plan 2017-2022. The policy recognizes that adolescent health indicators remain poor. The SRHR Policy also is linked to the Malawi National Health Service and Malawi Reproductive Health Services National Standards, the HIV/AIDS policy and the Malawi Gender Policy.

46. The overall goal of the SRHR Policy has been to provide a framework for provision of accessible, acceptable and affordable, comprehensive SRHR services to all women, men, and young people in Malawi. The policy also seeks to enable them attain their reproductive rights and goals safely. The Policy objectives are as follow:

- Provide direction to decision makers and programme managers for effective implementation.
- Provide guidelines for capacity building for provision of quality SRHR services;
- Assist in evidence, harmonization, and standardization of guidelines for provision of SRHR services
- Inform and guide stakeholders and partners on SRHR issues[2].

47. In its purpose to address the pertinent SRHR problems neonatal and maternal health, family planning, STIs, HIV/AIDS, and other SRHR issues, the SRHR Policy considers the different age groups and diverse groups of Malawians. For instance, the policy addresses sexual and reproductive health needs of adolescents and young people in Malawi. To other groups such as the mentally disabled and disabled persons, like for every other person, the policy addresses SRHR issues of pregnancy, family planning, STIs and HIV and AIDS. Similarly, the policy also considers the needs of women with disabilities. The rights and equity based approach, gender sensitivity, ethical considerations, efficiency, accountability, communication, collaboration, coordination, decentralization, appropriate technology, and responsiveness[3]. Six topics among the 10 priority areas are addressed in the SRHR Policy namely: (i) Maternal health; (ii) maternal and neonatal health; (iii) sexually transmitted infections and HIV/AIDS; (iv) young people in reproductive health; (v) Harmful Practices/Domestic Violence, and (vi) Male Involvement in Reproductive Health. The policy provides provisions related to policy direction and strategies for each topic.

48. The implementation of the SRHR Policy is through national RH Programme coordination on Sexual and Reproductive Health and Rights (RHQ), which operates under the guidance of the SRHR Technical Working Group at the policy/technical level. The Policy implementation relies on community participation as well as a comprehensive Behaviour Change Communication (BCC) Strategy covering all aspects of SRHR. 51. Building on these tested models and urging all partners to buy them in and scale them out, the Strategy has outlined a framework for planning, implementation, coordination and monitoring of programs for engaging men and boys.
52. The Strategy has highlighted the barriers to men's engagement which are patriarchy and masculinities that are rooted in social norms and cultural practices. It has identified six key areas of focus: (i) challenging of male stereotypes, (ii) male involvement, (iii) increase of male engagement in GBV & HIV and SRHR services, (iv) transformative masculinity, (v) reinforcement of legal frameworks and (vi) strengthening of coordination mechanisms.
The strategy objectives are:
- To challenge male stereotypes, dominant negative social norms and construct transformative masculinities
- To ensure reinforcement of legal frameworks and implementation of policies;
- To promote male involvement in accessing SRHR services, eradicating GBV and reducing the risk of HIV transmission;
- To enhance coordination of Male Engagement activities at National, District and Community levels.
- To encourage research, learning and knowledge management on male engagement. |

53. In 2018, the government launched the National Strategy on Ending Child Marriage 2018-2023. The strategy advocates for a multi sectoral approach to reduce the prevalence of child
