### National Education Policy (2016)

54. The National Education Policy (NEP) 2016 was developed to provide proper guidance in the education sector given that a comprehensive policy framework was available to guide the successive education planning. The latter included the Education Development Survey (1964), the first National Education Plan (NEP I, 1973–85); the Second National Education Survey (1985–
1995); the Free Primary Education (FPE) Policy in 1994; the Policy and Investment Framework (2000); and the National Education Development Vision (NEDV) 2007-2017 that also helped put the discussion of the education sector’s long-term vision and priorities on the agenda for education development[6]. The NEP was intended to stand out as one guiding living document to inform the planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and review processes in education.

55. The Policy had the purpose of “guiding and providing a coordinated approach to the development of the education system for relevant knowledge, skills, competencies and values necessary for Malawi’s socio-economic transformation.” The NEP aimed at ensuring equitable access to education and improve relevance, quality and governance and management of the education sector[7]. The NEP outlined 7 priority areas that should guide the development of the educational system:

- Quality, accessible and equitable basic education including early childhood development and education; out-of-school youth education; adult literacy and primary education;
- Accessible and equitable secondary education;
- Quality teacher education (primary and secondary);
- Quality and equitably accessed Technical, Entrepreneurial and Vocational Education and Training (TEVET);
- Quality and equally accessible higher education;
- Quality and accessible lifelong learning and training;
- Enhanced Science, Technology and Innovation in Education[8].

56. The NEP was developed by the Ministry of Education with the support and coordination of the MNEP and implementation. Several other institutions were involved in its implementation such as the Malawi Institute of Education (MIE) for the development and evaluation of the school curriculum; the National Examinations Council (NEC) for the administration of national examinations and development of examination syllabuses; the Ministry of Health for the provision of health services to students, teachers and teaching government ministers; intellectual and civil societies with UNESCO: the Malawi National Library Service (MNLS); the National Curriculum Development Centre (NCDC); the Teaching Service Commission (TSC) for professional development programs, teacher recruitment, promotions, and discipline. The Policy implementation also involved the following Ministries: Ministry of Health; Ministry of Justice; Ministry of Finance and Economic Affairs; Ministry of Youth Development and Sports; Ministry of Gender, Children, Disability and Social Welfare; Ministry of Local Government and Rural Development; Ministry of Lands and Housing; Office of the Director of Public Procurement; National Audit Office.

[6] Policy Paper: Realizing the African Union Campaign to End Child Marriage. Emerging Lessons from Child Marriage Programming in Malawi and Zambia. BISA. UN Women, 2020.
[7] National Education Policy. Ministry of Education, Science and Technology, October 2016 - Preface


---
**Page 163**
---

57. Developed in the framework of the NEIP 2018, the National education sector investment plan (NESIP) 2020-2030 is the fifth formal education plan for the sector. It builds on the achievement of the National Education Sector Plan (NESSP) 2008-2017, the first comprehensive plan developed by the Ministry of Education, takes into account the NESPI challenges and defines what needs to be done in order to achieve the NEIP goals. The NESIP is a long-term strategic plan among others the overall increase in access to education across all levels of education; the review of curriculum content and pedagogy; the development of a national teacher policy framework, specifically, the development of the National Education Act and the National Education Policy.

58. The NESIP is a long-term strategic document for the education sector and it sets objectives and results, and guides the implementation of key activities to achieve national and international commitments from 2020 to 2030. Taking into account all the subsectors and implementing partners, the NESIP will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach bringing together all education stakeholders together and improve coordination across all levels of education. Relying on the goal defined in the NEP, the NESIP has defined three following thematic objectives:
- Increase access to equitable education programs at all levels of the education system;
- Enhance the quality of learning outcomes that are relevant to Malawi’s socio-economic development; and
- Strengthen inclusive, efficient and accountable governance and management of the education system.

59. The NESIP has been elaborated into 7 sub-programmes related to various levels of education. These sub-programmes have been developed in line with the NEIP strategic objectives that are aligned to the below thematic objectives and accompanied by priority actions. Among other sub-programmes, inclusive education, gender and other cross-cutting issues constitute a major part of the NESIP. The different aspects such as disability, HIV/AIDS, gender SHINHA and other crosscutting issues are integrated across all levels of the education system.

60. As it is the case for the NEP, the MoE will lead the implementation of the plan with support from all stakeholders in the education sector.

h. National Youth Policy

61. Drawing from the 1996 National Youth Policy that had guided previous youth programmes and services, the National Youth Policy 2013 considered new challenges and emerging issues that young people face today. The policy was developed to provide a framework for commitments to which Malawi is signatory (the African Youth Charter, the Commonwealth Plan of Action for Youth Development, the United Nations Millennium Development Goals, the International Youth Health Protocols, etc.). The policy sought to provide a framework with guidelines for the facilitation of meaningful participation and engagement by young people, full participation of the youth themselves at all levels. Its goal consists of creating an enabling environment for young people to develop to their full potential in order to contribute significantly to personal and sustainable national development. The policy has four main strategic objectives:
- Guide policy makers on issues relating to young people;
- Mainstream youth development agenda in all national development programmes;
- Provide a framework for the implementation of youth programmes for youth;
- Guide the adequate allocation and prudent use of resources (financial, human, and material) to youth programmes;

29


---
**Page 164**
---

- Provide guidance for the protection of young people;
- Advocate for the equal participation of young people in the formulation of legislation and policies affecting youth at all levels;
- Mainstream gender equity and equality in all youth programmes;
- Provide adequate resources to youth-led and youth-managed programmes and ensuring youth are included as active participants; and
- Provide a multi-sectoral and multi-disciplinary institutional framework for coordination and implementation of youth programmes*.

62. While clarifying the rights of youth as enshrined in the Convention on the Rights of Child (CRC), the Policy also addresses issues such as child labour, child trafficking, child abuse, etc., The Policy exposes the responsibilities of youth in terms of health; education; economic, social and cultural development; and the protection of human rights. The Policy also highlights the roles of adults towards the Youth. One particular aspect of the Policy is its advocacy for the review of legislation that do not adequately address needs of children with disabilities, including those with disabilities related to mental health. The Act, National Sports Council Act and Alcohol Policy it also pled for the enactment of specific legislation to protect youth in specific circumstances namely; child abuse/harassment/incest; harmful cultural practices; corporal punishment; drug, alcohol and substance abuse; early marriage; sexual exploitation; dropping out from primary and increased access to secondary school education; juvenile justice; and child trafficking.

### 3.2 Gender and Youth Sectoral and Climate Change Policies

#### 3.2.1 Women and Youth and National Climate Change Investment Plan (2013-2018)

63. Considering the adverse impacts of climate change on food security, water availability and quality, and energy that affect mostly rural community's livelihoods, the formulation of the National Climate Change Investment Plan (NCCIP) was initiated in 2014. The NCCIP is a set of planned plans of activities to provide the foundation and basis for implementing comprehensive and coordinated actions geared towards arresting climate change and its hazards.

64. The NCCIP identifies four main areas of focus: adaptation, mitigation, research and management that are adaptation; mitigation; climate change research, technology development and transfer; and capacity building. As shown in the table below, eleven programmes will be pursued to implement concrete actions within these priority areas.


| Priority area | Related Programmes |
| --- | --- |
| Adaptation | - Integrated Watershed Management Programme; <br> - Improving Climate Change Community Resilience through Agriculture Production; <br> - Climate Change Proofing of Infrastructure Development; <br> - Enhancing Disaster Risk Management |
| Mitigation | - Emission Reductions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+); <br> - Waste Management and Pollution Control Programme; <br> - Enabling Energy-Saving Technology Programme |


---
**Page 165**
---

Priority area | Related Programmes
---|---
Climate change research, technology development and transfer | Climate Change Adaptation Technology Development (CCATD); Multi-Country Climate Change Investment Plan; Climate Change Mitigation Technology Development (CCMTD); Adaptation and Mitigation Technology Transfer (AMTT) Capacity building | Capacity Development in Climate Change

65. The NCICP ensured that the key priority areas of the actions to address climate change and their effects are timely and sufficiently supported with resources in order to ensure that the economy is resilient, sustainable and inclusive, and that it contributes towards a safe, just and sustainable environment, safeguarded from major climate change effects, and with responsibility towards present and future generations. The NCICP was expected to guide the Government to allocate resources for the implementation of the actions on climate change in line with the contribution of the sector to the key priorities of the MGDS II. It also provided a framework for monitoring, reporting and evaluation (MRE) of progress made by the sector towards achieving its strategic priorities and targets. The Plan took stock of existing investment portfolios in climate change and assessed related challenges and gaps.

66. The NCICP was also generated among the challenges that limit the country’s response to the impacts of climate change. At the same time, social, cultural and gender issues are cited along with the plan's sustainability factors as the Plan recognized the importance of the participation of key stakeholders in the implementation of the actions on climate change at all levels of society. The NCICP also referred to the need to draw from the experience in mainstreaming HIV/AIDS and gender, through the allocation of a minimum percentage of budget to climate-related activities.

3.2.2 Women and Youth and the National Climate Change Management Policy (2016)

67. The Government of Malawi has taken measures to address climate change issues for signing the ratification of UNFCCC in its Kyoto protocol, reconciliation of the country's vulnerability to adverse effects of climate change. It developed the National Climate Change Management Policy (NCCMP) which is a policy document that provides guidelines on national climate change management which is to reduce the socioeconomic impacts of adverse effects of climatic change.

68. The Policy’s goal was to promote climate change adaptation, mitigation, technology transfer and capacity building for sustainable livelihoods through Green Economy measures for Malawi; and its medium-term outcome consisted of improved community resilience to climate change, increased productivity in sustainable livelihoods and reduced emissions of GHGs. In terms of objectives, the NCICMP aimed:

- Build and sustain the social and ecological resilience of all Malawians;
- Contribute to the stabilization of greenhouse gas concentrations in the atmosphere at a level that would prevent dangerous human-induced interference with the climate system within a timeframe that enables social, economic and environmental development to proceed without undue risk.


---
**Page 166**
---

- Integrate climate change into planning, development, coordination and monitoring of key relevant sectors in a gender-sensitive manner, and integrate cross-cutting issues on climate change management through an appropriate institutional framework.*

69. By articulating the goals and objectives for climate change management in Malawi, the Policy adopts as a principle for harmonizing and enhancing the planning, development, coordination, financing and monitoring of climate change initiatives and programmes in the country.

70. Gender equality is considered among the Policy’s guiding principles given that women and girls are disproportionately affected by climate change and are more vulnerable to its impacts. Gender equality must therefore be promoted as a response both in terms of mainstreaming as well as through specific interventions. The Policy recognizes that gender equality is an enabling development point out the will to address poverty reduction and equity. The notion of inter- generational equity, which is based on the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities and joint global efforts, is also considered one of the Policy’s guiding principles. Gender equality, intra-generational parity, and inter-generational equity relates the right of all people within the current generation to fair access Earth's natural resources. As the participation of vulnerable groups are stated as a principle in the Policy, the adverse effects of climate change on women and girls’ livelihoods and circumstances are also considered as one of the Policy’s guiding principles since the adverse effects of climate change are more pronounced for women and girls than men. Vulnerable groups such as women - especially poor and/or rural women, children - especially infants and child-headed families, and the aged - especially elderly people.

71. The NCCMP identified 6 Priority Areas namely (i) climate change adaptation; (ii) climate change mitigation; (iii) capacity building, education, training and awareness; (iv) research, technology development and transfer, and systematic observation; (v) climate finance change; and (vi) institutional strengthening. It is important to note that the identification of these areas may exacerbate vulnerability to climate change and variability or may undermine effectiveness of adaptation interventions. In addition, the Policy recognizes that there are other cross-cutting issues cutting Priority Areas, no specific mention on youth is made. The way the document is written makes it appear as though the Policy does not have a youth policy. However, the cross- cutting issue raised is population dynamics but more refers to population density, urbanization, reproductive health and family planning. Policy statements related to cross-cutting issues are as follows:

*Maximum gender and issues affecting the disadvantaged groups into all climate change strategies, plans and programmes.*

72. Besides, the first Priority Area on Climate Change Adaptation considered women and girls’ participation in the design and implementation of climate change adaptation interventions, and made it one of its 3 Policy Statements.

73. Key stakeholders for Policy implementation include: government, non-governmental organizations and civil society, the private sector, academia, development partners, local communities, faith-based organizations and domestic networking groups.

3.2.3 Women and Youth and Malawi’s National Adaptation Plan Framework (2020)

74. The national Adaptation Plan (NAP) Framework is intended to guide the country in advancing its NAP process. It constitutes the guiding structure of Malawi's efforts on the development, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the NAP. The framework aims to integrate climate change adaptation into relevant new and existing sectoral and national planning structures and processes. The framework was developed by the Ministry of Natural Resources, Energy and Mining Environmental Affairs Department in December 2014, with the aim to reduce vulnerability to the impacts of climate change by building adaptive capacity and resilience.

75. The NAP Framework builds on the NAP Roadmap, developed and adopted in 2016, and validates that Malawi is identified which is “A country with people, ecosystems and infrastructure that are resilient and have adaptive capacity to the impacts of climate change”. It also validates the 5 NAP Modules:

- Improve community resilience to climate change through enhanced agricultural production, infrastructure development and disaster risk management;
- Enhance sustainable utilization of natural resources especially forest, water, fisheries and wildlife resources;
- Improve environmental management especially soil and land management;
- Enhance capacity building for climate change adaptation;
- Provide climate change adaptation advocacy to policy-makers and other stakeholders with a view to mainstreaming climate change into policies by-laws on climate change as well as environmental and natural resource management.

76. The 4 building blocks for the NAP process are identified in the NAP Roadmap namely: national development strategies, capacity development, knowledge and information management and monitoring and evaluation.

77. The NAP Framework is designed to maintain the momentum of the NAP process in advance of receiving adaptation planning readiness funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF). It provides a strategic framework for the implementation of Malawi’s NAP and serves as a guiding framework for adaptation planning processes to be undertaken by government ministries and sub- national authorities, as well as for dialogue with non-governmental stakeholders.

78. The Framework considers gender in multiple ways. It is based on the NAP Stocktaking which provided an overview of the existing gender equality policies and strategies considered including “the inclusiveness of gender, disability and other socially excluded vulnerable groups” in the implementation of climate change adaptation interventions. The framework also takes into account for the medium- and long-term adaptation planning horizon of the NAP process. Besides the inclusion of gender in the formulation of Malawi’s NAP as per the National Gender Policy (2015), the Framework also emphasizes on the use of gender and human rights approach in the process of formulation and implementation of the NAP. This will be done by ensuring that women are involved at all levels of decision-making, including policy dialogue as well as their participation in program’s implementation at community level. The Malawi NAP also considers the role of women in climate change adaptation interventions under the National Adaptation Plan and ensure that women's voices are heard and taken into consideration in planning and implementation of the NAP. Gender inclusiveness including gender parity, women's

3.2.4 Women and Youth and the 3rd National Communication of the Republic of Malawi to the Conference of the Parties of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change

79. The Third National Communication (TNC) of Malawi was an improvement, follow-up and continuation of activities under the Initial National Communication (INC) and the Second National Communication (SNC) completed in 2003 and 2011 respectively. It aimed at:

- Enhancing the ability to impact on climate change issues through increased involvement of all stakeholders in the process.
- Enhancing general awareness and knowledge of climate change related issues in Malawi; and
- Contributing to the national dialogue on climate change policy and action, as well as helping in building national capacities for participation in climate change activities.

80. The TNC comprised a stocktaking exercise of the INC and SNC and set priorities for implementation in a manner that ensures effective allocation of resources. The communication focused on:

- Describing the national circumstances that are relevant to climate change;
- Conducting national greenhouse gas inventory in the areas of energy, agriculture, forestry and other land-use, industrial processes and product use, and waste management;
- Comparing Malawi’s national circumstances with those of other countries; reviewing various measures and strategies that can be used by vulnerable communities to adapt to the impacts of climate change on their livelihoods (e.g. agriculture, health, infrastructure, land resources, fisheries, forestry, tourism, industry, wildlife and gardens);
- Conducting mitigation analysis that provided information on measures and strategies for mitigating climate change in the areas of energy, agriculture, forestry and other land-use, industrial processes and product use, and waste management;
- Providing additional information that are considered relevant to the achievement of the objectives set out above; and
- Identifying constraints and gaps, and related financial, technical and capacity needs; and
- Proposing climate change projects.

34


---
**Page 169**
---

81. The Commission dealt with gender by considering the issue as one of the thematic areas of the VCA assessments. The related section provided some highlights on gender and its link to climate change, a description of the gender context in the country and the importance of gender when dealing with climate change issues, some elements on vulnerability in terms of gender, and some recommendations for action. The Commission also touched upon training, civic education and public awareness and climate financing. The same chapter on Vulnerability and Adaptation (VA) Assessments also touched upon women in the assessment related to human health and well-being.

3.2.5 Women and Youth and the Updated Nationally Determined Contributions (2021)

82. As part of its commitment to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Paris Agreement, Malawi submitted and communicated in 2015 its commitment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 37% below business as usual levels using the Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDC). The Government’s vision is that the country will be a low-carbon economy with reduced vulnerability to climate change. It aims to overcome challenges of climate change and is embracing the opportunities available to enable the country lay a solid foundation for a sustainable and prosperous Malawi in line with the Malawi Vision 2035. In order to achieve this, the Government has developed the NDC. Through the revision, the NDC outlines the country’s climate change priorities for the period from 2020- 2040 and provides concrete strategies for addressing the adverse effects of climate change through adaptation measures. Being prepared together with accompanying documents that facilitate its implementation, mainstreaming, monitoring and reporting including implementing a Mainstreaming Guidance Document, Gender Action Plan, and Resource Mobilization Strategy, the NDC provides concrete strategies for addressing the causes of climate change as well as to the adverse effects and impacts in line with provisions established under the Paris Agreement.

83. The updated NDC builds upon the NCMP in its approach to climate change through a multi-sectoral mitigation and adaptation response, as well as other key national guiding documents including Malawi 2030, the Malawi Economic Growth Strategy (MEGS), and the Malawi Growth and Development Strategies (MGDS). It also aligns with Malawi’s NAP Framework. The NDC is structured in three parts: Part I provides an overview of climate change among the sectors most affected by climate change. Treated with social protection, gender is therefore one of the key areas that are addressed under this part. Part II focuses on the identification, assessment, and prioritization of adaptation actions and measures. As a result from the process, the NDC has developed an Action Plan for Climate Change Adaptation (APCCA) that aims at promoting public health and social protection in the face of climate change. Treated with the issues of social support, the gender adaptation actions and measures’ focus are put on the mainstreaming of gender into all sectors of the economy. The NDC also calls for building programmes for vulnerable groups, civic education and public awareness are to be supported.

84. The focal point line ministry for the implementation of gender measures of the NDC is the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Welfare. The roles of NGOs/CSOs including gender networks are also high in this regard. In order to ensure that all adaptation climate change adaptation actions planned with a gender lens, and capable of tracking its progress and outcomes by gender are needed. The updated NDC has an improved Monitoring and Evaluation framework for the NDC which is gender-sensitive allowing to track the integration of gender and vulnerability across sectors for adaptation activities.

3.2.6 Women and Youth and the National Environmental Policy

85. The Government of Malawi adopted a National Environmental Policy in 1996 to provide guidance and set standards for development of sector policies in environment and natural resources management. In 2004, the Ministry of Environment and Tourism reviewed these policies and revised them to ensure that they were consistent with the principles of sustainable development. The review process was inclusive and participatory. However, as new sectoral policies and legislation in successive years revealed some policy gaps, conflicts and duplications that adversely affected the effective implementation of the policies; and which combined with the occurrence of natural disasters, droughts and floods, prompted the Government to revise the National Environmental Policy.

86. Responding to new challenges and increasingly important role played by the National Environment Policy in promoting the importance of (i) creating an enabling policy and legal framework for cross sector coordination, participation of non-state actors, (ii) strengthening the environmental governance structure, and (iii) ensuring effective implementation of environmental management and governance. Its overall goal consisted of promoting sustainable social and economic development through the sound management of the environment and natural resources in Malawi. The policy aimed at achieving this by addressing issues such as: policies management; preservation and restoration; enhancement of public education and awareness on various environmental issues; promotion of participatory approaches to the implementation of sustainable environment and natural resources management into the decentralized governance structure; promotion of local community, non-governmental Organisations (NGO) and private sector participation in the conservation and use of natural resources; development, promotion and application of local knowledge and norms; development and regular update of environmental information systems; strengthening of institutional capacity for environmental management as well as development of mechanisms for management of conflicts in the environment and natural resources.

87. Three of the Policy's guiding principles are oriented on children, youth, women and communities. These principles emphasized on:
- Children and youth awareness of environmental issues to facilitate their participation in the conservation, protection and management of the environment as future custodians;
- Women empowerment through provision of information and education for better participation to enhance their role in natural resource use and management activities; and
- Communities' involvement in decision making and planning from the sustainable utilization of natural resources on customary and public lands.

88. Among its 12 cross-sectoral policy objectives, six subjects are particularly relevant to the project seen from gender and social inclusion perspective: environmental and public awareness; private sector participation in conservation and management of forests and children's education; housing settlements and health; and land tenure and use. The Policy specified proper objective for each subject. For example, under the objective of promoting environmental and public awareness, it was stipulated that the policy should ensure that all Malawians are aware of their rights and responsibilities in relation to the environment. This objective included several areas covered by the project including agriculture and livestock, forestry and fisheries.

3.2.7 Women and Youth and National Land Resource Management Policy and Strategy (2000)

89. Malawi adopted the National Land Resources Management Policy and Strategy in 2000. Its overall goal was to promote efficient, diversified and sustainable use of land-based resources


---
**Page 171**
---

for both agriculture and other uses so as to avoid sectoral land-use conflicts and ensure socio-economic development. In terms of outcomes, the Policy was developed to assist in:

- *Balancing requirements for different land uses and regulating the competing demand for land by different users;
- Designating the best use of land for optimal socio-economic development;
- Providing guidelines and strategies for the sustainable utilization of the land resource base;
- Defining roles of different players in land resources management issues, guide the formulation and implementation of sectoral policies and strategies on land resources, its management; and
- Ensuring sustainability by giving the priority application of both internal and externally provided resources.*

90. The Policy continues among its guiding principle the full involvement of community based organizations and local communities, the equitable and secure tenure over land and resources for users as a fundamental requirement for promoting the adoption of sustainable natural resource management; the inclusion of women and youth in policy, programmes and project implementation; and the consideration of environmental, social and economic threats the carrying capacity of environment, natural resources and the economy; among others.

91. The policy outlined 16 specific objectives in different areas including women and gender. The objective is to ensure that women and men have equal access to land and other resources so that men and women as equal partners have shared responsibility over sustainable use and management of land and forest resources, with the provision for the utilization of positive traditional knowledge of women in sustainable land use and management; development and implementation of policies that recognize and enhance the legal rights of women to land on an equitable basis; and the protection of the rights of children especially women and children, in all programmes addressing land use and management issues.

92. The specific objective on institutional capacity building also highlighted among its strategies is empowerment of rural community to allow these latter effectively manage their land resources.

### 3.2.8 Women and Youth and the National Forest Policy (2016)

93. The National Forest Policy of 2016 took over from the National Forest Policy of 1996 and the National Forest Programme formulated in 2000 to guide the implementation of the 1996 Policy, which aimed at promoting sustainable use and management of forests and forest resources, offering an enabling framework for all stakeholders participating in the management of forests; sustaining the contribution of the national forest resources for the upliftment of the quality of life.

94. *The goal of the National Forest Policy is to improve provision of forest goods and services to contribute towards sustainable development of Malawi through protection and conservation of forest resources, promotion of sustainable use of forest resources and enhancement of strategies that contribute to increased forest cover by 2% from the current 28% by 2021 as well as to sustainable management of existing forest resources. The overall objectives of the Policy are to:*

- *Provide an enabling framework for promoting the participation of local communities, the civil society and the private sector in forest conservation and management;*


---
**Page 172**
---

- Promote the establishment of forest based small and medium scale industries;
- Promote the growth of trees by all sections of the communities in order to achieve sustainable self-sufficiency of wood for local derived products and services;
- Promote sustainable management of forests for the protection of the environment, conservation of biodiversity and enhancement of ecosystem services;
- Promote the development of initiatives for adequate and sustainable short, medium and long term forest resource utilization by all sections of the communities;
- Enhance the development of requisite human resource commensurate with the implementation of the policy;
- Promote scientific management and utilization of forest resources based on research findings and recommendations;
- Promote the establishment and monitored and monitored forest sector with clearly defined forest standards and guidelines; and
- Enhance cooperation in forestry related issues at national, regional and international levels through collaboration and partnership within and outside the forest sector.

95. To address sustainable forest management, the Government identified 10 Priority Areas namely: (i) Community Based Forest Management; (ii) Indigenous Forests, Forest Reserves, and Ecosystems; (iii) National Forest Policy; (iv) Forest Extension and Education; (v) Priority Regulation and Quality Control; (vi) Forestry Knowledge Acquisition and Management; (vi) Capacity Building for the Forest Sector; (vii) Promotion of the Development and Establishment of Forest Based Industries; (ix) Regional and International Cooperation; and (x) Financing Mechanisms. Sustainable Forest Management will ensure continuous provision of forest goods and services to Malawians.

96. While the only priority area that emphasized on the participation of local communities was the first one related to Community Based Forest Management, the involvement of women or youth was not specifically mentioned in any other priority areas except for capacity development for the forest sector also related the needs to develop the human resources required for the sector but this mention remained vague. Although the Policy was well developed from a technical point of view, insufficient provisions were made from a gender and youth perspective.

3.2.9 Women and Youth and the Malawi National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (2015- 2025)

97. The National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan II (NBSAP II) is a framework for action that will guide the country in achieving its long-term vision of sustainable use and conservation status of the biological resources in Malawi and provides strategies, targets and actions to be taken to ensure their sustainable management. The strategy is in line with the Malawi Growth and Development Strategy (MGDS), which seeks to integrate biodiversity management programs among other socio-economic and environmental issues.

98. The goal of the strategy is to enhance the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity for environmental, social and economic benefits. This goal will be achieved through the attainment of five specific objective targets that rely on 16 targets and a set of actions:

- Improved capacity and knowledge on biodiversity issues;
- Increased mainstreaming of biodiversity management into sectoral and local development planning;
- Reduced direct pressures on biodiversity;


---
**Page 173**
---

- Improved status of biodiversity by safeguarding ecosystems, species and genetic diversity;
- Enhancing access and benefit sharing from biodiversity and ecosystem services.

99. The strategy refers to gender roles and responsibilities of the youth in the strategic objectives on the enhancement of access and benefit-sharing from biodiversity and ecosystem services. Recognizing 15 years as a critical period for the empowerment of women, men, the youth, and women in biodiversity management and in ensuring that these groups benefit fully from the ecosystem services that biodiversity provides.

### 3.2.10 Women and Youth and the National Agriculture Policy (2016)

100. The National Agriculture Policy 2016 sought to sustainably transform the agriculture sector from subsistence farming towards commercialization, increase agricultural production, marketed surpluses of commodities, and real incomes. Since the principal responsibility of agriculture is to produce food for Malawians, the policy aimed at increasing agricultural productivity to increase agricultural incomes; the vision of the NAP to ensuring food security and nutrition is based on a more coordinated approach between smallholder farmers and commercialization of agricultural products. A particular focus had also been put on smallholder farming as an important strategy in order to optimize resources that go to the smallholder subsector, although the policy recognized all types of enterprises and their contribution towards increasing productivity and incomes from their production. Poor-poor linkages between large-scale estates and smallholder farmers were also promoted through the establishment of a National Farmers’ Federation (NFF) which was also available in the sector, the Government may need to develop accompanying strategies that enable many Malawians to leave the agricultural sector and move into remunerative non-agricultural activities. The NAP 2016 was based on the principle of inclusiveness and coordinated partnerships, and relied on the key consideration being about the place of smallholder farming in the development pathway of agricultural transformation.

101. Standing as a clear and comprehensive policy guidance for the agricultural sector, the Policy aimed at increasing productivity, incomes, and competitiveness of Malawi’s agricultural development and to the economy as a whole by farmers, processors, and traders. Its main operational rationale was to improve coherence across the wide range of agricultural sub-sector policies and programs.

102. The Policy identified eight priority areas that are: (i) Sustainable Agricultural Production and Productivity; (ii) Sustainable Irrigation Development; (iii) Mechanization of Agriculture; (iv) Agricultural Inputs and Services; (v) Food Security and Nutrition; (vi) Improved Access to Nutrition Security; (vii) Agricultural Risk Management; (viii) Empowerment of Youth, Women and Vulnerable Groups in Agriculture and HIV/AIDS. The development, Coordination and Capacity Strengthening within which Policy Area 1 on Sustainable Agricultural Production and Productivity raised women and youth’s inadequate access, control, and utilization of productive assets, including land, inputs, credit, extension services, markets, information, technology, agro-processing and Value Addition made provisions on the promotion of women and youth's access to training, education, and skills development. The Policy Area 2 on Empowerment of Youth, Women and Vulnerable Groups in Agriculture emphasized on the high involvement of women and youth in the agricultural sector, as well as their inability to effectively contribute to the agriculture sector due to inadequate access to land, inputs, credit, extension services and productive assets in the sector. The Policy statements and related objectives are as follows:

## 3.2.11 Women and Youth and the National Agricultural Investment Plan (2017/18–2022/23)

### 104. The National Agricultural Investment Plan (NAIP) is a medium-term investment framework for the agriculture sector covering a five-year period. Aligned to the Comprehensive Africa Agriculture Development Program (CAADP), the African Union Malawi Declaration on Accelerated Agricultural Growth and Rural Transformation, the United Nations Millennium Development Goals, the national development policy blue print, the Malawi Growth and Development Strategy (MIGS III), it built its achievements and lessons from its predecessor, the Agriculture Sector Investment Plan (ASIP). The NAIP is a multi-sectoral plan that covers the next five years as the main implementation vehicle for the 2016 National Agriculture Policy (NAP), and provides a framework for the development of sector strategies, plans and budgets. It involves development partners, non-state actors (civil society, farmer organizations and the private sector).

### 105. The goal of the NAIP consists of sustainable agricultural transformation that will result in significant increase of the agricultural output, expanding markets for farm households, improved food and nutrition security for all Malawians, increased agricultural productivity, and three mutually reinforcing impact-level objectives that are: (i) broad-based and resilient agricultural growth; (ii) improved well-being and livelihoods of Malawians; and (iii) improved food and nutrition security.

### 106. Estimated at $3.219 billion US$, the NAIP is structured around four programs and 16 Intervention areas (IA). The four programs and related objectives are summarized in the below table.


---
**Page 175**
---

Program

Objective

A. Policies, institutions and coordination To improve policy and regulatory environment, stakeholder cooperation and accountability

B. Resilient livelihoods and agricultural systems To strengthen resilience of livelihoods and natural resource base for agriculture

C. Production and productivity To increase production and productivity of a more diversified agricultural sector

D. Markets, value addition, trade and financial services To enhance market access, value addition, trade, and access to finance


107. Calling across the four programs, the following 16 (A) are needed to achieve the objectives of the NAIP: (i) Policy, Program and Stakeholder Coordination; (ii) Farmer Organisations; (iii) Public agricultural services delivery; (iv) Food and Nutrition Security; (v) Food safety and quality assurance; (vi) Agricultural research services; (vii) Risk management; (viii) Pest and disease management; (ix) Agribusiness innovations systems; (x) Access to inputs; (xi) Natural resource management and Climate Change; (xii) Irrigation development and water resources management; (xiii) Markets and trade; (xiv) Investments in agribusiness; (xv) Access to financial services.

108. With regards to gender, the description of the country and sector context gives visibility to women's roles as primary producers and their contribution towards household food security. The NAIP also recognizes through its second impact-level objective on improved well-being and livelihoods, the heterogeneous nature of rural communities and the importance of tailoring specific measures for women and youth in agriculture. The NAIP further emphasizes the importance in agriculture, supporting youth employment and entrepreneurship; support labour-constrained households with access to agricultural inputs and services; and the inclusion of women and youth as an additional strategic consideration that should be mainstreamed in all parts of the NAIP given their critical role in food security and rural development. The NAIP also recognizes the challenges both women and youth in rural communities experienced. The NAIP various interventions are therefore expected to purposefully target the participation of at least 30 percent of women and the occupation of at least 25 percent of youth in all activities.

109. Under the IA on farmer organizations, the output contributing to Program A on addressing capacity gaps in gender related aspects; and output contributing to Program B on the establishment of a national network for women's agricultural cooperatives, including women youth, and their capacity building, would support the outcome on strengthening the performance and outreach of farmer organisations at all levels.

110. The NAIP's impact-level objective towards security clearly identified a gender outcome on "women and youth empowered and land tenure security enhanced" and a gender intermediate outcome on "increased participation of women and youth in agricultural value chains and institutions". These outcomes are expected to be achieved through the following outputs: (i) output A; (ii) Government staff trained on gender, and (iii) update of agricultural curriculum on gender. The NAIP also recognizes that the following outputs would contribute towards this impact: (iv) women and youth groups strengthened and empowered in relation to land tenure security; and Program D; (v) agribusiness SMEs owned/operated by women and youth trained on various technical and business aspects, and (vi) youth and women agribusiness entrepreneurs receiving business mentorship.


---
**Page 176**
---

112. Some examples of gender and youth mainstreaming measures to encourage participation of women and youth in agriculture include: review of existing policies and approaches, review of the agricultural sector gender and HIV strategy, training for Government staff in gender and youth issues, gender analysis of projects or an performance targets and indicators, targets for gender and youth participation in training.

3.2.12 Women and Youth and the Agriculture sector. Gender, HIV and AIDS strategy (2012- 2017)

113. The Agriculture Sector, Gender, HIV and AIDS strategy 2012-2017 took over from the first strategy implemented between 2003 and 2008 and 2009. Building on the gaps and lessons identified from the evaluation of the strategy in 2009, the Ministry in collaboration with various stakeholders developed the Strategy to guide the implementation of gender, HIV and AIDS mainstreaming measures.

114. The empowerment of women was at the core of the strategy goal which consist of "contributing to sustainable and equitable food, nutrition and income security at national, communal and household levels through the promotion of gender equality among different gender categories". The purpose of the strategy is "to promote gender equality, prevent the spread of HIV and AIDS, reduce poverty and improve agricultural productivity in line with ASWAQ priorities". The Strategy has 3 pillars namely: i) Promote gender equality for other vulnerable gender categories in ASWAQ focus areas and key support services; ii) Gender, HIV and AIDS mainstreaming measures to encourage participation of women and youth in agriculture, capacity building and resource mobilization. Specific objectives of the Strategy included:

- To improve food and nutrition security and agro-based income among vulnerable groups, in order to reduce gender disparities and contribute to HIV prevention and mitigation of impacts;
- To strengthen women's access to and control over agriculture resources, opportunities, benefits and decision-making power within their households;
- To reduce factors that promote gender inequality and spread of HIV due to agricultural related activities;
- To promote generation and dissemination of gender, HIV and AIDS responsive advisory services;
- To strengthen leadership and coordination of the Gender, HIV and AIDS Strategy for harmonized and decentralized implementation;
- To ensure adequate access to information, financial and technical resources for the implementation of Gender, HIV and AIDS Strategy;
- To enhance the effectiveness and efficiency of agriculture sector monitoring and evaluation system for enhanced accountability, learning and sharing;
- To build and sustain capacity of agriculture sector institutions and vulnerable groups to effectively implement gender, HIV and AIDS mainstreaming measures.

115. In terms of implementation, the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Security was in charge of providing policy guidance in the implementation of the strategy through the Executive Management Team. The National Gender and HIV/AIDS Secretariat was responsible for Gender, HIV and AIDS was responsible of technically guiding the implementation of the strategy. At district


---
**Page 177**
---

level, the strategy was implemented through the District Agriculture Extension Services System with collective efforts of all stakeholders.

116. Various groups were identified as focus of the strategy implementation: Women, Female Headed Households, Orphans and other Vulnerable Children, Child Headed Households, People with Disabilities, People Living with HIV, Mobile and Migrant Workers and the Elderly. Although the Strategy is gender-sensitive, it does not specifically target women and girls. The groups include young women. The reference the Policy made to Children may also consider Youth since many Acts consider people under the age of 18 as children.

### Gender and Youth

#### 4.1 Basics Statistics

##### 4.1.1 Gender Parity

117. With a Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.483 in 2019, the Human Development Report ranked Malawi 176th out of 189 countries and territories and classified the country among the ones in the low human development category. The HDI is based on a person’s expected years of life (1959–2019 with an average of 8.2 years of life expectancy at birth) and an increase of means of schooling and expected years of schooling of 2.3 years and 5.8 years respectively. The Gross National Income (GNI) per capita decreased from $476 in 2018 to $459 in 2019, which is a decrease of 4%. The GNI per capita average for countries in the low human development group and for countries in Sub-Saharan Africa, which are 0.531 and 0.51 respectively.

118. When it comes to inequality-adjusted HDI (IHDI) - an indicator that takes into account inequality in the three dimensions of HDI by “discounting” each dimension’s average value according to its level of inequality, Malawi’s HDI stood at 0.365 which represents a loss of 28.6 percentage points from the HDI (41% for inequality). The Maternal Human Inequality coefficient of 0.28.5 percent appears to be lower than the average of 37.9 and is 13.4 percent for low HDI countries and end 26.5 percent for Sub-Saharan Africa.

119. In 2019, the value of the UNDP’s Gender Development Index (GDI) - an indicator that considers gender parity in education, health, and economic resources - was 0.483 with a Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.986 for Malawi with the HDI value of 0.493 for females and 0.500 for males. The GDI value of 0.986 placed the country into Group 1, a category that includes countries with absolute deviation from gender parity in education, health, and economic resources.

## 4.1.2 Population

Malawi’s population increased from 3.6 million in 1960 to 19.6 million in 2021[2], and women account for 50.7 percent[3]. The 2018 Malawi population and housing census reports (2018 PHS) show that the total population of Malawi is 19,490,000 as compared to the 1966 census of 3,665,000. With an intercensal growth rate of 2.9 percent per annum, the total population increased by 35 percent between the 2008 and 2018. When the population is divided into urban and rural areas, it shows that in urban areas, at 94.2 at national level, four cities were identified with more males than females namely Lilongwe, Blantyre, Zomba, and Phalombe. The Central region had the highest proportion of males (51 percent) of the total population, followed by the Central and Northern Regions which had 43 percent and 13 percent respectively. In rural areas, the Southern Region had the highest proportion of females at 97.8 percent while the Eastern Region had a female proportion of 60.2 percent.

In urban areas, 12 percent of the total population lived in the four major cities of Blantyre, Lilongwe, Zomba and Phalombe, and 4 percent resided in other towns and Bomas. The population of the ESAM targeted districts are shown in Table 8.

### 4.1.3 Literacy and education

#### 126. Literacy
The Education report of the 2018 PHC described literacy as the ability to read and write in any language. Malawi's literacy rate for persons aged 5 years and above was 68.6 percent in 2018. Literacy levels showed an effective improvement based on the increase from 57.3 percent recorded in the 2008 PHC, 49.7 percentage point difference compared to the rate of 63.9 percent recorded in the 2017 PHC, and a 4.7 percentage difference compared to the rate of 63.9 percent recorded in the 2008 PHC. The literacy rate for females increased more than that of males. The rates for males increased from 58.7 percent in 2008 to 61.9 percent in 2018 (4.2 percentage point difference) whereas the rates for females increased from 58.7 percent in 2008 to 65.9 percent in 2018 (7.2 point difference). The female literacy rate improved more than that of males between 2008 and 2018 considering the 10.7 percentage point difference in 2008 and the 5.7 percentage point difference in 2018. The tremendous improvement in female literacy rate by 2018 could be attributed to the government's efforts towards gender equality[9] [10] [11] [12] [13] [UNICEF 2017].

#### In terms of age distribution, the national literacy level for the population in the country tended to decrease with the increase of age, regardless of sex. The literacy rate for 55 years and over is the lowest at 24.1 percent, then for the age group of 5 to 9 years old at 27.9 percent. Besides, there was a significant difference in male and female literacy rates with the proportion of literate women decreasing with age, and the trend for men being not as steep as for women.

122. School attendance is a function of age, level of education, and years in primary school; 4 years of secondary school and another 4 years of university-level education. The official school going ages are 6 to 13 years for primary school level, 14 to 17 years for secondary school level and 18 to 24 years for tertiary level.

130. The 2018 PHC observed school attendance for population aged 5-24 years old since some learners enter school at the younger age of 5 years. The school age population was estimated using the 2018 Malawi Population and Housing Census (PHC) data. Of the total school age population, 9.5 percent were secondary school age population and 14.6 were post-secondary school age population. No major differences in distributions of these categories were noted by sex.

131. In terms of school attendance, the 3-5 year old children attending preschool represented 29.7 percent in 2018. More children in the urban areas (47.3 percent) attended than in rural areas (27.1 percent). The Central Region had the highest proportion of population who were not currently attending pre-school, followed by the Southern Region (32.9 percent) and the Central Region (25.1 percent). A higher proportion of children aged 6-8 years old were currently attending school than those who never attended school was observed in the 5-9 age group (22.7 percent) compared to the other age groups. The population of those who have ever attended school was highest for youth aged 20-24 years and lowest among children aged 6-8 years old, followed by young adults aged 15-19 year age group (Figure 2). The proportion of population currently attending school increased from 27.9 percent in 2000 to 38.8 percent in 2018 and decreased again to 36.4 percent in 2018. Those who never attended school both decreased from 50.5 percent in 2000 to 46.8 percent in 2018 and from 21.6 percent in 2000 to 17.9 percent in 2018. The Central Region had the highest proportion of population that never attended school (30.5 percent), whereas the Northern region had the highest proportion of those who never attended (19.7 percent), whereas the Northern region had the highest proportion of those who ever attended (53.3 percent) and those currently attending school (48.2 percent). The Central Region had the lowest proportion that never attended, and there are more males than females among those who had ever attended and currently attending school. Those households headed by single women have the highest proportion of people (49.3 percent) who never attended school were traditional believers.

In 2018, primary school enrolment by age groups revealed that 72.6 percent of children (74.1 percent were female and 71.1 percent male) fell under the appropriate primary school age range of five to nine years old. The proportion of children aged ten to fourteen years was 93.6 percent (5.1 percent female and 4.5 percent male), and 22.7 percent (20.9 percent female and 24.5 percent male) were fifteen to nineteen years old. The proportion of twenty-year-olds was 74.3 percent or slightly above between 2008 and 2018, where some cases showed more girls in reflection in the population gender split in the country. The presence of under-aged and over-aged learners was reflected by the fact that the proportion of children aged five to nine years old was 72.6 percent or slightly above between 2008 and 2018, where some cases showed more girls in reflection in the population gender split in the country.

The presence of under-aged and over-aged learners was reflected by the fact that the proportion of children enrolled in primary school expressed as a percentage of the total number of children aged five to nine years old. The proportion for females was 78.6 percent, while it was 71.9 percent for males. As for the national primary school Net enrolment rate (NER) that measures the proportion of children who are enrolled in primary school as a percentage of all children of primary school age group, the NER for five to nine years old was 80.3 percent.

The proportion of children aged ten to fourteen years old was 93.6 percent or slightly above between 2008 and 2018, where some cases showed more girls in reflection in the population gender split in the country. The presence of under-aged and over-aged learners was reflected by the fact that the proportion of children aged ten to fourteen years old expressed as a percentage of the total number of children in the same age group, the rate for 77.8 percent (79.8 percent for females and 76.8 percent for males) showed that some children of official primary school age are not accommodated by the system.

The proportion of children aged fifteen to nineteen years old was 22.7 percent or slightly decreased from 11.7 percent in 2009 to 3.2 percent in 2018, retention remains a challenge. Primary completion rate for those who started primary school at age five and completed primary education is 45.6 percent.

In addition, since it takes thirteen years on average to graduate from the eight-year primary school program, the high representation of children aged twenty-four years old eventually led to drop-out and low completion rate that increased out-of-school children.

133. Secondary school enrolment depend on primary school education completed and transition rates. In 2018, transition rate from primary to secondary school was at 38.3 percent. Secondary school completion rate for those who started secondary school at age fifteen years old was 40.2 percent, and it was 3.8 percent for young people less than fourteen years old and five percent for those aged ten years old or more. ### 4.1.4 General health

136\. Malawi's total life expectancy at birth has continuously improved from 45 years in 2000 to 65 years in 2020[2]. In 2020, female life expectancy at birth estimated at 68 exceeds that of men by 6 years. Adult mortality rates have also decreased since 2002, with a reduction of more than half between 2002 and 2017. The country's adult mortality rate is lower compared to the situation in Malawi in 2000 is worse than the world average. Life expectancy at birth of Malawians and most other African countries is lower than that for the world population, while male adult mortality rate is worse than those of sub-Saharan Africa and the world. In 2000, the causes of death in Malawi followed the same trends as in Sub-Saharan Africa: 50 percent of the deaths were due to communicable diseases and maternal, prenatal and nutrition conditions; 40 percent

### HIV/AIDS and related knowledge and behaviour

139. Although Malawi has made significant progress in reducing the number of HIV infection over the last decade, the annual number of adults aged 15–49 newly infected with HIV falling from 39 000 in 2010 to 17 000 in 2020. HIV/AIDS remains the number one killer in the country. In 2018, the prevalence among adults was estimated at 6.5 percent (95% CI: 4.3–8.7). This is higher than the 4.9 higher percentage point compared with the Sub-Saharan African average of 3.6%. The HIV prevalence in Malawi has been consistently above the national average since the first three successive Malawi demographic health surveys (MDHS) of 2004, 2010 and 2015–16 with the rates of 11.8 percent, 10.6 percent and 8.8 percent respectively. The 2015–16 MDHS described the prevalence among adults as being highest in the Central region (9.4%) followed by the North (7.3%).

The prevalence for young women aged 15 to 24 is almost double that for young men (3.7 versus 1.7 percent). The prevalence of HIV infection was higher among those who were married or in a union compared with those who were single, separated or divorced. The prevalence rose aged 15–19 to 8 percent in men aged 20–34, with the highest prevalence of 20.5 percent among men age 40–44. The sexual debut is occurring later for women: age group 15–19 (no large group) of 40–44 with a pattern ranging from 3.3 to 18.8 percent. The prevalence was highest in urban areas among those aged 45 or 49 (16.9 percent). HIV prevalence was twice as high in urban areas compared to rural areas, and the prevalence of HIV infection is higher for women than men.

The prevalence of HIV infection varies by region with a higher prevalence in the North and Central regions where the prevalence is 8.2 and 7.3 percent respectively. The prevalence among adults aged 15–49 was highest in urban areas at 9.6 percent compared to rural areas (6.0 percent). In adjusted analysis, this percentage is reduced by more than half for the Central and Northern regions where the prevalence is 5.6 and 5.1 percent respectively. Women in the highest wealth quintile have higher HIV prevalence than women in other quintiles.

HIV prevalence is also influenced by marital status with a higher prevalence among men and women who have been married or in union compared to those who are single, separated or divorced. The HIV prevalence was estimated at 3 percent. For women and men reporting ever being married, it was 9.8 for those who were married or in union (10.4 versus 7.2). The prevalence of HIV infection is lower for those who were divorced or separated and 32.3 percent of widowed women and men. Those in polygamous union also had higher HIV prevalence. While HIV prevalence is higher among women who have been married, the risk of acquiring HIV is highest among women aged 15–19 (10.4 percent for women who had their first sexual intercourse at age 16 or 17; 9.9 percent for those with a first sexual intercourse before age 13). The risk was also higher for women and men for those who initiated sexual activity before age 18 than for those who had their first sex at older ages (5.4 versus 2.0).

The prevalence of HIV infection is highest among women aged 20–29 years old (16.7 percent) and lowest in the 30–39 year-old group (11.8 percent). Women and men with a Sexually transmitted infection (STI) or symptoms of an STI had a higher HIV prevalence than those who did not (14.8 versus 9 percent).

The estimated number of people living with HIV in Malawi was 705,000 in 2016 and were estimated at 170,000 in 2020, of which 73,000 concerned young people aged 15–24. HIV and AIDS orphans are estimated to be around 89,000.

### With respect to HIV/AIDS related knowledge and attitudes, about three-quarters of women and men aged 15-49 know that using condoms consistently can reduce the risk of HIV. More than eight in ten women and seven in ten men used condoms and more than five percent of men aged 15–49 know that limiting sexual partners is one way to reduce their risk of HIV infection. The higher education men and women have, the more informed on prevention methods they are. Women with secondary or tertiary education were more likely to report using condoms and limiting sexual intercourse to one uninfected partner as a way to avoid getting HIV compared with 74 percent of women and 70 percent of men with more than secondary education.


---
**Page 187**
---

The percentage of young people aged 14-24 with comprehensive knowledge of HIV appears low compared to the average in the 15-49 age group, and it was slightly higher for men (44 percent) than for women (38 percent), with no significant change noted between the two M DHSs of 2010 and 2015-16. Young women and men in urban areas are more likely than their counterparts in rural areas to have comprehensive knowledge of HIV. The proportion of young people who had sex with five or more partners was highest among those who had more than one partner (52 percent) compared to their female counterparts (36 percent).

The 2015-16 M DHS found that over 9 percent of women and 14 percent of men have discriminated against someone living with HIV. This discrimination can achieve people's willingness to be tested and treated if they are tested positive. Discriminatory attitudes decrease among young people, especially those who have been tested for HIV.

Most of the women (86 percent) knew where to obtain an HIV test, while only 70 percent of men ever had been tested for HIV (83 percent versus 70 percent). A substantial increase in HIV testing among young people was noted from 2014-15 to 2015-16. The highest HIV testing coverage were observed in the Northern region of Ethiopia, where 90 percent of women and 80 percent of men aged 15–24 had been tested (42 percent). HIV testing coverage among young people aged 15-24 who had sex in the past 12 months before the survey were 53 percent for women and 45 percent for men.

### Reproductive Health

1. The M DHS and Health Survey (M DHS) reported that 69 percent of women and 70 percent of men aged 15-49 were married or living together with a partner as they were married during the survey period. While the percentage of women married or living together with their partners increased from 2008 to 2015, the decrease for men between 1992 and 2010 was very significant: ranging 74 to 57 percent but no significant change noted in 2015-16. The median age at first marriage for women aged 25–49 is 18.2 years, while it is at 23 years for men within the same age groups. Between the M DHS of 1992 and 2015-16, the median age at first marriage for women increased by 7.2 years (from 10 to 17.4) but decreased by 8.2 years (from 31.2 to 23) for men. The highest increase in the median age of first marriage was observed for men from 2008-09 to 2015-16.

12. Thirteen percent of women reported to be in a polygamous marriage and the percentage of men who were in such marriages is higher than that of women (7 percent). Women living in rural areas are more likely to report cowives (14 percent) compared with their counterparts living in urban areas (8 percent).

13. The proportion of young women reporting polygamy (18 percent) followed by the Central (14 percent) and the Southern (11 percent) regions. Less educated women are more likely to have co-wives. 21 percent of women with no education were in such marriages, while other wives compared with only 2 percent of women with more than secondary education.


---
**Page 188**
---

143. Comparison of median ages at first sex and first marriage showed that in general both women and men in Malawi have sex before marriage. On average, men initiate sexual intercourse at slightly older ages than women; the median age for sex was for women and men aged 25–49 were 16.8 and 18.5 years respectively and it remained unchanged between 2000 and 2015-16. Risk of early sexual debut is higher among women in rural areas compared to urban areas (17.6 years). The percentages of women aged 25–49 who had first sex at earlier age were higher than for men, with the highest percentage being observed among women aged 20–24 years old before age 18 compared to 11 percent and 42 percent for their male counterparts. By age 20, 85 percent of women and 68 percent of men had experienced sexual intercourse. Since the median age at first sex was 17.3 years in 2000, it is clear that a large proportion of young people are sexually active before marriage. Among women who were married for less than five years (median age at first marriage: 16.8 years versus 18.2 years), many women engage in sex before marriage and may be at risk of unintended pregnancy or STIs. The median age at first sex was 17.3 years among those who had never been married.

The median ages at first sex and first marriage for men aged 25–49 (18.5 years versus 23 years) show that they are older than women in the same age group. This is consistent with the fact that sexual intercourse increases with level of education and household wealth both for men and women: there is a gap of 3.7 years and a gap of 1.8 years in the median age of first sex between women aged 25–49 who are in the lowest quintile (0.6 children per woman) compared to those with the highest quintile (2.9 children). Similarly, there is a gap of 3.7 years and a gap of 1.8 years in the median age at first marriage between men aged 25–54 with the same characteristics. Median age for first sex for women aged 25–49 in the lowest quintile (0.6 children per woman) is one year younger than those in the highest quintile.

For men aged 25–54, the median age at first sex in the lowest quintile is 1.1 years younger than in the highest wealth quintile (18.2 years versus 19.3 years).

144. Total fertility rate (TFR) in Malawi declined markedly from 6.7 in 1992 to 4.4 children per woman in 2015–16, a decline of about one-third over the period. The TFR is higher than the sub-Saharan Africa average of 4.8 in 2020[3]. Women in rural areas have higher fertility, on average, than women living in urban areas (TFR: 4.9 versus 3.7). This is consistent with the fact that there are more children per woman. Women with no education had an average of 5.5 children compared to those who completed secondary or higher education having only one child. In areas with the level of poverty, women in the lowest wealth quintile have an average of 2.8 more children than women in the highest wealth quintile (TFR of 5.7 versus 2.9 children).

145. Median age at first birth in Malawi had changed little over the last two decades and it was 19.3 years for women aged 20–49 in 2015-16 compared to 18.9 years for women aged 25–49 in 2015-16. Women aged 25–49 in urban areas had had their first birth, on average, 1 year later than women in rural areas (13.9 years versus 12.9 years). The median age at first marriage was 18.7 for women with women, a difference of 6.3 years were observed among women aged 25–49 with no education and those with more than secondary education (median age at first birth: 18.6 and 24.9 years respectively).

146. With regards to contraception, the MDHS 2015-16 reported that contraceptive knowledge is almost universal in Malawi. 98 percent of women and nearly 100 percent of men aged 15–49 know at least one method of family planning. The most commonly known methods are condoms (73 percent) followed by injectables (25 percent). The percentage of women between 15 and 49 years old practicing or whose sexual partners are practicing contraception was 60 percent in 2015–16, a slight increase from the previous survey. This age was at 44 percent for sexually active unmarried women. Nearly all married women and sexually active unmarried women using contraception used a modern method[3], 58 and 43 percent respectively. The use of modern contraceptives by married women showed a significant increase

### Teenage pregnancy

147. Teenage pregnancy and child marriage are highly correlated in Malawi. Girls who marry at early age are more likely to become pregnant, and those who become pregnant are often forced to marry. In 2015–16, the proportion of women aged 15–19 who had their first birth or were pregnant with their first child was estimated at 29 percent in 2015–16. The proportion of women who started childbearing in rural areas of 31 percent exceeded by one percentage point the proportion of those who started childbearing in urban areas (28 percent). This is consistent with the percentage of women aged 15-19 who have begun childbearing decreasing from 44 percent among women born before 1970 to 26 percent for women born after 1990. Among women and men aged 15–19, 3 percent of women and just 2 percent of men had their first sexual intercourse before age 15. One percent of women and no men in the same age group gave birth within two years of marriage.

The teenage years are a critical period in a woman’s life when she makes choices that will have long-term implications for her future well-being and productivity. Decisions such as whether and when to marry, how many children to have, and what kind of education to pursue can affect labor market outcomes later in life – an aspect that has been confirmed by the young ladies members of a fish processing group[16]. Women who begin childbearing at a young age are likely to have more children over their lifetime. Those who become pregnant before they are 20 years old, as a result, have worse economic outcomes.[17] Low awareness among girls regarding harmful social norms, early marriage and related issues contributes to increased teenage pregnancy.

## e. Maternal, neonatal and child health

150. In Malawi, the maternal mortality ratio indicating the number of women who die from pregnancy-related causes while pregnant or within one year of pregnancy termination per 100,000 live births has decreased significantly—from more than 749 in 2006 to 349 in 2017. Although the ratio of 349 deaths per 100,000 live births is still considered as high, it remains very low compared to that of other countries in Sub-Saharan Africa and the world.

The neonatal mortality rate (under one year old) has decreased from more than those in the world but stayed far behind the average rates in Sub-Saharan Africa. The causes of neonatal mortality (a 19 per 1,000 live births in 2020) include stillbirths, premature birth, and infection; and the under-5 mortality estimated at 58 per 1,000 births are due to malnutrition, diarrhea, and pneumonia.

areas (77 deaths per 1000 births versus 83 deaths per 1000 live births in 2015–16) and it declines with increasing level of the mother’s education and household wealth[9]. Most children in Malawi are being vaccinated with 76 percent of children aged 12-23 months reported to have received all basic vaccinations by the 2015-16 MDHS and 70 percent having received the basic vaccination series at birth. The most common causes of death among children under five years old were acute lower respiratory infection (ALRI) (48 percent), malaria (29 percent), and diarrhea (23 percent). Other common illness affecting children under the age of 5: 29 percent had fever, 22 percent had a diarrheal episode in the past two weeks, and 17 percent had an acute lower respiratory infection before the survey. Children with ALRI symptoms were most often taken to a health facility or provider for advice or treatment (78 percent) compared to children with fever (67 percent) or diarrhea (66 percent).

### 153.
With respect to nutrition, the 2015-16 MDHS reported that 37 percent of children under age 5 are stunted (too short for their age). A percent of children under age 6 are wasted (too thin for their age), and a percent of children under age 5 are overweight or obese. Stunting is a sign of chronic undernutrition, wasting a sign of acute undernutrition, and being overweight/obese a sign of excess energy intake. The prevalence of stunting and overweight decreased significantly from 55 percent in 1992 to 37 percent in 2015-16. Stunting has declined from 48 percent in rural areas (in 1992) to 21 percent in urban areas (in 2015–16). The decrease in wasting and overweight had been small or non-existent, with wasting prevalence decreasing slightly between 1992 and 2015-16 from 38 percent to 30 percent (rural) and 29 percent to 24 percent (urban areas), respectively. Overweight/obese prevalence increased significantly in rural areas from 7 percent in 1992 to 12 percent in 2015-16, while it remained stable at around 8 percent in urban areas.

### 154.
In 2019–20, seventy-four percent of households in Malawi had access to improved sources of drinking water compared with 73 percent in rural areas. Seventy-one percent of urban areas had access to improved water sources compared to 71 percent of households in rural areas. Fifteen percent of rural households travel more than 30 minutes round trip to fetch drinking water compared to 7 percent of urban households. The majority (85 percent) of rural households collect their drinking water from unprotected dug wells and unprotected springs[1] of which 11 percent exceeds that of urban households by 9 percentage points. In contrast, only two percent of urban households use unsafe water such as rivers, lakes, or open channels concerns only rural households (3 percent)[2].

### 155.
Regarding sanitation, Multiple indicator cluster surveys (MICS) 2019-20 reported that 46 percent of Malawian households used improved toilet facilities[3] (43 percent of urban households vs. 48 percent in rural areas). The most common types of toilets are pit latrines and dry toilets with no access from human contact and include: flush/urine to flushed sewer system, septic tanks or pit latrines. Improved sanitation is associated with a reduction in the incidence of diarrhea, which can reduce the transmission of cholera, typhoid, and other diseases. The use of shared toilet facilities of an otherwise acceptable type are also common, especially for 49 percent of urban households compared with 35 percent of rural households.
