156\. As for hygiene, twenty-eight percent of the population has basic hygiene services referring to the availability of a handwashing facility on premises with soap and water, slightly above 1 in 2 in urban and 1 in 4 in rural areas. 48 percent of the households have limited access to latrines with significant gap between rural and urban areas (48 percent and 36 percent respectively)###.

157\. The gender implications of water and sanitation go beyond the safety and adequacy of drinking water and sanitation facilities. Women are often responsible for collecting water, which can be tasked with collecting water when it is not available on the premises, the time and energy spent on collecting water has a direct impact on women's health and well-being. The lack of access to forms of human capital, lack of Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (WASH) in school settings has been correlated with lower educational attainment for girls as girls may miss school due to menstruation or other WASH related issues. Lack of access to safe water supply at home is highly correlated with increased infections that may increase maternal mortality rates###.

4\.1\.5 Food security and nutrition

158\. The 2023 IPC analysis for Malawi indicates that 3 million people are experiencing high acute food insecurity (IPC Phase 3 or above). No improvement seems to be recorded since the World Food Program reported in its January 2024 Country Brief an acute food insecurity for 4.4 million Malawians, representing 22 percent of the population###. As per the latest available national health data, 15 percent of children under five years old are stunted (short for their age), 3 percent wasting (thin for their height), 12 percent underweight and 60 percent anemic. The prevalence of stunting in Malawi is high. Levels of child malnutrition are influenced by the mother's Body Mass Index (BMI), mother's level of education, place of residence and household wealth. With regards to feeding practice, only 8 percent of children aged six months or older were exclusively breastfed for at least four months up to anemia, sixty-three percent of children aged 6-59 months and 33 percent of women aged 15-49 are anaemic###. The data highlight the need to set up immediate measures to reduce food consumption gaps.

159\. The important role that women play in ensuring nutritionally healthy diets and its contribution towards household nutrition security is often overlooked. Traditionally, women produce the roles that men and women are expected to play in the household food security and nutrition. Women's contribution consists of production and reproductive dimensions: (i) a productive dimension, which includes activities such as growing crops, raising livestock, managing food production and purchase; (ii) a reproductive dimension, entailing multiple responsibilities such as cooking, cleaning, childcare, and other domestic chores###. Women are often the first to bear their direct impact on household food security, but which are often unpaid and less valued. Access to clean water, sanitation and good hygiene can influence household nutrition health, and sanitation, and ultimately, the health of the household environment; has always been seen as women's and girls' responsibility###. Historically, women's productive and reproductive work has been heavier

## Poverty

161. Poverty levels in Malawi remain high and pervasive. Since 1995, Malawi’s annual economic growth has averaged about 0.3 percent. With population growth of close to 3 percent per capita, this has resulted in a slow decline in the share of people living below the national poverty line, despite a lack of robust economic growth. With a GDP per capita of US$603 in 2020 and a HDI of 0.481* in 2019 which is below the average of 0.513 for countries in the low human development category and below the average of 0.701 for countries in the medium human development category, Malawi remains one of the poorest countries in the world. The country is categorized among least developed countries by the classification of the United Nations and among low human development nations by the classification of the World Bank based on data from UNDP 2019/2020. The Malawi Poverty Report 2020 estimated that 50.8 percent of the population lived below the national poverty line.

While the proportion of poor showed a slight decrease compared to what was reported during 2016/2017 (HRS 48.0 percent), the proportion of the population living in extreme poverty increased by 0.4 percentage point. Analysis by place of residence shows that 56.6 percent of people from rural areas were poor compared to 19.2 percent of those who lived in urban areas. 162. Income inequality as measured by Gini coefficient showed an improvement by decreasing from 0.423 in 2015 to 0.379 in 2020. This value means that the share of total income households would have to earn to reach a level where all households had equal income. The richest 20 percent of the population controls 51.7 percent of total income while the poorest 20 percent control 6.4 percent of total income.

163. Through the UNDP global Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI), deprivations affecting individuals are measured across 10 indicators in three equally weighted dimensions of health, education and standard of living. The MPI is a composite index that takes into account the variables for Malawi’s MPI estimation revealed that** 95 percent of the population in Malawi is multidimensionally poor*** while an additional 27.5 percent is classified as vulnerable to multidimensional poverty. This means that, on average, people living in multidimensional poverty have a higher average deprivation score among people living in multidimensional poverty, it is 43.5 percent. Material MPI value was estimated at 0.618 which indicates that approximately the share of population that is multidimensionally poor adjusted by the intensity of the deprivations.

164. The rate fell by two percentage points in 2020 as an impact of the Covid-19 pandemic, it showed a modest increase of one percent in 2021. The labour force participation rate describes among men and women do not follow the same trend.

167. In terms of earnings, the average monthly mean and the median gross incomes in 2013 are 41,643 and 13,600 Malawi Kwacha respectively (approximately 114.34 and 37.48 USD). Significant differences are observed between male and female mean monthly gross wage of 15,000 MK and 9,200 MK respectively for men and women in urban areas. 168. Wage work. Due mostly to women's care responsibilities and differences in educational attainment, women have fewer opportunities than men to participate in the labour market, and have access to lower-quality employment opportunities than men.

169. In Malawi, women are less likely to be engaged in wage work and often receive lower pay when they do engage in such activities. Women's share of total employment of the labour force was engaged in wage, salary or commercial activities. The proportion of men salaried workers of 12 percent (approximately 30.5 percent) were more likely to participate in wage work than women who are more likely to participate in wage work (65.3 percent) than those without any education (5.6 percent). For women employed in agriculture, only 36 percent receive payment for their work. Women are much less likely to be engaged in wage work compared with men. The proportion of the female labour force compared to 25 percent of the male labour force (11%). However, women and men have similar levels of education. Women's share of total employment was engaged in wage work (7 percent) (10%). Women in urban areas are most likely to be employed in the sales and services sector (26 percent), or in the private sector, or commercial sector (30 percent), while men in rural areas were more likely to be employed in agriculture (54.8 percent). Women who have more than a secondary level of education are more likely to work in professional occupations compared with those without any education. Women in rural areas and who are unemployed work mostly in the agricultural sector and are often unpaid.

170\. In terms of gender pay gaps, the average difference of monthly earnings between men and women is 36.2 percent, with wage workers earning about 84 cents for each dollar earned by men (19). Women in low-skill earnings in wage employment may be partly due to time constraints related to domestic chores, such as collecting water or gathering firewood, and childcare responsibilities. In addition, women are more likely than men to work part-time because they have to accept lower quality jobs (than men) in exchange for the flexibility they need to fit domestic work around their paid employment. The increasing participation of women in the formal sector at work may require a redistribution of domestic responsibilities. Analysis of the latest LSMS data for Malawi finds that women wage workers work fewer hours than men and that women spend more time on household activities.

### 4\.1\.9 Migration

171\. The INHS observed the geographic movement of people across rural and urban areas, districts and abroad (outside Malawi) to establish a new residence within the last 5 years. The results show that 20 percent of the population in Malawi moved from one area to another. Rural and rural areas represented 17.0 percent and 6.7 percent of the population respectively. The proportion of migrants in the Northern Region was the highest (13.8 percent) compared to Central (9.4 percent), Southern (5.2 percent) and Eastern regions (4.8 percent). In urban areas, 10.2 percent were higher than female headed households (6 percent). It was observed that the higher proportion of women in rural areas who migrated was due to their involvement as migrants represented 23 percent of household head with tertiary education while household head with no education had only 5.3 percent migrants. In urban areas, a higher proportion of women whose heads were never married (19.8 percent) followed by households whose heads were either married (9.9 percent), divorced/separated (7.2 percent) and widowed (6.4 percent). The proportion of female parents/family moving, marriage, start of work/business/earning, return from work/job transfer was 30.5 percentage points that is higher than male migrants by 18.2 percentage points. The proportion of migrants who migrated due to marriage were higher for females (36.8 percent) compared to males (15 percent).

172\. Looking at the movement patterns of migrants, the majority of the migrants moved from rural areas to other rural areas (55.0 percent), followed by those who moved from rural area to urban areas (49.0 percent). The same trend was observed for both sexes and for different age groups. Amongst migrants living in urban areas, 61.8 percent were from outside Malawi while 38.2 came from other urban areas and 1.6 were from outside Malawi. 86.8 percent of migrants in rural areas who moved were from other rural areas; 8.9 came from urban areas and 4.1 came from outside Malawi.

### 4\.2 Labour division, decision making and access to economic opportunities

#### 4\.2\.1 Household labour division

173\. In Malawi, social cultural patterns nurture an unequal gender division of labour dividing some household chores more evenly between men and women. Men are mostly involved in intermittent household chores and concentrate much on outdoor productive and money making tasks such as constructing houses, khitas for livestock, or rearing animals (20). Women bear the heavy burden of time-consuming routine tasks, which include house cleaning, meal planning and cooking.


---
**Page 198**
---

174. Despite the fact that women from matrilocal systems are somewhat spared in certain tasks, studies have shown that household tasks are considered to be for women [18]. The socialization of girls and boys is also different. Girls are taught to share food with others and in community task sharing for which many are mainly entrusted with administrative activities and women are given the other duties. Within the household, children are raised with the thought that household chores should be done by women. This is especially true when it comes to cooking for men with the help of boys. The Chewa culture from the most predominant ethnic in the country (04.4 percent) is a good example where girls are taught to share food and cook while boys are taught to manage. In contrast to this, the majority of boys grow up in families where fathers would always be away from home or rest in their villages doing domestic work and small activities to provide for the household needs. Boys are involved in unskilled labor and are not involved in the handling of finance. These boys reproduce the patterns that they have always seen their fathers giving out to them.

175. The significant involvement of women in household tasks does not exclude their participation in income-generating activities with their husbands. Rural women also often apply diversification strategies (e.g., growing crops, raising livestock, keeping poultry, fishing, weaving, working for wages in agriculture or other rural enterprises, etc.) in order to increase income and improve the household's well-being. Consequently women working hours per day far exceed those of men [19]. This is especially true when it comes to cooking, cleaning, childcare, sanitation and lack of clean water obligate women and girls to walk longer distance and allocate more time to firewood collection. Women are also responsible for managing the household budget and thus are willing to accept lower quality jobs in men in exchange for the flexibility they need to fit domestic work into their schedules [20]. This greatly impacts their economic productivity and so their bargaining power within the household.

176. Although the majority of women expressed their need in males helping them on household chores and their will for a more balanced distribution of domestic work, voicing out their need remains difficult due to fear of being labeled as lazy or ungrateful. This is especially considered disrespectful and may constitute grounds for divorce. In addition, women hardly find support at community level where men are the majority and even when the tradition leader is a woman, men are always consulted [21].

177. Women and men are triggered to perform their gender roles as per society's expectations. While men get criticized for not being able to provide for the family's needs, women get punished for not cooking or cleaning well enough. In some cases, women are even beaten up, insulted or sent away from their marital home for not performing their gender roles such as not cooking or

178. Apart from societal norms, previously factors may also influence women’s ability to reduce the time they spend on household and childcare responsibilities and increase the time allocated to paid economic activities. These includes access to education, higher-paid employment, productivity, and the ability to move. Government policy can also alleviate gender differences, as examples by providing maternity leave or supporting affordable childcare services [16].

### 4.2 Division of labour in agriculture

179. Both men and women contribute their labour in farming but their roles are clearly differentiated. While men perform roles that are physically demanding such as ploughing and making the fields ready for sowing, women are responsible for planting seeds and processing crops. Women also typically control marketing of the crops than women. There is some little interchange of roles, with both men and women performing a role in some cases but the overall pattern remains gender-based division of roles stand. Men dominate production roles; the economic roles that generate money or gain in return for productivity such as fishing, producing cash crops or trading hence their work attracts more value than women’s work. Women are more involved in processing and marketing activities to be of small-scale in nature whether it is related to crops (maize, groundnuts, beans, etc.) or livestock (chicken, pigs, goats).

### 4.3 Division of labour at home

180. The 2015-16 MDHS considers that women are participating in household decisions if they are involved in sole joint (with their husband) decision-making within the household in all the three areas of women’s health care, major household purchases and visits to women’s family or relatives. Women were most likely to be involved in decisions on women’s health care (83 percent), followed by decisions about their healthcare (68 percent) and major household purchases (55 percent). Employed women, especially those employed for cash (54 percent), are more likely to be involved in decision-making than unemployed women. Women were also more likely to be so among urban women compared to rural women (56 percent versus 45 percent). The number of women reporting involvement in household decisions has increased from 2013-14 to 2015-16, and the increase concerns mostly women’s involvement in joint decision-making. On women’s exclusive decision making, women were most likely to be involved in decisions on visits to their family or relatives (78 percent), followed by major household purchases and women’s sole decision to visit family even decreased from 25 percent in 2010 to 18 percent in 2015-16. Women’s participation in decision-making rises with increasing levels of education, employment status and wealth quintile. The quality of the relationship between a woman and her husband may reflect in part their ability to choose and enforce their choices (whether they are related to sexual relations or other decisions). A study by the World Bank found that women have more sex with their husbands than men do. The World Bank reported the same proportion of women making their own informed decisions regarding sexual relations, contraceptive use and reproductive health care as in 2014 [35-36].

**Page 200**

181\. Since the culture in Malawi expects men to look for household needs - a fact that leads to male domination of money-making tasks, decision making power and control over household resources are generally left in men's hands\(^{2}\). A study conducted by the University of Agriculture and Natural Resources in 2015 showed that 70 percent of men are handling the household finances. Field consultations confirmed that decisions related to expensive goods' purchase are made by men, while women make decisions on food purchases as well as those related to family farm are limited\(^{3}\). Customarily, while gender roles force women to perform domestic and reproductive tasks, they must also help and assist their husband in the whole farming process. When the woman is not involved in the decision-making processes regarding money and expensive tasks, they return have little or no access to the money gained from the farming activities once the produce is sold\(^{4}\).

182\. With respect to income, women who work and are paid in cash have some control over their earnings. Their involvement in decisions about their earnings has increased over time: 58 percent of women (30 percent of whom with 78 percent of them) make decisions solely or jointly on their own earnings\(^{4}\). The 2015-16 MDHS reported the proportions of women with sole or joint control over their cash earnings to be at 28 percent, those who decide jointly with their husband at 47 percent and those for whom husbands make decisions alone at 25 percent. Women in rural areas (39 percent) are more likely to make/contribute in decision-making on the use of their own wages (43.2 percent making decisions solely or jointly compared to 18.6 percent who have no say), while women with a higher education level and those whose husbands earn less than they do, are more likely to be sole decision makers on their earnings (50 percent). Women in rural areas are also more likely to make decisions about the use of their own cash income compared to urban women (31.6 percent) \({ }^{4}\). Younger women, women without higher secondary school education, and women in the lower wealth quintiles are least likely to have control over their earnings\(^{5}\).

183\. In terms of decision-making implications, a World Bank analysis found that women who stand as sole or joint decision makers on their own cash income are less likely to experience physical or sexual intimate partner violence, a situation that highlights the positive impact of husbands agreeing on engaging their wife as sole or joint decision maker. Besides, women participating in decision-making processes are more likely to access financial services and have fewer children\(^{6}\).

### 4.2.4 Entrepreneurship

184. Women represent a significant part of the entrepreneurial workforce in Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA). Women are constrained in engaging in entrepreneurship compared to men as they meet some limitations in pursuing wage work or other economic opportunities due to low level of education and skills, lack of access to financing domestic responsibilities.

185. In addition, cultural beliefs and norms that gender biases against women create stereotypes that continue to hinder more women to engage in business ventures. Pre-established cultural norms include women's reduced likelihood to operate enterprises in more profitable male dominated sectors. Women are also less likely to be aware of government policies or business development services that respond specifically to the needs of women, and limited capacity to adopt new technologies. As a result, women-owned enterprises are more often constrained to produce lower returns than those owned by men.

186. Within the entrepreneurial sector, business inputs used by women and men managers differ significantly. Men's start-up capital usually comes from agri-based savings earnings. In fact, since most women do not have access to financial services, they are able to save less money and reinvest more money in their business. Women, on the other hand, are more likely to use start-up capital for personal consumption rather than reinvestment. This creates a significant gender gap since women dedicate much time that they could allocate to production activities to household and childcare responsibilities. As such, women-owned enterprises tend to have lower wages paid to workers. Unlike men who are more likely to hire male workers and pay higher wages, women are forced to rely on family members and female labor with limited productivity. Women's barrier in hiring skilled workers may also lead them to operate in low-value-added sectors that require less skilled workers.

187. Due to cultural norms, women are often constrained to set up business in more traditional locations such as the home or roadside compared to male counterparts since they must fulfill care work and domestic responsibilities and have to operate their enterprises in locations that are considered safer or gender appropriate by the society.

188. The Covid-19 pandemic has been especially detrimental for women-led than men-led businesses. (i) Women owned firms are on average smaller and have lower sales and profits, (ii) women's ability to cope with the crisis is hindered by their weak professional skills and lack of access to credit, and (iii) school closures and childcare responsibilities prevented women from continuously running their activities [12]. A World Bank survey in 40 countries found that 85 percent of women-owned micro-enterprising sectors which experienced a more severe demand shock from the pandemic, 93 percent of women's businesses sell to final consumers compared to 87 percent of men's businesses [13].

[12] Male-far gender assessment, Eastern and southern Africa gender platform, World Bank Group, March 2022.

[13] Ibid.

# Access to and control of resources, assets and services

## Land

189. The 2016 Land Act regulates two categories of land: public land that comprises Government or unallocated customary land and freehold and that includes customary estate, leaseholds (Figure 3). Often referred to in the colonial era through the term "customary land", private land was used for large-scale production of export crops, such as tea and tobacco. While these estates were initially owned by British companies, they were sold to Malawians once free for export, smallholder farmers were only allowed to sell their crops at lower prices for local consumption. From independence in 1964 when these estates passed into the hands of Malawian elite with a few exceptions, the land sector has been characterized by the colonial-era dual structure of an agriculture sector made up of large estates and smallholders.

### Figure 3: Contemporary categorization of land in Malawi

| Land Type | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Public | Government Land |
| Unallocated Customary land | |
| Customary Estate | |

Source: Land Act 2016

190. The share of agricultural land with respect to total land was at 59.9 percent in 2018. Smallholder farmers are responsible for most agricultural production and own more than half of the land cultivated by small farmers is devoted to maize, combining the staple crop maize with a wide array of legumes, gourds and leafy vegetables. The share of agricultural land owned by women has increased from 32 percent in 1987 to 40 percent in 2016. However, the majority of smallholder farmers are men.

191. Malawians access land through inheritance (32 percent) and outright (18 percent)(*)). A higher proportion of women own land through inheritance than through outright ownership (53 percent), with variations on the region. Malawi dominates land ownership in the North with 40-60 percent of men and 70-90 percent of women owning land, while the Southern Region has a lower percentage of male owners (28.0 percent for males versus 38.1 percent for females) but the Southern Region has more pronounced gender based disparities since 46.5 percent females own land compared to 37 percent for males. Rights in customary land are often shared between men and women, with matrilineal systems in the country: the patrilinial system predominant in the Northern Region and two southern regions (Mulanje and Nkhotakota), while the matrilinial system is predominant in the central and Southern Region districts. A mixed system borrowing from the two systems is practiced in some areas. In mainstream line, women in the family inherit land and the man

(*) Country Profile Malawi, Landlinks, USAID, August 2010 (https://www.land-links.org/country-profile/malawi)

68


---
**Page 203**
---

moves into the woman’s family home after marriage. In patrilineal systems, inheritance is passed on to the sons and the women move in with their husband’s family. Customary law in patrilineal systems often gives women land-use rights acquired through kinship relationships and their status as wives, mothers, sisters and daughters. These rights are therefore linked to women’s role as household heads and caregivers. Women who do not have these rights may be forced into subsist. Although women tend to have better access to land in matrilineal systems, men often remain the primary decision makers regarding land use and management. A study conducted in four rural areas of Malawi under matrilineal system showed it in 2018 that 69 percent of the farm managers were men. Even in matrilineral societies, men still “own” the land (28). Besides, in the few cases where women have access to land, their rights are often conditional and run on the interpretation of local customary system. As an example, lead farmers consulted in Ngweve Village in Malawi reported that they had more access to land than their wives because “women do not own land than men” (19). Women and men farmers met in Munguti Village in a patrilineal community of Rumphi District said: “land belongs to men, it is allocated to sons because all daughters get married.”

192. Other means to access land included allocation from traditional leaders, land leasing, government resettlement programs, and land purchase. An estimated 20 percent of landholders obtain their land through customary authorities; roughly 1 percent of landholders obtain land through purchase (34).

193. Tenure insecurity is evident for women moving to patrilineal societies, men moving to matrilineal societies or women who are widowed, divorced or separated after the divorce or death of their spouse. The high prevalence of HIV/AIDS among the adult population exacerbates the degree of insecurity a spouse may experience (35).

194. With the 2016 Land Act, the 2016 Customary Land Act and the 2016 Registered Land Amendment Act, new land laws were introduced in Malawi. These laws were developed and enacted for effective land administration and more equitable access. The 2016 Land Act enables the conversion of customary land to freehold title. It also provides for the establishment of a new land administration framework through the establishment of Traditional Land Management Areas (TLMA) and the creation of Local Committees. The 2016 Customary Land Act and The Customary Land Act (2016) elaborate the legal responsibilities of local land committees established at Group Village Headman level to manage the land within the general boundaries of the TLMA. These committees are also responsible for registering customary land with private and corporate bodies if they remain unworked after five years. The Registered Land Amendment Act (Malawi 2016) provides for the registration of freehold title for land that has been subject to a lack of state capacity to implement this in practice. No specific mention for access and control of land by men and women is observed in legislation.

### 4.3.2 Agricultural inputs and assets

195. In Malawi, more than 80 percent of the gender gap in productivity is owing to women’s low use of improved seeds, improved seeds and fertilizers. Although women and men farmers are aware of the importance of their use, women have more difficulty than men in accessing the quality


---
**Page 204**
---

### 196. As for physical assets, the level of access and control men and women have over them varies.

High value assets such as ploughs, animal carts, fishing boats, etc., are generally owned and controlled by men. Women usually have limited access to these assets except in cases where hand hoes and knives. Given the difficulties women face to access productive male labour and considering their time poverty due to unpaid care and domestic work, the use of labour-saving technologies is a key strategy for increasing women's productivity and income.

### 197. The non-availability of male labour to work on female-managed farm plots accounts among the most important contributors to gender gap in agricultural productivity[20]. Indeed, women plot managers are more likely than men to be single or widowed (58% compared with 34%), as well as most of them are not married (70% of them being either widowed, divorced or separated) in contrast to male farmers who are more likely to be married. Women also have fewer household members that could provide labour on their farm. They have no choice but to use less productive alternative sources of labour such as hired labour and family members[21]. Moreover, female plot managers that could afford hiring male labour may not afford better quality labour and may experience lower labour-productivity since hired male workers may work less hard for female plot managers due to cultural bias[22].

### 4.3.3 Extension services

#### 198. Through the Ministry of Agriculture namely the Department of agriculture extension services (DAES), Malawi has a demand-driven and pluralistic agricultural extension system. The system is based on the principle that farmers should be able to access appropriate information and advisory services from those best able to provide them[23]. The DAES relies on various approaches to ensure that farmers of all gender categories have access to extension services including: radio, television, newspapers, magazines, posters, leaflets, demonstration plots, with various service providers to achieve their vision; the clusters and Mindandandas[24] used as platforms for information dissemination. The DAES also works in collaboration with agricultural enterprises in the same catchment area or locally; the Farmer field schools (FFS) to enhance the adoption of improved technologies and practices; the Farmer input supply programmes (FISP); business schools (BBS) to promote market-oriented production among smallerholder farmers; the Lead Farmer[25] approach to increase extension coverage for the adoption of improved practices and technologies. The DAES also works in collaboration with other stakeholders such as NGOs that ensure recommended practice, technology and information dissemination for easy and equitable

The Household and Community Surveys conducted by the International food policy research institute (IFPRI) in 2017 [199] states that AEDOs remain the dominant players in agricultural extension services in Mali. The majority of households received advice from other stakeholders such as non-governmental organizations (NGOs), community-based or farmer-based organizations. Ninety-nine percent of group villages indicated the visits by government officials, NGOs, and farmers to their communities for training or employment agents or workers. The non-state service providers are dominated by international NGOs that mostly operate in specific regions or districts. In addition, there is a significant role played by local NGOs. The few local organizations involved in the provision of extension service need to be strengthened to become more effective in providing agricultural extension services. According to the extension policy, extension service provision is heavily supply-driven; a significant proportion of farmers who received advice did not request for it. Three-quarters of the households involved in the surveys reported that they had received advice from an agricultural extension agent and this percentage was about the half in the last 12 months.

The agriculture topics of advice received included crop rotation practices, methods on weather forecasting, soil testing, fertilization, improved varieties, soil-fertility management practices, and water harvesting practices. Male respondents were more likely to receive advice on weather forecasting, improved varieties, soil fertility management, and irrigation; female respondents were more likely to receive advice on pest control, crop disease control, and irrigation and water harvesting practices. Advice on health, nutrition and dietary diversity (six food groups) in food consumption, washing hands before and after consuming food, and the use of clean drinking water was received by 10% or less of respondents in the IFPRI surveys advised on postharvest practices, a quarter on

## Financial service providers in Malawi include commercial banks, Savings and Credit Cooperatives (SACCOs), licensed microfinance institutions (MFIs) and payday lending companies. SACCOs are the most common financial service provider in rural areas. They offer loan products, MFIs and PLGs also provide small credit only. Basic mobile money services, particularly money transfers are also available through two mobile network operators TMN and Airtel. Although SACCOs have a strong presence in rural areas, they do not reach all adults. Credit products are offered at scale. The FinScope Consumer Survey Malawi 2014 reported the percentage of adults who had access to financial services by location. Access was highest in urban areas (73 percent), followed by peri-urban areas (54 percent) and rural areas (34 percent). Women were less likely than men to have access to formal financial services, with only 28 percent among males (55 percent) compared to females (51 percent). As for access to informal financial services, the same survey indicated its use by 92 percent of adults. As an example, the Community based financial service provider (CBFP) is a SACCO that has been active in Malawi since 2004 and the financial inclusion of 1.1 million members from urban and rural areas, of which about 47 percent are women. The CBFP provides financial services to more than half of all adults living in urban and peri-urban areas represented about one third of those who accessed informal financial services. Women were less likely than men (35 percent) to have access to formal financial services among urban and peri-urban residents, but they account for the largest number of adults without access to financial services (35 percent). The proportion of women excluded from the financial system is more than double that in urban areas (22 percent). The proportion of women excluded from the financial system is more than double that in urban areas (22 percent).

percent of creditline beneficiaries used the creditlines to set up businesses and 14.4 percent used them to purchase agricultural inputs for food crops in urban areas. The highest proportion of respondents who borrowed for business start-up was in rural areas (40.2 percent) compared with urban areas (27.6 percent). Main sources of credit/loans mentioned by recipients were village banks, relatives, neighbours, lenders and NGOs. Loans obtained from village banks were higher in rural areas (31.8 percent) than in urban areas (9.5 percent). Households headed by women were more likely than those headed by men to borrow for food crops in rural (56.8 percent) than in urban (92.7 percent) areas. The first reason raised by 22.2 percent of rural households as well as 20.2 percent of households run by women who never applied for a loan is the lack of collateral, while 14.3 and 15.8 percent respectively cited the need to go to debt into suspicion, of refusal, high cost of service, inadequate collateral and lack of knowledge. The proportion of rural households mentioning inadequate collateral as reason for not applying for a loan was higher compared to 21.7 percent of rural households. While the first reason for not applying for a loan accounted for 20.5 and 19.7 percent respectively, lack of knowledge was cited by 34.5 percent of rural households as the lack of need, 22.6 percent of female-headed households and 22.2 percent of rural households.

203. If the rural poor are seen to be placed at disadvantage in accessing financial services due to the remote areas they reside in, women's situation is worse. Indeed, women's financial inclusion is constrained by their financial capability as well as social and environmental factors [16]. Financial capability refers to a woman's ability to manage her income and expenditure effectively. Women have about managing their resources, and understanding, selecting, and making use of financial services. Social and environmental factors include the lack of awareness and knowledge of available financial products and ways to engage with the financial system (for examples: short-term, medium- or long-term loans; savings accounts; credit cards; mobile money; insurance products, etc.) and affect their attitude towards risks and general financial behaviour.

204. Societal factors consider socio-economic and welfare characteristics such as demographic characteristics, household size, gender, education, economic activity and incomes of individuals. Women's access to financial services is constrained by the fact that they limit the chances to access credit considering the difficulties to present collateral or personal guarantees. The lack of property rights for women compared with men may constitute a legal discrimination for their access to finance. Women's lack of formal education also excludes them as less attractive clients and reduces the interest of formal financial institutions in providing them with loans, especially those required by women for high-value sectors and enterprises [16]. Regarding incomes, people with higher income are more likely to open a bank account to save part of their income, are therefore more likely to use other financial

products and be financially excluded compared to low-income earners. The study of financial inclusion in Africa[1] found that being a man, richer, more educated and older favour financial inclusion with a higher influence of education and income. Women's low earnings which impede their ability to save prevent them from accessing financial services and investing in businesses and enterprises. This is because women are less likely than men to have access to capital. Thus forced to start their business with capital gifted to them by their families, unlike men who are more likely to use savings or loans. In a rural village in a multilateral community reported the lack of working capital among their biggest obstacle[2].

For environmental factors, the availability and accessibility of financial products and services are worth mentioning. Since most financial institutions in Malawi are located in urban centres and towns, women living in remote areas with limited access to these institutions, especially in rural areas which leaves much to be desired, women are certainly disadvantaged because of travel and time constraints[3]. The study also mentioned the lack of financial products designed to meet their needs during field consultations.

Regarding the use of technology to access financial services, men and women's overall use of bank accounts and ownership of mobile phones are low. The 2015-16 MDHS reported men as more likely than women in rural areas to have a bank account (34 percent compared with 18 percent) while for urban areas the difference is even greater (10 percent and 33 percent, respectively). The likelihood for men and women in urban areas to use a bank account was higher among those who had attended secondary school or above rather than for men and women in rural areas. For example, 28 percent of urban women use a bank account compared with only 9 percent of rural women[4]. Urban women are also more likely to own a mobile phone compared with 26 percent of rural women, and 39 percent of women in urban areas use their phone for financial transactions compared with 21 percent of women in rural settings[5].

### 4.3.5 Access to markets

The gender-based division of assets, commodities and existing market organization allocates women to the less profitable products and markets. In addition, women's access to market is inhibited by several factors including limited access to information, restricted mobility due to childcare responsibilities, lack of education and training, low levels of decision-making power and voice in marketplaces. Women have also been bearers of consequences of the gender inequalities affecting them all along the production chain. In fact, women have less products due to gender productive roles, lower access to inputs such as land, plow, unequal use of inputs such as labour, labour-saving technology, improved seeds, etc.

Most of the women involved in FGM commercialize their products and access to markets through informal networks. The majority of these women are located in rural areas or villages located further away from the production site. Few women groups mentioned engaging in commercial agreement with F-GW.

Barriers mentioned by women and youth farmer groups met during field consultations were related to the lack of knowledge on the identification of profitable business, the difficulties to identify buyers and conclude formal agreements, a situation that creates an unhealthy competition among women and youth farmers[6]. In addition, most women and youth farmers reported the difficulties with the process of certification with the Malawi Bureau of Standards. As a result, although few groups were able to identify market

213. In Malawi, contemporary formal state politics is constructed as a masculine domain in which women can only participate to a limited extent. Traditional cultural beliefs relegate women to non-political roles, the society assigning them to roles in the private and domestic sphere rather than in public life. The lack of education for girls compared with boys, the unequal distribution of resources and lower economic status compared to men hold them back from political leadership.

214. Since female voice and leadership are needed in government to promote and enforce policies pertaining to women, women's participation in national as well as local government is essential to creating opportunities for women to articulate their needs and concerns. Even though Malawi has a quota system that guarantees 30% of seats reserved for women in the legislature and equal representation through quotas, elected political representation in the country remains

---

unequal and female political participation has seen little progress. Indeed, at central government,
Malawi has had only one female president (Joyce Banda, 2012-2014) among six presidents since its independence in 1964. As of 2019, only 23 percent of elected parliamentary seats were female.
Within local government, as the system in Malawi includes local councils that are responsible for proposing and implementing development projects, women elected to local councils can advance and advocate women's interests, concerns and issues. However, women's representation at this level has been slow to increase. Women were first allowed to sit on local government councils since 1998, when local government councils were created through decentralization. The proportion of women elected to local councils has not changed significantly from 20% in the 2004 elections (35 out of 176 seats) to 20% in the 2019 elections (40 out of 180 seats for women among a total of 460 elected seats)(1)). This proportion remains low compared to the Sub-Saharan average. The lack of female representation at this level is not only due to the fact that it has a negative impact on female representation at the national level as local government is a valuable path to access decision-making positions, but also because women are still underrepresented in principal representation in elected positions. 60 percent of positions with decision-making responsibilities in the public sector are held by men. The persistence of gender inequity within ministries is motivated by several factors.

215. Another significant barrier that is also worth mentioning for women to access elected positions is the cost of campaigning. Male candidates were found to spend 14 per cent more money that their female counterparts in electoral campaigns(2).

### 4.4.2 Women's participation in agricultural organizations

The National Smallholder Farmers' Association of Malawi (NASFAM) which is the largest smallholder farmers' membership organization in Malawi, registers 36 percent of female members.
This low proportion of female member translates well the crop production patterns of NASFAM members. The majority of women are involved in subsistence farming(3). A case study analysing gender equality in co-operatives in Malawi reported that traditions holding back women from participating in agricultural activities as well support from their life partners are the causes of non-participation in cooperatives(4).

217. The FGD have shown that existing farmers organizations support smallholder farmers in providing essential services at all value chain levels (access to inputs, marketing, etc.) that would otherwise be difficult for them to access. The FGD also highlighted that women face challenges in managing to serve and deliver services to poor and vulnerable people (including women and youth), which is a major barrier to the sustainable use of natural resources as well as the violation of women and youth within farmers' communities.

218. In terms of natural resource management, various natural resources committees could exist, function in an isolated manner or collaborate in a coordinated/migrated way. Women are usually active in natural resources committees but they do not always assume the role

### 4.5 Gender-based violence including sexual violence

#### 4.5.1 Gender-based violence and other harmful practices

219. The UN Declaration on Violence against Women 1993 defined violence against women as "acts of gender-based violence that cause, have caused or are likely to cause physical harm or suffering, threats of such acts, coercion and other deprivations of liberty whether occurring in public or in private life". Gender-Based Violence (GBV) is a form of human rights violation and includes all forms of violence that excludes women, girls and children from acquisition and control over resources such as land, jobs, education, credit and other goods and services based on their sex and statistics. Despite the numerous international declarations and conventions aimed at eliminating GBV, many of its forms (physical, sexual, emotional or psycho-social and economic) are still prevalent in varying degrees across all regions of the world.

220. The most common form of physical violence among both females and males in Malawi was beating, hitting or battering. According to the 2015-16 MDHS, 34 percent of women aged 15-49 years reported experiencing physical violence during their lifetime. Among those aged 15-49 years who had ever been pregnant, 38 percent reported experiencing physical violence during pregnancy. Youngest women aged 15–19 were more likely to experience physical violence than older women (42% compared with 30%). The proportion of 38 percent women experiencing physical violence in urban areas was higher than that in rural areas (33 percent). By region, the highest percentage of physical violence on women was observed among those living in Southern and North-Western regions (41 percent), followed by Central regions (33 percent). Perpetrators of physical violence against ever-married women were current and former husbands (90%), while 27% of the women who experienced sexual violence during pregnancy reported perpetrators to be family members (including mothers and step-mothers, fathers or step-fathers; sisters or brothers, and other relatives), current boyfriend for 6 percent and teacher for 6 percent.

221. With regards to sexual violence, the Gender Based Violence Survey 18 reported unwanted touch, marital rape, sex deprivation, forced early marriage, attempted rape, incest, defilement and the entry of a man into a woman's bedroom without her consent as forms of sexual violence in 2013. The most prevalent form of sexual violence experienced by males was sexual deprivation, while the most common form for females was unwanted touch. According to the 2015-16 MDHS, 14 percent of women experienced sexual violence the year before the survey and 21 percent had experienced it at some point in their lives (35% of those who experienced it before age 18). The lowest percentage of sexual violence on women were observed among the 15–19 age group, and highest proportion between the 25-29 (13.5) and 27-29 years old groups. Women in a stable relationship with their partner are more likely to experience relationship with age. As for family status, divorced, separated, and widowed women are most at risk (31% percent) compared to never-married women (10 percent). This category includes women

as employees in the workplace facing sexual harassment and other forms of violence). The proportion of sexual violence was higher (in rural (21 percent) than urban areas (17 percent). The prevalence of sexual violence varies by region with the highest rates reported in the Central region, followed by the Northern Region and Southern Region [24.5, 20.8 and 17.1 percent respectively]. In the Central region, 39 percent of women indicated current husband as the most common perpetrator; for never-married women, 31 percent former husband and 5 percent strangers. As for never-married women, current or former boyfriend is the most common perpetrator [20].

222. The experience of physical violence was more common among women with children (23 and 10 percent respectively) compared to married women (33 percent and 22 percent respectively). The experience of physical and sexual violence was more common among employed than unemployed women, and the prevalence increased with increasing level of wealth, with variation with women's level of education was non-linear.

223. Overall, 40 percent of all women who experienced any type of physical or sexual violence sought help from a health provider. Among those who received help (46 percent) however, women who experienced both physical and sexual violence were more likely to have sought help (55 percent) than women who had experienced only physical violence (35 percent) or only sexual violence (20 percent). Women most commonly sought help from their husband's family (33 percent) or a friend (10 percent). At institutional level, 10 percent of women solicited the police for help. The police were more likely to be approached by women seeking help in the Central Region than either the Northern or Southern Regions [24].

224. Under the umbrella of GBV, Intimate partner violence (IPV) constitutes a serious concern, with two-thirds of women reporting that they had experienced IPV and/or sexual harassment and/or sexual violence [18]. An important aspect of IPV is the attitudes of both men and women about the justification of husbands beating their wives. The Malawi DHS 2015-16 highlighted that 16 percent of women reported that they were beaten by their husband because they had not cooked a meal to their satisfaction. Husbands' alcohol consumption is a significant factor that increases the risk of IPV, 79 percent of women whose husbands drink alcohol are more likely to experience IPV than only 25 percent of women whose husbands do not drink. IPV also demonstrates intergenerational transmission since women whose father beat her mother are more likely to experience IPV themselves.

225. Despite increased legal protections, GBV continues to be a significant challenge throughout Malawi. The Malawi Police Force (MPF) has been working towards the protection and gender-based violence developed in July 2020, victims of domestic violence demonstrate a continued lack of trust in the police force and other law enforcement agencies and formal authorities such as the police. Women met during field consultations said that “yaukudima twaluta yawali”, meaning literally that “these are marriage’s secrets” and that GBV is one of the things that should be kept secret [25]. The women also reported that they were not reporting their husbands when the latter are not treating them well, but once women tell them about their misconduct, they become abusive. Women also reported that they did not know where to report in various the GBV cases and where to report them to (marriage counselors, community leaders, police etc.). The preference for informal versus formal reporting is likely due in part to a low

# Child marriage

226\. Malawi has one of the highest rates of child marriage in the world with about half of girls married before their 18th birthday and 12 percent of girls married before their 15th birthday [1]. A significant gender gap is observed between the women and men's first age of marriage estimated at 10.2 years for women compared to 7.4 years for men.

227\. Early child and forced marriage are a deeply embedded practice in Malawian society. Child marriage is often seen as an important rite of passage, marking the transition from childhood into adulthood upon reaching puberty [2], a practice that is often considered as encouraging early sexual activity. In fact, young girls are viewed as ready for marriage at an early age and are expected to be married before they reach 18 years old.

228\. Early marriage has significant and long-term consequences for girls and women: high probability of school drop-out that would significantly [3] lead girls to give birth at a young age, present a higher risk of maternal mortality and morbidity, increase the likelihood of domestic violence, and increase the likelihood to have more children throughout their lifetime. (ii) limit their level's of literacy and economic opportunities and become more dependent on their husbands; (iii) reduce women's decision making power within their homes and increase the likelihood of experiencing IPV.

# Child labour

229\. Although the law prohibits all forms of child labour in Malawi, for instance the employment code provides a list of hazardous work for children accompanied by a fine or imprisonment for violations, sets the minimum age for employment at 14, and prevent children between 14 and 18 from working in hazardous jobs; the country is characterized by extensive


---
**Page 214**
---

child labour**. One out of two (52 percent) Malawian children is a working child and most of them (81 percent) started to work before the age of ten years old**. High prevalence of child labour is due to the combination of poverty and difficulty of access education at intermediate and above levels. Many children are therefore forced to engage in child labour in the informal economy. Child labour is much more prevalent among girls than boys, with 58 percent of working children being girls. Over 90 percent of the children engaged in child labour live in rural areas and the remaining 12 percent are in urban areas where a significant gap is related to the dominating agricultural sector in Malawi's employment structure.

230. Child labour was most prevalent with tea, cocoa and livestock herding, brickmaking and construction, and domestic service. Forced child labour also occurred, particularly in agriculture, construction, and domestic service. Children were often forced into child labour by their families. Children often worked 12-hour days, frequently for little or no pay. Many boys worked as vendors, and young girls sold goods on the streets. Some children were used as domestic servants, moving low or no wages. Further analysis and development on child labour are available in Annex I of an Environmental and Social Management Framework.

### 4.6 Coping with Hardships

231. Most groups met during field consultations have mentioned that economic hardships are due to their inability to meet basic necessities – particularly for women head of households (high number of children, care to extended families, etc.), increasing cost of living and price volatility. The financial strain is further exacerbated by the need to pay school fees and other education-related costs such as uniforms inputs. The most critical times during the year are the rainy season corresponding to the planting and cultivation period when food security is a major concern for households with children who cannot be sent back to school. To respond to these constraints, one of the most frequently used strategy to deal with substance and daily economic constraints is the sale of small livestock.

232. The second coping mechanism mostly reported by the FGD participants is the "gamy**". Described as a form of informal credit, it can be provided in various forms, some of which is piecework weaving or ridging on the fields of other smallholders, or on agricultural estates. "Gamy" is usually used to meet basic needs such as food and school fees for children. It helps poor households in the crucial hungry period before store foods running out and the next harvest**. While "gamy" can be a useful coping strategy during lean periods, it can also lead to a loss of cash income from the gamy**, the need to do so gamys to obtain an immediate supply of food may conflict with own-farm production which could lock poor households into a vicious cycle of food insecurity. Gamy** is also used as a form of informal credit and can be provided in various forms, some of which structural anomalies such as small land sizes, credit constraints and labour and fertilizer shortages. Less educated males are more likely to supply gamys labor. Gamys labor supply increases with household size, while it decreases with the level of crop farming and size of land owned**.


---
**Page 215**
---

233. Village savings and loans associations (VSLAs) is a transparent, democratic and structured version of the informal saving groups[19], often considered by some poor and rural poor as a mechanism that helps face economic hardships. In many cases, VSLA allow their members to save their own money in safe place, to access loans and to obtain emergency insurance. Members can take out loans for various purposes such as purchasing seeds, buying livestock or other productive assets, or they can use the loans to invest in income generating activities to raise household income[20]. The VSLA model has been shown to lead to significant improvements in household health and wellbeing, and an overall improved quality of life.

234. Another coping strategy some women reported during the FGDs is the diversification of income generating activities. This option cannot be accessible to the most vulnerable since its implementation requires a certain level of education and access to resources. Some ongoing diversification activities mentioned the need to invest in 10 large soap moulding trays of 15m by 5 meters, which are used for making soaps, as well as other items such as jerry cans, buckets, water etc., as well as some capacity strengthening on how to make soap[21]. This strategy is obviously difficult to implement for the poorest who cannot invest in the medium/long term and who are less educated.

### 5. GENDER INEQUALITIES EXACERBATED BY CLIMATE CHANGE

235. Most of the women, men and youth farmers and community members consulted during the project design process indicated to have noticed changes in the frequency or severity of climate change impacts on their livelihoods over the past years[22]. Climate change inequalities exist in all societies and shape the ways in which climate change impacts play out in society. Climate change could be considered as a “threat multiplier” for existing social, economic and environmental inequalities. Women and communities most face climate risks and hazards, vulnerability of individuals varies depending on their gender, geography and age.

236. Global warming and environmental damage are generated because the ability of women to adapt is hampered by several factors. Women are more likely to live in poverty than men and consequently have less access to resources such as land, water, seeds, tools, finance, improved technologies, income and decision-making authority to be able to cope with climate shocks and stresses. Women and men have different social status and different access to physical resources like land, water, seeds, tools, finance, improved technologies, income and decision making authorities and credit. Therefore, men usually have multiple strategies and options for coping with climate change impacts while women are more likely to be left behind in the face of climate change issues. Besides, since women are often more dependent on natural resources, loss of access due to climate change impact also weakens their bargaining position in the household as they are responsible for food security[23]. Women are also more vulnerable during the situation leaves men in a stronger “fall-back position” during crisis[24].

237. Women are considered responsible for food security in Maasai societies. When food is limited and there is no money to buy it, women have to reduce their consumption by adults for the benefit of children; it is women who get the smaller food portions within the family and mothers are the ones who are only left out on a nutritional diet in favour of children. When the women taking the food and dietary cuts are those in their childbearing age, a cyclical process is in operation.

---

motion where women who were themselves stunted in childhood will be unable to meet the food needs of their children and thus condemn their growth, creating an intergenerational cycle of poverty and malnutrition that is often a breeding ground for violence.

238. When climate change leads to lower yields and crop productivity, poor and subsistence farmers have little or no surplus to sell to earn money for purchasing goods like medicine, clothes, food, and other necessities. This makes it difficult for women in these families to farm. Food production for family feeding or income generation becomes a challenge that puts women head of households at risk of violence. Women are often the primary caregivers and providers of income. This renders them more susceptible to sexual exploitation, which can take various forms such as transactional sex in exchange for goods or being trafficked into commercial sexual exploitation. In addition, women who have limited access to education and training face family financial hardship compared to boys. They are forced to drop out of school to take care of domestic chores and other responsibilities at home.

239. As is the case across sub-Saharan Africa, gathering water and firewood is widely deemed the responsibility of women and girls in Malawi. Since environmental degradation leads to a lack of clean water sources and fuel wood collection becomes more challenging and time-consuming, women are forced to travel farther distances to collect these resources. This increases their exposure to rape and sexual assault. In some cases, they must also walk through areas controlled by armed groups or guarded by those who control access to limited natural resources, for instance at water collection points.

240. The impacts of climate change also increase women's burden within the household. With natural resources degradation, water and fuel wood collection becomes more challenging and time-consuming. Women are often responsible for collecting these resources. This places attributes to women the task of caring for the sick, climate related disasters worsens their workload considerably as they have to travel farther distances to collect these resources. Evidence increase. Women are therefore highly disadvantaged since they must fulfill reproductive and caring roles within the household while also having to meet the demands of migration. To access better income opportunities, especially in times of crisis, men migrate to urban areas, other cities or to other countries. In addition to the labor constraints this situation entails, women are forced to bear the burden of providing for their families by migrating and living on their own, often without the help of their migrant husbands who rarely send remittances?

241. Climate change also adversely affects women's sexual and reproductive health and rights. Extreme weather events such as floods and droughts can lead to contamination of water sources, as well as sanitation facilities, leading to clean water shortages and water pollution[3]. In these circumstances, women are incinerated to save water for household use for the detriment of appropriate hygiene practices. Women also face increased risk of sexual violence in conflict zones, particularly those related to menstruation. Family cut off food consumption may also affect general and maternal health.

### 6. TAILORING PROJECT ACTIVITIES BASED ON THE GENDER ASSESSMENT

243. The field consultations conducted in the project area during the design process which involved women smallholder farmers, local government officials, extension officers, community representatives, ongoing project non-governmental agriculture association, social enterprise acting as demonstration and training center to promote EOA solutions, confirmed the existing gender inequalities and barriers that hinder women’s participation in climate change adaptation. Literature review and described in the above sections. These barriers are summarized as follows:

244. Social barriers and human capital. The cultural norms and the socialization process in the country have created a situation where women smallholder farmers have been under heavy burden of unpaid domestic and reproductive tasks, and men and boys are mainly entrained with outdoor productive and money-making tasks. As men are generally considered as the head of households, they control most of the resources such as land, livestock, tools, etc., while women control over household resources are left in their hands. Besides, women and girls are equally limited by other social norms that prevent them from accessing education, health care services, credit, etc. Increased number of school drop-out driven by early marriage and teenage pregnancy and vice versa, lack of economic independence plays a role in exposing them to more unequal and risky sexual relationships that in turn increase their exposure to HIV/AIDS.

245. Women involved in VNRMC farming and VSL in Makwizu Kapalapatla village confirmed women’s roles in domestic tasks such as fetching water and cooking fuel176. They shared their main challenges in having their sources of water at 2 km walk from their village.

247\. Knowledge and technical barriers. Although both women and men in Malawi generally have low access to information through the various forms of mass media, a significant gender gap is observed in proportion of men and women who receive agricultural extension services (between 38 percent in 2019-20). In terms of advisory services, females are less likely to receive advice on weather forecasting, crop management, post-harvest handling, marketing, and improved farming techniques. Women's limited access to information and extension services results in lower level of innovation and adoption of new technologies (e.g., use of fertilizers, improved seeds, agro-technique, especially among the vulnerable) and weak technical capacity (e.g. limited capacity to integrate catchment into VLPAs). Knowledge and technical barriers combined with limited access to inputs prevent women from increasing their productivity.

248\. Market and financial barriers. Gender-based division of assets, commodities and existing market organization allocate to women the less profitable products and markets. In addition, women have limited access to information on marketing strategies and business knowledge namely in terms of identification of profitable business as well as identification of buyers and constitutes a significant barrier that is worsened by low level of formal education. Gendered taboos limit women's mobility, a situation that gives men more power and voice in marketplaces. Another constraint faced by most women and youth farmers is the difficulty to understand and comply with the terms of credit agreements. Women are also less likely than men to have access to finance constitutes also a significant barrier that is worsened by factors like low level of formal education, difficulties to present collateral or personal guarantees, etc. Many women, men and

youth involved in FGD mentioned not to be interested in accessing formal financial services as they do not want to be trapped with formal financing institutions and their tedious procedures.

249. Women farmers in matrilocal Mpale village shared their biggest concern on their poor access to land compared to men, due to lack of capital, high cost of farming and poor access to markets[15].

250. Institutional barriers: Women’s and girls’ effective participation and protection in policy-making processes and consultations appear limited due in part to the institutional mechanisms in place to take the vulnerable point of view into account. Policy drafters and approvers and law makers do not always consider women’s and girls’ perspectives.

251. The analysis of the CCA barriers in the gender assessment has thus contributed to the construction of the project’s theory of change. The identification of upstream barriers helps ensuring that the components of the project are designed so that they are relevant to the context and potential beneficiaries’ constraints and needs.

252. Field consultations also allow to identify lessons learnt and opportunities that the project can exploit at scale. ### Under Component 1, ESAM will promote socially inclusive and meaningful participation of the vulnerable groups in NRM decision-making processes of Integrated Landscape Management (ILM). To this end, the project will promote the use of the Dimira Clubs as an entry point for engaging and women, men, female and male youth, and other vulnerable groups into the entire catchment management process. The clubs will be used to: (i) in addition to capacity building activities that are related to climate change adaptation and the integration of catchment/landscape into village-level planning processes; (ii) provide a platform for dialogue on gender issues, including strengthening the skills of the vulnerable and (iii) promoting equitable sharing of decision-making power at household level. The clubs will also be used as an entry point for engaging women in ILM. This will create space for dialogues both in the households and communities that will allow discussing and challenging gender and social norms as well as other issues and challenges and finding solutions; and, will provide a platform for dialogue on gender issues.

Gender and social inclusion issues will be discussed with a specific focus on (i) gender and sex: (a) violence against women and girls including domestic violence, sexual harassment, sexual violence (GBV) and Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH), including masculinities and patriarchal norms; (b) female genital mutilation/cutting (FGM); (c) forced early marriage and Child, Early and Forced Marriage (CEFM); (d) the right to freely decide on family planning and Free Prior Informed Consent (FPIC) process; (e) vaccines, motives and results of gender discrimination and social exclusion; and (v) gender and social inclusion in community development and welfare, including food and nutrition security. The project will support the dissemination of knowledge and information through the community. The project will support 1 110 clubs of Dimitra Clubs and implementation of the Gender and Social Inclusion Strategy (GSIS) and the Community Based Household methodology, to spread the use of the approach at village level through "Dimitra Clubhouse" in order to ensure that women are included in decision-making processes. Besides, the processes for VNRMC and SCRM selection charter, member selection and capacity development will include awareness raising on gender and social inclusion such SEAH and GBV. The project will also support the implementation of the Gender and Social Inclusion Management plans are gender and youth sensitive and socially inclusive. For the implementation of the project’s technical components, the project will ensure that women and other vulnerable groups make sure that they benefit from the project's technical as well as inputs and equipment support.

### Under Component 2, from a gender and social inclusion perspective, ESAM will promote resilient livelihoods and food systems by (i) ensuring the adoption of agroecological farming mechanisms and increasing the use of ILM-based production systems; (ii) enhancing the business and market access conditions for better participation of EBA-trained women in value chains; (iii) promoting the development of skills and knowledge, including technical skills; and (iv) developing and delivering financial services that are adapted to the various project’s target groups.

256. For the promotion of EBA-based production systems, the project will ensure gender balance among extension workers and FFS facilitators for the delivery of EBA agricultural extension services. In addition, the project may need to consider implementing women-only/proof-of-age FFS and pay particular attention on how to better involve the vulnerable (ultra-

## Regarding access to financial services, EBAM will build upon FFSM's existing strategy which includes the establishment of Village Savings and Loans Associations (VSLA), households/ groups/associations to ensure women and youth have access to the financial opportunities offered by the project. The project will also support the development of a financial literacy program aimed at meeting the needs and aspirations of women and youth. Through MoF-Finance which is one of EBAM's co-financer, EBAM will support the effective participation of the vulnerable in Community based Financial Organizations (CBFO) such as Village Savings and Loans Associations (VSLA), Microfinance Institutions (MFI), etc. The project will also ensure that women and youth financial groups are able to access microfinance services from formal financial institutions. In addition, CBFO members will include group governance and dynamics, savings and loans group best practices, economic activity selection / business planning and management, market linkage with off-takers, supplier development, and value chain analysis. The project will also support the Financial Technology (FT) and mobile money banking: Gender Action Learning System (GALS) and Household (HH) methodology. In addition to the above, the project will ensure that women and youth groups supply side, EBAM will support Formal Financial Institutions (FFIs) to develop and deliver adapted financial products and services for women and youth in collaboration with the FFI and a GDA solutions. Particular attention will be given to the development of specific products tailored to women and youth's needs. The project will also ensure that the various studies related to the development of financial inclusion for women and youth are gender-sensitive and socially inclusive.
