non-financial services will be assessed for their gender sensitivity and their awareness on the project’s priority groups.

261 Under Component 3, gender sensitive and socially inclusive activities related to enabling institutions and financial environment include assistance to NCFD to develop its gender strategy as well as capacity strengthening for NGOs’ women and men’s staff on EBA, climate risk analysis and capacity building in gender equality. National Consultations will be held. National Consultation will also be supported on its gender strategy and M&E trainings. Women’s participation in the various projects will be promoted through the Gender Action Plan (GAP) and other mechanisms. Inclusion of women in national policies, women’s participation in policy dialogue will be promoted. Gender principles will be considered in the update of policies and key frameworks. Specific economic analysis looking at natural resource management will be conducted.

262 To ensure gender mainstreaming is clearly visible in the implementation of project’s activities, a Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been developed. It reinforces all project activities and sub-activities with gender equality as an objective. The plan includes clear targets, gender design features and measurable indicators ensuring women’s participation and benefits.

### 7. PRINCIPLES OF PROJECT FORMULATION AND IMPLEMENTATION

#### 7.1 Guiding FAO and GCF Policies and Frameworks

263. The following FAQ and GCF policies and frameworks specifically guide the above assessment and project Gender Action Plan (refer also to other policies and framework in the Environment and Social Safeguards). They are aligned with the overall objective of the project that the project aims to achieve, in addition to climate change adaptation and mitigation outcomes.

- **FAQ Policy on Gender Equality 2020–2030** strives to achieve equality between women and men in sustainable agriculture and rural development for the elimination of hunger and poverty.
- **GCF Updated Gender Policy (2019)** reinforces the responsiveness of GCF to the culturally diverse context of gender equality to better address and account for the links between gender equality, climate change adaptation and mitigation, and disaster risk reduction.
- **FAQ Protection from sexual exploitation and sexual abuse** (PASEA) N° 1831237. The principles of integrity, professionalism, respect for human rights and the dignity of all people underpinning the GCF’s policies on preventing, responding to, and reporting sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA).
- **FAQ Policy on the prevention of harassment, sexual harassment and abuse of authority** N° 1830257 and FAQ policy on sexual harassment (13 February 2019) which states Sexual Harassment in all its forms is contrary to the United Nations Charter, the Staff Regulations and Staff Rules of the Organization and the Standards of Conduct for the International Civil Service.
- **GCF Revised Environment and Social Safeguards Policy** N° 5 on Policy on the Prevention and Protection against Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Harassment (PASEA) N°1830264 into refresh obligations for GCF-project related persons to prevent and respond to SEA and to refrain from condoning, encouraging, participating in, or engaging in SEA.
- **FAQ Environment and Social Safeguards Policy** N° 7 on ESS 1 – Gender equality: the fight against discrimination, practices; equal opportunities for men and women.

88

**Page 223**
---

to take part and to benefit; and 2) ESS 7 – Decent Work: Creation of better employment opportunities, particularly for women and young people; Non-discrimination and equal opportunities in occupation and income; Protection of workers’ rights; Workers' and producers' organizations.

- These ESS are consistent with the objectives of GCF adopted Performance Standards, PS 1 - Access to Information on Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts; PS 2 – Labour and Working Conditions; PS 4 – Community, Health, Safety, and Security and PS6 - Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources.

### 7.2 Principles of Project Formulation and Implementation

#### 7.2.1 Social Acceptance of Gender and Youth Empowerment

264. The project will promote approaches that enhance the social acceptance of gender and youth empowerment both at household and community levels. At household level, the household approach will be applied to ensure that women and men in a given household are empowered to agree on, in particular, what they want to become, what they want to be engaged in and how they can achieve their goals. The project proposes applying the ILM facilitators’ training as a tool for women for the household through guided dialogues among them, which allow women and men to understand and challenge gender norms and their connection to food and nutrition security as well as other aspects of life. At community level, the project will apply the Dlima Cluba methodology, aiming through gender-sensitive participation. The project proposes applying the household approach in households where a woman is the head or where one of her male family members has household head or some household member(s) concentrates decision-making power. The former will be supported by the ILM facilitators’ training as a tool for women and men to engage youth, boys and girls family members in developing their livelihoods.

265. At community level, an important link in ensuring sustainable behavioral change is that the religious, traditional and community leaders and other key figures give a stamp of approval to the changes being made. This will ensure that the social acceptance of gender equality and masculinity is not threatened and that the changes are welcome to the entire community. The religious, traditional and community leaders and other key figures will be involved in gender empowerment at the household and community levels; some will also participate in the ILM facilitators’ trainings as trainers on Dlima Cluba facilitation and household methodology, so that their capacity is strengthened for engaging youth, boys and girls family members. This will ultimately endorse changes in behavior. The Dlima Cluba used by the project to mobilize and engage communities is considered an important tool with gender and social inclusion issues.

#### 7.2.2 Interactions Based on Gender and Context Analysis

266. Understanding the social and cultural context of a society is crucial to ensure the adequacy of a proposed intervention. Malawi’s traditional and social framework is characterized by the existence of two societies: a patriarchal society that discriminates exclusively against women; and a matrilineal society, which non-exclusively discriminates against men. These societies are developed in some respects.

267. Although vulnerable groups were pre-identified at design stage, the project will ensure during implementation that optional content is re-evaluated and that necessary supports are provided to promote the participation and inclusion of individuals and groups who effectively need this support. It is therefore possible, in contrast to what we are usually used to seeing, that in


---
**Page 224**
---

some societies and specific activities, gender mainstreaming targets men who are more disadvantaged than women. Despite similarity to the target group process conducted during the formulation of the project, the project will consider addressing the different priorities, needs and opportunities of the different target groups (women, female youth, male youth and others), for equality, effectiveness and impact.

### 7.2.3 Inclusive and Cooperative Process for Mutual Benefits

#### 7.2.4 Women and Youth Empowerment for Meaningful Participation

269. Climate change is a social challenge profound enough that it does not alone leave human capital behind as it has been to date, it demands both women and men to make strategic decisions at all levels so that their livelihoods are well adapted to the changes. Women and young people have a role in decision-making processes and should be involved in such processes.

270. To make full use of the human capital in the country, the proposed project will compensate for the investment that the society had the tendency to neglect; it will provide technical and marketing support to women and youth entrepreneurs so they can venture into the formal sector and capture the market segments that are more profitable, as they are already equipped with some business acumen. All trainings and consultations under the proposed project will be gender-sensitive for maximum impact.

271. Trainings for government officials and technicians will similarly consult women officials and technicians and include female youth participants. They will also be trained on gender issues so that they can more effectively accompany the process of gender empowerment.

### 7.2.5 Empowerment of Women and Youth

#### 7.2.6 Stakeholders' Involvement

272. Stakeholder's involvement has been at the core of the project development process whether at identification stage, all along the design process as well as during implementation. Government authorities have provided policy orientations and potential beneficiaries particularly women and youth have shared their views on the project concept, its objectives, needs, fields and aspirations. Their participation in the project development process aims at fostering the ownership of the project by the stakeholders and ensuring that they are not left out or feel of being assisted during the lifetime project and do not feel responsible for the results achieved and their sustainability once the project is completed. Moreover the proposed project promotes stakeholder's participation, ensuring that women and youth are involved in all stages more


---
**Page 225**
---

273. Gender mainstreaming and women’s empowerment could exacerbate existing risks of GBV and sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) within households and communities as those with power may feel threatened with losing it to those who have been empowered. It is therefore important that the project should ensure that all stakeholders are involved in addressing the sub-groups at risk including women and girls, female head of households, and persons living with HIV/AIDS. The project will also ensure that GBV and SEAH issues are integrated into planning and responding to GBV and SEAH related issues. Capacity building should allow community members to identify and report cases of GBV and SEAH, as well as provide information on investigation measures, the mechanisms to file complaints related to GBV and SEAH using existing channels (marriage counselors, community leaders, police, etc.) and also the comprehensive Grievance redressal mechanism. The project will ensure that all stakeholders are involved in planning in terms of procedures and possible outcomes[273].

274. Since GBV and SEAH are fueled by deep rooted and cultural beliefs and social norms, it is important to engage the buy-in of community leaders and traditional gatekeepers on these issues. They are the ones who should convey the message on GBV and SEAH as well as GBV and SEAH commitments and train them to monitor and report cases that occur within their communities[274].

275. The project will adopt a zero-tolerance approach to SEAH it must ensure the availability of effective reporting mechanisms accessible to women, girls and the vulnerable groups at risk which minimize the reporting burden on victims, offer gender-sensitive services and minimize the risk of re-traumatization. The project will also ensure that all cases are reported to the GBV/SEAH unit including confidential reporting with secure and ethical documentation. With identified cases, the project will provide appropriate support such as counseling, referrals for medical and health services, psychosocial support, legal advice, police surveillance, and shelters. The project will also disseminate information on the county referral list on child protection and GBV[275].

[273] 5 steps to prevent the risks of sexual and gender-based violence in projects. Maria Amelia Vilari. May 2022 https://tonga.auth.org/handle/10482/6595 Steps to prevent the risks of sexual and gender-based violence in projects.

[274] National Action Plan on Gender-Based Violence (GBV) in Malawi. Ministry of Women, Children’s Affairs and Social Welfare. July 2014

[275] Child protection and gender-based violence referral guidelines for Malawi. Government of Malawi, UNICEF, UNFPA. July 2020

8.2 Consultation Questions

### 8.2.1 Core Consultation Questions for Institutions

#### Gender and youth
- Understanding of gender/youth issues, including legislations, regulations and international commitments:
    - Are you aware of the international agreement commitments made by the government pertaining to gender equality?
    - Are you aware of the National Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan?
    - Which components of the National Gender Policy and Action Plan does your office support?
    - Does your work take various policies, regulations and international conventions into account?
    - Do you make specific efforts to consider issues faced by gender/youth?
- Knowledge of formal rights, laws, regulations, and commitment of the country to international commitments:
    - The country has policies, laws and regulations to safeguard the rights of all persons, regardless of their sex. Are you aware of these? Which ones are they (National Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan)? Are you aware of them?
- What are some of the challenges that women face in Malawi today?
- Do you have information indicating that women’s conditions and needs are different from men's?
- Do you promote your gender concerns at meetings, internal and external?
- Do you work with private commercial entities? How do you encourage them to consider the issues related to gender equality?

#### Roles and responsibilities in production and livelihood systems
- Types of livelihoods: agriculture, livestock, fishery, others:
    - What is the main source of livelihood of your household?
    - What are the roles and responsibilities of other members (the interviewee) ?
    - What is the work of other household members?

### 8.2.2 Core Consultation Questions for Female Farmers

#### Roles and responsibilities in production and livelihood systems
- Types of livelihoods: agriculture, livestock, fishery, others:
    - What is the main source of livelihood of your household?
    - What are the roles and responsibilities of other members (the interviewee) ?
    - What is the work of other household members?

- What is the biggest problem related to your work?
- Are you part of production groups or cooperatives?
- What are the benefits of being part of production groups/cooperatives? Drawbacks?

### Management of various natural resources: responsibilities and resultant social status

- Who is responsible for managing water sources (e.g., wells, rivers)?
- Which livestock is raised for consumption at home? For sale?
- Who is responsible for drinking water, including animals? How fair/close do they fetch the water from the source?
- Who is responsible for cooking fuel?
- Are there any resources that are currently exploited by the community? If yes, are there clear rules for this?
- How has access evolved since your parents/grandparents' generation?
- How do you think resources will evolve for your children/grandchildren's generation?

### Food security and other basic needs (to cloth, etc.): financial and procurement responsibilities

- Who is responsible for finding/purchasing food? Who pays for it?
- Who is responsible for finding/purchasing clothes? Who pays for it?

## Access to and control of resources

### Productive and household assets: ownership, access and control

- What are the assets that are used to generate income (e.g., land, livestock)?
- Who controls those assets?
- Should each asset belong to only one family? Or are there assets that can be jointly exploited?

### Income and expenditure: primary and secondary sources, items and control

- What are the main sources of income in the household?
- Who is responsible for raising the household income?
- Who decides on expenditures?
- Do items of expenditure vary according to the person who decides?

### Access to resources: land

- Who has access to land? Under which conditions?
- Do the modalities of access to land influence its exploitation?

### Access to water sources

- What is the source of drinking water (e.g., well, river, pond)?
- Is it shared by livestock and other animals?
- How much water is available for human use for drinking?
- What do you think about water quality?
- Do you have enough water for your needs?
- Has the amount of available water changed?
- Who controls access to these sources? Who control the water sources?
- How far is the water source?
- What challenges do you face in accessing water?

### Access to energy

- What is the source of cooking fuel?
- Are there other fuels used?
- From whom do you buy them?

### Access to agricultural services: extension, (rural) finance, (agricultural) marketing, etc.

- Is access to these services free or at a cost?

Living conditions

  - Are the measures effective?

# PART II: GENDER ACTION PLAN

## 1. INTRODUCTION

277. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is the project tool which ensures that gender mainstreaming is clearly visible in the implementation of project's activities. The GAP reinforces all project activities and sub-activities from a gender and social inclusion perspective, and includes clear targets, monitoring indicators, and evaluation criteria for measuring participation and benefits.

## 2. GENDER AND SOCIAL INCLUSION APPROACHES, TARGETING AND IMPLEMENTATION MECHANISMS

278. EbAM's gender and social inclusion approaches. The project will rely on the use of the Dimitris Katsikas' inclusive approach (HK4), two methodologies that have proven being effective in terms of community engagement, social mobilization and inclusion, collective action, gender equality and equitable sharing of decision-making power both at household and community level.

279. At community level: Described as voluntary and informal groups of rural women, men, young women and young men which discuss common problems and seek solutions by acting together. The Clubs are a unique approach in the EbAM context. They represent an appropriate entry point to mobilize and engage all community members (women, men, female and male youth) in the process of decision-making and action-taking. At village level, the Clubs stimulate active and voluntary participation of the whole community, whether it concerns discussion, decision-making or actions; and will allow each community member to express their views and ideas on issues that concern them. They will also replace the various technical committees existing at village level (Village Natural Resource Management Committee, etc.). The establishment of a Club in every village where EbAM is implemented will be a good starting point for the development of integrated VLAPs. The Clubs enable members to discuss any subject and access relevant information that responds to their needs. The approach contributes to improving the capacity of community members to identify problems and strengthens the willingness to make change and take actions. Indeed, the Clubs belong to their members and are run by them. They will be responsible for defining their own agenda (what to be discussed, frequency of meetings, types of monitoring, etc.). Since communication, interaction and networking among community members is a key element in the process of capacity development, particularly those related to the adoption of EBA solutions in the EbAM context, whether carried out through the FFS or other channels. As the development and implementation of VLAPs advance, the Clubs will also give a space for redressing any grievance that may arise.

### 104

# Dimitra Club

Dimitra Club consists of self-managed groups of various gender compositions, which organize meetings to discuss common problems and develop ways to solve them together and using local resources. The meetings are organized by Dimitra Club Carers (or Supervisors) who are chosen among the village residents for each Club. Agreements are made on how to manage the club funds and how to use the credit cooperatives. The clubs have played a crucial role in promoting gender equality, empowering women and youth, and supporting them in overcoming long-standing political disputes, mobilizing to adapt to climate change; establishing credit cooperatives to avoid debt and improving access to markets.

In May 2022, the system was introduced to Malawi for piloting in 20 villages in the Districts of Mangochi and Kasungu. The changes observed so far in the involved communities are initiatives to: actively participate in natural resource use and conservation; improve food security and nutrition (especially among women and youth); increase income generation (especially among women and youth). The Clubs have been considered a success beyond expectations by the Malawian press. — and EAM will put this initiative into practice.

## Household Approach

The Household approach[1] will be implemented within households engaged in farming for home consumption and income generation. The approach aims at empowering all household members (male and female, adults and youth) to have better gender and power relations, and to achieve equitable access to resources and benefits. In the Malawian context, this approach will also enable household members to identify and address HIV and other issues[2] which results in improved livelihood of all household members.

The Household Approach is based on a participatory methodology that includes psychosocial dialogues that help them understand their household livelihoods, needs, challenges, roles, gender norms and power relations. The approach also aims at creating an enabling environment that takes into account the aspirations of all as a family and whose implementation is carried out in a participatory manner. Since the methodology facilitates reflection, behavioural change and household planning through group discussions, it helps to address issues related to the distribution of resources within gender and power relations in the household, and also to the increase of household incomes and food security. The approach also aims at increasing awareness on HIV/AIDS prevention, leading to increased knowledge and skills for both women and men, and assertiveness of women and youth. HIV will be addressed through a participatory methodology that includes adaptation to climate change in the household participatory need assessments, vision setting and planning. Lead families and male champions will promote gender equality through action learning.

[1] Household Approach Implementation Manual for Extension Workers and Local Facilitators, Ministry of Agriculture, Irrigation and Water Development, Department of Agriculture Extension Services: 2019

[2] HIV and AIDS issues are at the core of the HH approach promoted and institutionalized by the Ministry of Agriculture at country level


---
**Page 240**
---

281. Apart from climate change adaptation issues which are at the core of the project, the use of the clubs and the clusters approach will help address gender and social inclusion related issues that constitute core barriers to adaptation. A specific focus will be put on (i) gender and sex; (ii) GBV and SEAH; (iii) FFS and other forms of child marriage; (iv) early pregnancy/marriage and drop-out; (v) examples, motives and results of gender discrimination in the context of climate change adaptation; and (vi) gender equality in youth development and welfare, including food and nutrition security. For the latter, the implementation of FFS under Component 2 will also allow discussing the impact of integrated mixed-farming systems on women’s empowerment.

282. The project will adopt a “zero-tolerance approach” to SEAH. It will give specific attention on mitigating risk of GBV and SEAH, particularly on vulnerable groups including female-headed households and young people. A multi-level grievance redress mechanism (GRM) will be put in place to prevent and grievance redress mechanisms to respond to arising issues will be developed in close consultation with the communities. The GRM will comprise four parallel systems: (i) a community-based system, (ii) a project-level GRM, (iii) the GCRM comprising four parallel systems: (i) a community-based system, (ii) a project-level GRM, (iii) the FAO-level GRM and (iv) the GCC Independence Redress Mechanism (IRM). This will allow individual members to report incidents of GBV and SEAH in a safe manner. The IRM will be less formal or more formal channel to lodge a grievance and file complaints. It must ensure the availability of specific procedures for reporting and responding to GBV and SEAH by vulnerable groups at risk that minimize the reporting burden on victims, offer gender-sensitive services and minimize the risk of retaliation. Those mechanisms should contain specific procedures for GBV and

# Project beneficiaries and targeting

The project is expected to directly benefit about 61,549 households and about 270,820 individuals (considering the 2018 Population and Housing Census data). Women located in the project areas represent 52 percent of the total population, and the quota of women directly targeted in project activities is 60 percent. The project targets as a priority smallholder farmers living in vulnerable households and young people who are among those most affected by climate change. The targeting approach is combined with criteria of local vulnerability to climate change and specific criteria according to the activity promoted.

## Project will specifically target

- Vulnerable households living in targeted catchments including poor rural households of farmers, households headed by young women, widows or women abandoned by their husbands.
- Farmers' organizations and associations in the intervention zone, which will be identified in each village under selected micro watersheds and involved in VLAPS development process.
- Women farmers' groups and associations living in the intervention area and involved in VLAPS.
- Young women and men and youth groups in the intervention area who are interested in participating in project activities, particularly in public works, digitalization and agriculture related issues.
- Middle-income and better-off farmers living in targeted catchments.
- SMEs and cooperatives that will be identified based on defined value chains under the market analysis and preferred crops identified by EoM.

## Selection of beneficiaries

The selection of beneficiary households and individuals will be made (i) on a geographic level, taking into account the results of the land-use planning and resource-mapping operations undertaken by the project's implementing partners; and (ii) on the basis of specific criteria developed in participatory manner for each activity. EoM implementation approach will thus be essential to ensure that beneficiaries will be identified based on their interest and motivation to engage in project activities.


---
**Page 242**
---

286. EbAM will ensure that the identified target groups participate and benefit from the project activities. It will combine various targeting mechanisms including geographic targeting, direct targeting, and community-based targeting.

287. Geographic targeting includes different stages (detailed in section 1.4 from the Feasibility Study):
- Identification of the districts and Water Resource Units (WRUs) that are on average more than 500 ha based on the climate vulnerability analysis (see section 1.4 for details). These are the higher administrative and watered/level areas identified.
- Identification of households inside the WRUs. This level coincides with approximately TA level (1,500–6,000 ha). This will be done through a Social and Community Mapping (SCAM) will be supported, and SCMP formulated.
- Identification of households in villages and communities part of the watersheds as possible project beneficiaries, at micro-catchment level (500–1,500 ha). This is the level where Villages will be supported and VLA/PLA will be established.

288. EbAM’s direct targeting will orient project supports to specific groups (e.g., poor households, young women who dropped out of school due to teenage pregnancy/nearly marriage, young men candidates for immigration, etc.) based on vulnerability criteria and technical criteria specific to the project activities. The project has ambitious targets and aims at reaching 60 percent of women among directly targeted individuals for sub-activities. More vulnerable groups will be prioritized in terms of access to services and benefits. Specific poverty criteria will be established at the start of the project, to ensure that the most vulnerable (low income, disabled, female-headed households) benefit from the project. EbAM will also put in place empowerment measures to improve beneficiaries’ capacity for social engineering process, capacity building, etc.).

289. In line with the community-based natural resource management approach, the project will also rely on a participatory selection mechanism. This approach implies consultations with the relevant communities and other local stakeholders (community leaders, etc.) in the selection of projects and activities to be used. The use of the Dimitra Club Approach will support the project community-based targeting.

290. The project will also consider self-targeting approach which includes the provision of goods and services that are aligned with the priorities of users, assets, capacities and livelihood strategies of the communities involved in the project. Self-targeting is a deliberate selection of project areas of intervention and allows individuals, producer organizations and entrepreneurs to decide on their own what they need from the project and how much support they will receive or support from the project. The following points relate to some self-targeting elements identified during design phase: women and young people’s interest in participating in income-generating activities; community members in climate change adaptation activities; women and young people’s will to run their own business/income generating activities; micro and small enterprises’ interest in receiving technical assistance and training for the development of new products and services, how to participate and create commercial relationships with other market players; small entrepreneurs who show interest in developing new goods and services that can contribute to local pro-poor economic and social development dynamics, etc.

291. Implementation mechanisms: The coordination of the day-to-day implementation of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) will be done by a specialist on gender issues who is part of the team who will be recruited by the project. The Specialist will work as part of the Central Project Implementation Unit (CPIU) within FAO (as Executive Entity - EE) and under the direct supervision of the Project Director.


---
**Page 243**
---

292. Regarding project management, the Specialist must ensure that the planning, implementation of all project activities, monitoring and evaluation reporting are gender sensitive. The project’s monitoring framework and monitoring and evaluation system includes recommended measurable indicators for women and youth that allow assessing the sensitivity of project's component and sub-component to these groups. The various technical and progress reports should consider gender-disaggregated information and analysis. The qualitative change on GEWE relating to “women empowerment and inclusion” relating the qualitative change on GEWE will be monitored. It will be measured through the percentage of women perceiving improved support for women's empowerment.

293. As for gender and youth specific activities, the implementation of the Dimitra Clubs approach will be carried out by 30 ILM facilitators recruited through NGOs (see Annex 2, sub- component 1). The facilitators will train 600 young people in the Dimitra Club Approach. The facilitators (who will be trained on the Dimitra Clubs Approach) will be in charge of sensitizing local authorities and community leaders to promote the implementation of the project’s activities and to mobilize women from the communities. Regarding health issues such as HIV and AIDS, apart from their consideration at the core of the household approach, the project will involve Community Health Committee members (CHCs) who are already trained in this area. The CHCs will be supported by local facilitators working in the locations. As for the household approach, EDAIM will work closely with the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD) to ensure that agricultural extension services officers (AGRESSO) will take the
